abstract_en: "In the beginning of the XXI century a new concept of technology appeared,\
  \ promising\r\nto change the way we see and interact with the real world. Breaking\
  \ the barriers\r\nbetween the real and the virtual, for the first time the objective\
  \ was not to achieve more\r\npowerful devices but to obtain tiny, portable and low-cost\
  \ devices, capable to\r\ncommunicate with each other. Equipped with sensors, these\
  \ extremely cheap devices\r\ncould be spread all over the world connecting everything.\
  \ The concept of Wireless\r\nSensor Networks (WSNs) was thus born.\r\nDuring the\
  \ last decade Wireless Sensor Networks have evolved in an attempt to meet\r\nthe\
  \ requirements posed by each of the possible applications. Related work targeted\r\
  \nWireless Sensor Networks for military, healthcare, education, surveillance, environment\r\
  \nand transport applications, among others. The paradigm has varied according to\r\
  \napplications and evolved in the last years based on the experience obtained with\
  \ real\r\ndeployments. Initial applications previewed networks constituted by thousands\
  \ of motes\r\nrandomly deployed to monitor distinct phenomena, whereas the latest\
  \ approaches have\r\nappeared more focused and close to the paradigm of the Internet\
  \ of Things.\r\nIdentified as one of the most promising technologies of this century,\
  \ after all this time,\r\nreal applications of Wireless Sensor Networks are still\
  \ scarce. Uncontrolled\r\nperformance and unreliability has seriously postponed\
  \ its use, mainly in critical\r\nscenarios. The lack of official standards and the\
  \ non-support of mobility have\r\ncontributed to the delay of its massification\
  \ in the world’s everyday life. On the one\r\nhand the portability of these tiny\
  \ battery powered devices, naturally leads to mobility,\r\nbut on the other hand\
  \ the constrained hardware highly restricts its support.\r\nHence, we present in\
  \ this thesis a study of new models to support the mobility of nodes\r\nin Wireless\
  \ Sensor Networks, guaranteeing reliability end-to-end. We start by\r\npresenting\
  \ a deep study of the mobility support panorama in this technology. Based on that\
  \ study we evaluated possible solutions bringing existent concepts from cellular\
  \ and\r\nIP networks. At the end we concluded that although the most popular solutions\
  \ worked\r\neffectively, none of them could guarantee the desired performance and\
  \ reliability.\r\nTherefore we designed and assessed a new model to support mobility\
  \ in Wireless\r\nSensor Networks, guaranteeing the support of different requirements\
  \ and assuring endto-\r\nend reliability. Furthermore, we extended our model to\
  \ scope not only mobility but\r\nalso other demanding tasks such as routing and\
  \ QoS, releasing the constrained devices\r\nfrom the heaviest tasks, creating the\
  \ concept of Assisted Wireless Sensor Network.\r\nIn addition, we have applied the\
  \ developed models in real industrial scenarios, in which\r\nperformance and network\
  \ reliability are cornerstones."

Agradecimentos 
 
 
A realização desta tese e de todo o trabalho inerente, desenvolvido ao longo dos últimos 
anos, foi possível somente graças ao suporte dos que me rodeiam e que sempre me 
apoiaram, nos bons e nos maus momentos. 
 
A ti Vanessa, pela dedicação, amor e compreensão que sempre demonstraste e pela 
força que sempre me deste para trabalhar em prol dos nossos objectivos. Amo-te! 
 
À pequena Constança que é a razão da minha vida. Ensinar-te-ei os primeiros passos, as 
primeiras palavras. Guiar-te-ei ao longo da tua vida até que as forças me o permitam.  
Sempre a teu lado, com muito amor. 
 
Aos meus Pais, Maria Adélia e Bertolino, por todo o esforço que têm feito com um 
único objectivo: de poderem proporcionar a mim e à Rita uma vida melhor do que 
aquela que puderam ter. Guardo-vos no coração. 
 
À Rita por todo o apoio que sempre me deste como irmã mais velha e amiga e pela 
fonte de inspiração que és. E à Margarida claro, que nos veio mostrar o quanto simples 
e importante a vida é. 
 
A todos os meus amigos André, Teresa, Ana, Zé, Morão, Miriam, Jéssica, Maria, 
Kaxolas, Rogério, João, Bruno, Li, Grande, Sara, Grilo, Michael, Carlos, Paulo e todos 
os outros que têm sido o meu grande pilar nesta caminhada da vida. 
 
Aos colegas do LCT e todos os outros com quem fui trabalhando ao longo destes 
últimos anos: André Gomes, André Rodrigues, Dien, Vinicius, Fernando, Adriano, 

 VIII 
Alexandre, Custódio, Pedro, Milan, Jara, Zinonas e toda a equipa do GINSENG. 
Aprendi imenso convosco. 
 
Um especial agradecimento ao Professor Doutor Jorge Sá Silva por todo o apoio, 
amizade e orientação que tem dado neste meu percurso académico. Sem o seu 
entusiasmo e visão inovadora certamente não teria iniciado este percurso.  
 
Um especial agradecimento também ao Professor Doutor Fernando Boavida, que 
sempre se mostrou disponível para apoiar todo o processo de produção científica e 
apoiar o curso dos trabalhos sempre que necessário. O seu método e experiência foram 
fundamentais para a qualidade desta tese e marcaram o modo como encaro os desafios 
profissionais. 
 
Ao meu tio António que foi a minha referência profissional na área das redes e 
telecomunicações e a todos os restantes tios, tias e primos que têm mantido a coesão e 
união familiar. 
 
Agradeço também a todos os outros que direta ou indiretamente me apoiaram nos 
últimos tempos, que mesmo não fazendo ideia do trabalho que desenrolava 
contribuíram para que a vida tomasse outras cores. 
 
Concluo agradecendo à Fundação para a Ciência e Tecnologia pela bolsa de 
doutoramento cedida (SFRH / BD / 60342 / 2009, financiada pelo POPH - QREN), que 
me permitiu dedicar exclusivamente a este projeto e também ao Departamento de 
Engenharia Informática que me acolheu e suportou a minha formação.

 XIII
 
Abstract (Portuguese) 
 
 
No início do século XXI um novo conceito tecnológico surgiu prometendo mudar o 
modo como vemos e interagimos com o mundo real. Quebrando as barreiras entre o 
mundo real e o virtual, pela primeira vez na história o objectivo não era alcançar 
dispositivos mais capazes, mas sim obter dispositivos pequenos, portáteis e de baixo 
custo, capazes de comunicar entre si. Equipados com sensores, estes dispositivos 
poderiam ser instalados por todo o mundo interligando qualquer elemento. Nascia assim 
o conceito de Redes de Sensores sem Fios (RSSFs). 
Durante a última década, as Redes de Sensores sem Fios tem evoluído numa tentativa 
de responder aos requisitos impostos pelas potenciais aplicações. Aplicações militares, 
saúde, educação, vigilância, ambiente ou transportes são exemplos de áreas para onde as 
RSSFs têm sido apontadas. O paradigma tem variado consoante as aplicações e 
evoluído nos últimos anos com base na experiência de campo, entretanto adquirida. As 
aplicações iniciais previam redes constituídas por milhares de nós espalhados 
aleatoriamente para monitorizar diversos fenómenos, enquanto que as últimas 
abordagens têm aparecido mais focadas e próximas do paradigma da Internet of Things. 
Apesar de identificada como uma das tecnologias mais promissoras do século, as 
aplicações reais de RSSFs mantêm-se escassas. O não controlo da performance e a falta 
de fiabilidade tem seriamente adiado o uso desta tecnologia, principalmente em cenários 
críticos. A falta de normas e o não suporte de mobilidade têm contribuído para o atraso 
da massificação das RSSFs no mundo real. Se por um lado a portabilidade destes 
pequenos dispositivos, suportados por baterias, leva naturalmente à aplicação em 
cenários móveis, por outro lado as restrições do “hardware” levam a que o suporte à 
mobilidade seja altamente complexo. 
Como tal, esta tese apresenta um estudo aprofundado de novos modelos de suporte à 
mobilidade em Redes de Sensores sem Fios, garantindo fiabilidade ponto-a-ponto. 

 XIV 
Começando por apresentar um estudo elaborado sobre o suporte de mobilidade em 
RSSFs, esta tese avalia depois novas possíveis soluções, trazendo conceitos já 
existentes das redes celulares e IP para as RSSFs. Concluindo que apesar de suportadas, 
as tecnologias existentes não garantiam os níveis nem de performance nem de 
fiabilidade requeridos, foi desenhado e avaliado um novo modelo de suporte à 
mobilidade capaz de assegurar tais garantias. Para além disso, o modelo foi estendido de 
modo a abranger não só a mobilidade mas também outras tarefas complexas de suportar 
em nós tão restritos, tais como o encaminhamento e a qualidade de serviço, criando um 
novo conceito de Redes de Sensores sem Fios Assistidas. 
Para concluir, os novos modelos foram aplicados num cenário industrial real, no qual a 
performance e fiabilidade das redes é um ponto completamente fulcral para garantir o 
normal desenrolar da atividade. 
 
 
 
  

 XV 
 
Abstract 
 
 
In the beginning of the XXI century a new concept of technology appeared, promising 
to change the way we see and interact with the real world. Breaking the barriers 
between the real and the virtual, for the first time the objective was not to achieve more 
powerful devices but to obtain tiny, portable and low-cost devices, capable to 
communicate with each other. Equipped with sensors, these extremely cheap devices 
could be spread all over the world connecting everything. The concept of Wireless 
Sensor Networks (WSNs) was thus born. 
During the last decade Wireless Sensor Networks have evolved in an attempt to meet 
the requirements posed by each of the possible applications. Related work targeted 
Wireless Sensor Networks for military, healthcare, education, surveillance, environment 
and transport applications, among others. The paradigm has varied according to 
applications and evolved in the last years based on the experience obtained with real 
deployments. Initial applications previewed networks constituted by thousands of motes 
randomly deployed to monitor distinct phenomena, whereas the latest approaches have 
appeared more focused and close to the paradigm of the Internet of Things.     
Identified as one of the most promising technologies of this century, after all this time, 
real applications of Wireless Sensor Networks are still scarce. Uncontrolled 
performance and unreliability has seriously postponed its use, mainly in critical 
scenarios. The lack of official standards and the non-support of mobility have 
contributed to the delay of its massification in the world’s everyday life. On the one 
hand the portability of these tiny battery powered devices, naturally leads to mobility, 
but on the other hand the constrained hardware highly restricts its support.  
Hence, we present in this thesis a study of new models to support the mobility of nodes 
in Wireless Sensor Networks, guaranteeing reliability end-to-end. We start by 
presenting a deep study of the mobility support panorama in this technology. Based on 

 XVI 
that study we evaluated possible solutions bringing existent concepts from cellular and 
IP networks. At the end we concluded that although the most popular solutions worked 
effectively, none of them could guarantee the desired performance and reliability. 
Therefore we designed and assessed a new model to support mobility in Wireless 
Sensor Networks, guaranteeing the support of different requirements and assuring end-
to-end reliability. Furthermore, we extended our model to scope not only mobility but 
also other demanding tasks such as routing and QoS, releasing the constrained devices 
from the heaviest tasks, creating the concept of Assisted Wireless Sensor Network.  
In addition, we have applied the developed models in real industrial scenarios, in which 
performance and network reliability are cornerstones.

 1 
Chapter 1  
 
Introduction 
 
 
 
Wireless Sensor Networks (WSN) present a great potential in monitoring systems. 
Constituted by autonomous and portable devices a vast scope of applications can take 
advantage of WSNs.  If on the one hand the traditional wired sensors and actuators can 
be replaced by this flexible easy-to-deploy technology, on the other hand the type of 
scenarios in which sensors and actuators can be applied is enormous considering their 
portability. However, the application of WSN in mobile-based scenarios does not 
correspond to the theoretical feasibility. Supporting reliable mobility from the 
communication point of view is tremendous, demanding and complex to these 
hardware-constrained devices. Hence, the objective of this work is to study the mobility 
support in WSNs, proposing a solution capable to ensure high reliability and end-to-end 
performance for application in critical scenarios. The entire work presented in this thesis 
had the target the Petrogal’s oil refinery in which we have deployed WSNs in the scope 
of the GINSENG Project [Sreenan et al. 2011]. 
 
This chapter is split into three sections. The first section presents the focus and 
objectives of this research work; the second section highlights its main contributions 
while the third section specifies the structure of this thesis. 

11.1. Focus and Objectives 

 2 
 
Wireless Sensor Networks are usually pointed as the missing extension to connect the 
virtual to the real world [Akyldiz et al. 2002].  Constituted by low power and low cost 
small nodes, WSNs have been projected for thousands of applications  in several areas. 
Military, healthcare, education, environment, transports or industrial automation are 
common targets for WSNs appliance.  
Although the number of use-cases has been increasing daily, the existent WSNs 
operating at the moment do not cover most of them. Such situation happens because the 
technology evolution is not following the theoretical WSNs potential. One failure is 
clearly the lack of a reliable mobility support, capable to guarantee end-to-end 
controlled performance. Since WSNs are seen to link the virtual to the real world, it is 
natural that the probability of monitoring mobile bodies is truly high. 
 
For instance, in healthcare patients monitoring is highly desired. Heart rate, breath rate, 
blood pressure and weight are common factors that must be constantly monitored. 
Therefore, using portable systems based on WSNs make possible, besides a most 
efficient medical service, to provide a new freedom for the patients. Senior citizens can 
also benefit from the mobility in WSNs. Usually, eldercare systems are provided in 
special places, forcing people to move from their own places. However, with WSNs 
supporting mobility, it will be possible to keep senior citizens in their houses for more 
time, monitoring in real time their health condition.  
 
Besides that, and still within the healthcare scope, mobility in WSNs will be the key to 
allow the monitoring of employees within hazardous areas. Hazardous areas are usually 
in oil refineries, chemistry and petro-chemistry industries among others. In such places 
employees can be under difficult and unhealthy conditions and therefore suffer hard 
consequences. Hence, it is highly demanded that employees are monitored in real time, 
regarding for instance, the basic vital signals, such as heart and breath rate. With 
mobility support in WSNs, employees can do their normal work while they are sure that 
if any uncommon situation in their health state is detected, an alarm will be triggered on 
time. Apart from hazardous areas, mostly common within industrial environments, the 
military area also demands such monitoring. For instance in battlefields, solders must be 
also under monitoring, controlling their health condition, not only because of the 

 3 
possible presence of hazardous areas, but also in order to detect injuries as fast as 
possible. To support such scenario, WSNs can be deployed in military vehicles and 
soldiers, constituting completely mobile dependent networks. Regarding vehicles, 
transports also benefit from the mobility in WSNs. Equipped with sensor nodes, for 
instance, the buses of any city can be used to monitor the city traffic. Besides that, since 
buses move through the same paths, they can be used to monitor the environmental 
conditions of the entire path, reporting and detecting anomalies, for instance based on 
CO2 levels. Furthermore, apart from monitoring situations, mobility in WSNs can also 
be used to help children learning in elementary schools. Monitoring temperature, 
humidity or light, students can learn what these factors really are, just by playing with 
them [Silva et al. 2009]. 
 
After the examples listed above, it is difficult to imagine WSNs massively used without 
a reliable mobility support. Hence, understanding how demanding the mobility support 
is, our objective is to achieve a solution capable of being widely used, supported by 
heterogeneous networks and possible to apply in the already existent deployments.  
 
In the dictionary mobility means: "The ability to move easily". In physics it is the state 
of being in motion, where motion means a constant change in the location of a body. 
Movement is natural; it is a physical activity and ability as well. In computer 
engineering, mobility comes associated to wireless communication and the ability of a 
device to move freely but remaining connected. Such device can switch from network 
to network, independently of the technology used and always remaining reachable from 
one or more network points. 
Mobility is more than a simple move, connection maintenance or ability.  
 
The objective of this work is to study, design, implement and assess possible solutions 
to provide reliable mobility of nodes between WSNs, considering soft handoff 
mechanisms.  We aim to evaluate related proposals and to compare different platforms, 
without any trend and assuming all the possibilities to support mobility, while 
guaranteeing the reliability of the entire system. We also assume that the existent 
standards can suffer some revision during this project, which should not affect the 
progress of this study.  

 4 
 
The objective of this work is to allow a mobile sensor node to move among different 
parents, within the same network or between networks, maintaining its connectivity and 
ensuring: 
• Controlled number of packet losses during the handoff process. 
• Routing optimizations, avoiding the triangulation effect. 
• An efficient throughput. 
• Controlled latencies. 
• Energy-aware parent selection and/or QoS based parent selection. 
 
Our main objective is to allow all the described use cases and much more, to benefit 
from such cost-effective technology, improving directly and indirectly people’s life 
quality and expectancy.  
 
Without loss of generality, the proposals presented in this thesis are being developed 
and assessed in the scope of the GINSENG FP7 European project, which uses an oil 
refinery as a case study. As mentioned before, many applications can benefit from 
mobility support and GINSENG, a performance controlled WSN developed specifically 
for critical environments and currently applied in the Petrogal’s oil refinery, represents 
one of those applications.  
GINSENG aims to achieve a controlled performance WSN able to guarantee the same 
reliability and performance level of the existent wired solutions, but providing an easier 
and cheaper deployment, while allowing the appearance of new scenarios, which could 
never be implemented using wired systems. One of those new scenarios scopes the 
employees’ health assurance and is one of the main targets of the GINSENG project. 
Within the oil refinery, workers are exposed to hazardous environments, in highly 
critical areas. In such scenario, it is absolutely essential to resort to a real-time 
monitoring system in order to constantly follow the workers’ vital signs, during their 
daily activity at the refinery. Due to its nature, this critical scenario requires highly 
reliable systems, with performance guarantees, which makes it ideal to be the focus of 
the study presented in this thesis. More than ever, a reliable mobility solution model 
must be assured to guarantee the efficiency of the whole solution. 

 5 
Towards the standardization and massification of WSNs, it is necessary to study 
existent paradigms assessing the possibility of their usage and contribution to these 
constrained networks and developing new models capable of achieving the desired 
objectives. Therefore, the work presented in this thesis follows a natural evolution, 
starting by a theoretical analysis of the existent state of the art, in which the first 
assumptions are made. Based on that, the second step comprises the assessment of 
existent models and possible improvements to enhance their usability in WSNs, while 
the third step proposes and assesses new models capable to go further and guarantee a 
completely reliable WSN with mobility support. Based on the new model, the paradigm 
is extended also to other areas, allowing the support of extra features such as QoS and 
security.    
The next section details these main contributions, whereas section 1.3 details the 
document structure. 
11.2. Contributions 
 
This section summaries the main contributions of this thesis. Considering the described 
objectives the following achievements were made: 
•        	
  

	


Mobility in Wireless Sensor Networks has been approached from distinct 
perspectives and just in punctual cases. In this thesis our first contribution was to 
join, analyze and compare the most important proposals in the state of the art.  
 
•   
  
	  
  
 
 

	

 !
Based on velocity and noise or by assuring a specific Packet Received Ratio 
(PRR) we contributed with different methods of mobility estimation to 
dynamically adapt the threshold and detect the need to trigger the handoff. This 
contribution is the basis for the success of any handoff procedure, assuring its 
occurrence in the exact time. 
 

 6 
• Implementation of MIPv6 in ContikiOS 2.4 and 2.5, evaluating its feasibility in 
Wireless Sensor Networks. 
Since IP in sensors became a reality that the possibility of using well-known 
solutions, such as MIPv6, to support mobility was to be seriously considered. To 
definitely remove all doubts about its feasibility we implemented MIPv6 in 
ContikiOS, for different versions, and evaluated it not only in real hardware but 
also by simulation. 
 
• Proposal, implementation and evaluation of new models based on MIPv6 
compressed version, following the 6lowPAN concept. 
After evaluating MIPv6 in WSNs there was another question about the 
possibility of improving its feasibility using the same methods of compressing, 
coding and fragmenting that 6lowPAN used to IPv6. Therefore, we proposed 
and submitted an Internet Draft  to the 6lowPAN WG including a complete 
scheme to reduce the entire MIPv6 protocol. We implemented and evaluated this 
solution, comparing it with the original version.  
 
• Proposal, implementation and evaluation of new models based on a new concept 
of Network of Proxies (NoP), a plug and play network-based solution, designed 
specifically to support mobility in WSNs. 
Once the doubts about the feasibility of MIPv6 in WSNs or the feasibility of a 
compressed version were dissipated, we proposed a novel solution based on 
proxies organized in a mesh network, capable to smoothly assist mobility in 
WSNs, without affecting or compromising their main function. Network of 
Proxies (NoP) is the main contribution of this thesis. Implemented in real 
hardware and evaluated, this contribution was compared with MIPv6 
demonstrating its high superiority regarding the time required to handoff and 
energy consumption.  
11.3. Document Structure 
 
This thesis is organized in six chapters, representing each one different parts of the 
research work carried on. The next paragraphs summarize them.   

 7 
This chapter introduces the subject of mobility in WSNs, stressing the main objective of 
this thesis and highlighting the most significant contributions. The case study 
GINSENG, in which the output of this work will be applied, is also described. 
The next chapter presents the state of the art, considering the existent work regarding 
mobility in WSNs and other related issues. After a brief summary of the technology in 
use, we started by characterizing the mobility and its applicability in WSNs. Then, 
focusing only in the mobility of motes we survey existent work at both MAC and 
Network Layers. Besides this, we present a study of reliability and QoS considering the 
mobility impact and also a set of the most important projects in the area, approaching 
mobility. We conclude this chapter by presenting the most popular industrial solutions 
currently in use, usually targeted for critical scenarios such as our main case study in 
GINSENG. 
Chapter III presents the initial requirements to efficiently support mobility in any 
system, by performing the right mobility estimation. Before applying any support of 
mobility it is necessary to build mechanisms to detect it and to trigger the handoff 
process on time. This chapter analyzes existent solutions used for mobility estimation 
presenting then a set of WSN-adapted solutions achieved to first detect when the 
handoff must be triggered, considering different methods and then to predict the 
movement direction of the MN in order to get the next configuration in advance. 
Mobility estimation is one of the most important features to assure reliability on the 
entire mobility process. 
Once detected the need to handoff it will be necessary to support mechanisms to get 
configurations and update links. Chapter IV studies and evaluates the feasibility of 
using a conventional solution developed to unconstrained networks in WSNs such as 
MIPv6 and based solutions. 
Chapter V presents the main contribution of this thesis, namely a plug-and-play 
network-based solution to support mobility in WSN – the Network of Proxies. By 
adding extra Full Function Devices into the WSNs, capable to communicate among 
them and establishing an unconstrained mesh network, we could achieve higher 
performance and reliability of the WSN. This chapter also includes the application of 
our proposal in the GINSENG project and the extension of this solution not only to 
support mobility but also other demanding mechanisms, such as security, routing or 
QoS, originating the new concept of Assisted Wireless Sensor Network – AWSN. 

 17

All cited algorithms deal with the mobility of sensor nodes, users or sink nodes in 
WSNs. However they only approach mobility from the application/practical level, 
ignoring the complexity of implementing the communication support. Only a few 
approaches regarding mobility are aware about the entire implementation, but only from 

 18 
the physical point of view. The next sections present some approaches that have 
provided some dynamics towards mobility support in WSNs. Different perspectives are 
approached crossing the native IEEE802.15.4 with the newer 6lowPAN and IPv6 
support. 
 
22.3. Mobility at the MAC layer 
 
This section approaches the duty cycle optimizations and its impact on mobility 
support. Separated in two parts, duty cycle schemes for static nodes are firstly 
approached, followed by duty cycle schemes for mobile nodes. 
 
2.3.1. Optimized Duty cycle schemes for static nodes 
 
To optimize the duty cycle scheme of MAC Layer, several algorithms have been 
developed. Most of them make use of node synchronization that requires the internal 
clock synchronization among all network elements, performing an optimized duty 
cycle, designed to save energy and increase the network lifetime. Such watch is used to 
wake up all nodes in a specific period of time, being this period a fixed and equal  value 
for all nodes of the same cluster. This scenario requires several synchronization 
messages or synchronization flags and forces edge nodes to synchronize with two 
different networks. Asynchronous mechanisms do not require any synchronization, once 
they are mostly based on long or short preambles before transmissions. Consequently, 
to avoid watch synchronizations, the overhead increases and therefore the number of 
messages required to transmit the same amount  of data. In the remaining of this section 
we present the most popular solutions of MAC protocols for WSNs, not considering 
mobility. 
 
S-MAC [Ye et al 2002] is a solution based on a synchronized switch, where nodes are 
grouped into clusters. All cluster nodes switch to passive and active mode at the same 
time. This algorithm forces the broadcasting of a synchronization message (SYNC) to 
synchronize the internal watch of each sensor node.  One S-MAC slot is composed by 
the period of time that the node is in the active state plus the period of time that the node 

 19 
is in passive state. All nodes broadcast SYNC messages periodically and when a node 
wants to talk with another one, it sends a Request to Send (RTS) and waits for the 
authorization or being, the Clear to Send (CTS). The slot and duty cycle size are fixed 
values and non-adaptable to the network load, which could cause an increase in the 
latency and a decrease in the traffic transmitted between nodes. 
 
T-MAC [Dam and Langendoen 2003] is also a synchronized based solution, which has 
the S-MAC functionality as base. The difference between both is in the use of a 
dynamic duty cycle adaptable to the network conditions. The difference is in the listen 
interval that instead of happening in a pre-defined period of time, it happens just until 
the stop of all activities, during a short period of time. T-MAC also introduces a new 
concept, the Future Request to Send (FRST), which is a message to alert about the 
arriving of another packet. With FRST it is possible to avoid that in a short period of 
silence the node falls asleep while a new packet is arriving.   
 
TRAMA [Rajendran et al. 2003], traffic-adaptive medium access protocol, as the name 
indicates, is a traffic-adaptive TDMA MAC protocol capable to arrange schedules based 
on the traffic needs. TRAMA uses not only scheduled-slots for transmission but also 
contention-based slots (CSMA) for node admission and network management. Once 
capable to adapt the schedule regarding the amount of data each node has to transmit, 
TRAMA ignores the nodes without data to transmit allowing them to safely enter in 
sleep mode, saving energy. The biggest problem of TRAMA regards processing and 
memory requirement while computing the two-hop neighborhood list for each node, 
which can be a very demanding task in dense deployments. Besides, although it is 
energetically more efficient, it demonstrates higher delay than the contention-based 
solutions, which make it optimal only for delay tolerant applications. 
DS-MAC [Lin et al. 2004], Dynamic Sensor MAC, unlike TRAMA, applies a dynamic 
duty cycle mechanism to the traditional S-MAC, making it suitable for delay-sensitive 
applications. These solutions packets contain a one-hop latency that is analyzed at the 
received node. If the received considers that the latency is too high it duplicates the duty 
cycle by reducing the sleep period, maintaining the listen one. This mechanism allows 

 20 
reducing the latency when compared to S-MAC, improving the power consumption per 
packet. 
 
WiseMAC [El-Hoiydi et al.  2004] is a mechanism based on non-synchronization, 
avoiding any additional message to synchronize the internal clock of each node. 
Introducing the concept of low power listening (LPL), with WiseMAC, nodes have 
periodic wake up schemes and a short duty cycle that only allows receiving a preamble 
signaling the beginning of a new reception. The nodes, which do not know the period of 
time that the neighbors are wakeful, send a preamble with the duration equal to the time 
that the neighbor is sleeping. The neighbor node after receiving the preamble, answers 
to the initial node, sending it its own wake up scheme. Each node has an internal table 
with the neighbors wake up scheme, allowing minimizing the preamble size regarding 
the difference between both watches, optimizing the communication scenario by 
decreasing the delay.      
 
B-MAC [Polastre et al. 2004], ( Berkeley-MAC), provides an interface to allow the 
application to adjust the sleep schedule, regarding the network load. Both, WiseMAC 
and B-MAC suffer from the same problem: the overhearing provoked by the long 
preamble that affects the entire network. Therefore, during the preamble, all nodes wake 
up and wait for the end of the preamble to verify, whether or not, they are the receivers. 
B-MAC was implemented and evaluated in real platforms. When compared with S-
MAC and T-MAC, it proved to be more energetically efficient and to support higher 
throughputs. B-MAC was developed for the Mica2 and CC1000 radio transceiver. 
However, new radio transceivers do not support long preambles anymore but upgrades 
like B-MAC+ appeared to have solved the problem. 
 
X-MAC, [Buettner et al. 2006], used by Contiki Operating System and TinyOS as well, 
is also based on Low Power Listening (LPL), like WiseMAC and B-MAC. However, X-
MAC was designed to avoid the main problems introduced by the previous approaches, 
namely, the overhearing question. Hence, X-MAC replaces the long preamble by a 
sequence of short preambles and introduces an acknowledge message from receiver to 
sender, in order to notify the last that the preamble was already listened and therefore 
the transmission can start. Thus, to start a transmission, both entities do not have to wait 

 21 
for the preamble to end. Moreover, in order to avoid neighbors to become affected by 
preambles of messages targeting other destinations, each short preamble contains the 
receiver’s ID. With these type of preambles, neighbors that wake up and receive a short 
preamble will check the ID to see whether they are the receivers. If so, they will send an 
acknowledge message, the sender will stop the preambles and finally send the packet. 
Otherwise they just go to sleep again. Nevertheless, if the neighbors also want to send a 
packet to the same node of the preamble target, they wait a random period and send it 
without preamble. Thus, they give time for the receiver to wake up, to receive the first 
packet and later to receive their packet without any additional preamble. To support this 
feature, nodes after receiving a packet are configured to wait a random period before 
going to sleep again. 
 
LL-MAC [Marín et al. 2008] is a strict TDMA MAC protocol designed specifically to 
meet latency requirements in the most common WSN’s applications. Based on a time 
slot structure the author demonstrated the superiority of this solution when compared, 
for instance, with S-MAC. Using a strict and pre-defined topology, LL-MAC is capable 
to outperform most of the existent solutions regarding energy, latency and topology 
management, when applied to specific and controlled tree-topology based networks. 
Nevertheless, this solution presents scalability issues, which limits its applicability. 
 
TSMP [Pister et al. 2008], Time Synchronized Mesh Protocol is an advanced protocol 
designed and developed specifically for Industrial Automation. TSMP is TDMA based 
and implements more than a simple MAC Layer protocol. Security, reliability and high 
efficiency in controlled environments are ensured by this protocol. TSMP will be 
deeply approached later in this document, associated to another well-known standard 
for industrial automation. Nevertheless, to guarantee the required level of security, 
scalability, reliability and efficiency, TSMP loses in flexibility, dynamic and therefore 
in the mobility support.  
 
Unsynchronized MAC mechanisms with fixed and random interval [Hurni et al. 2010], 
proposes an additional period, where the node is wakeful or not, being it a random 
condition. For each cycle, there are two random periods in the active state and two in 
the passive state. This solution avoids the need to send preambles to get the neighbors 

 22 
scheme, because sooner or later, the wakeful period of each mote will certainly coincide 
and the wake up schemes will be known. Therefore, when one node wants, for instance, 
to send a broadcast message it has to wait for the period when more nodes are in the 
active state. To reach each node of the cluster, it might be necessary to send the same 
broadcast more than once. 
 
22.3.2. Optimized Duty cycle schemes supporting mobility 
 
Considering the potential of mobility in WSNs, some solutions have been proposed by 
attempting to efficiently integrate mobility support in the duty cycle mechanisms, 
keeping the energy consumption and latencies as low as possible. 
 
MS-MAC [Pham et al. 2004], Mobile S-MAC, was the first MAC approach considering 
mobility for WSNs. The mobility-aware S-MAC version is an extension of the 
synchronized MAC layer protocol S-MAC. This extension aimed to be the best solution 
for mobility support in WSNs, supporting both stationary and mobile networks and 
being energetically efficient.  With MS-MAC when a mobile node enters in a stationary 
network, the surrounding nodes in a range area, defined by the R variable, will form an 
active space with synchronized periods between shorter intervals than in the normal 
operation. Those shorter intervals are useful to support and to monitor the movement of 
the new neighbor. This extension was only tested by simulation, and as it is based on S-
MAC it suffers of the same problems. MS-MAC defined a shorter period for S-MAC 
Neighbor Discovery Period (NDP) in the mobile nodes and increases the complexity of 
the nodes introducing the link quality variable to detect the movement. It also requires 
an extra listening time for neighbors, mainly for the boundary nodes, in order to detect 
mobile nodes that are spending extra energy. 
 
MOB-MAC [Raviraj et al. 2005], Mobile MAC, appeared to solve the problem of MS-
MAC regarding the number of retransmissions caused by the high frame lost rate, which 
increases the energy consumption. MOB-MAC solves this problem by introducing 
adaptive frame sizes, considering the link quality. If the link quality is poor then the 
frame size is reduced, guaranteeing less energy consumption to transmit and decreasing 
the probability of error, which is proportional to the frame size. However, when the link 

 23 
quality increases the frame size should also increase meeting the critical time 
application requirements. This solution may present some limitations when pre-defined 
frame formats must be used to guarantee the application reliability. In most of the real 
scenarios, there is one or more pre-defined frame format which makes it difficult to 
make them adaptive regarding the link quality.  
 
MMAC [Ali et al.  2005] , a Mobility-Adaptive Collision-Free MAC Protocol for 
Wireless Sensor Networks, is a schedule-based MAC protocol capable to adapt the 
frame time, transmission slots and random-access slots concerning mobility. Based on 
TRAMA, this solution is based on the mobility prediction to previously adapt the 
conditions and to allow a more efficient synchronization of nodes. When there are 
several mobile nodes moving, if the frame time is fixed, each node will have to wait a 
considerable time until joining the network. However, using adaptive frame time, it is 
possible to reduce the time proportionally to the number of nodes moving, guaranteeing 
that they are going to join the network as quick as possible. The problem of MMAC is 
the high complex scheduling algorithm used to calculate the transmitter of each slot in a 
frame time. Besides, MMAC assumes that each MN is aware of its own position and 
based on that predicts the mobility pattern. This assumption may not fit in all real 
scenarios, which truly limits the applicability of this solution. 
MAMAC [Bing et al. 2007], Mobile Adaptive MAC, gathers the better of two worlds: 
the synchronized and desynchronized MAC protocols. MAMAC proposes a simple 
mechanism where nodes, mobiles or not, are not equipped with synchronized watches. 
Instead, each node wakes up between random periods sending always an 
acknowledgement beacon.  When a node wants to transmit, it starts to listen to the 
environment until receiving the acknowledgement beacon from the destination. After 
that the node starts the transmission. When comparing the time of channel occupation, 
this protocol is the most efficient, because each transmission only requires the ACK 
message and the data transmission instead of RTS and CTS used, for instance, in S-
MAC. According to the authors MAMAC was the solution for high mobility but they 
did not provide any practical evaluation of it. 
 
AM-MAC [Choi et al. 2008], Adaptive Mobility MAC, is a MAC protocol based on S-

 24 
MAC in which each node has a periodic listen-sleep schedule that periodically 
broadcasts through a SYNC message. AM-MAC claims the support of mobility by two 
methods: a secondary listening period and a smart scheduling adaptation. Nodes using 
the same schedule constitute a virtual cluster having each virtual cluster its own 
schedule. Border nodes are deployed between virtual clusters, operating in both 
schedules. When a MN listens a SYNC from a border node, whose schedule does not 
belong to its virtual cluster, the MN learns that it will probably handoff to another 
virtual cluster, using a secondary listen period to quickly perform the scheduling 
adaptation. On the other hand, if the MN moves without listening to the SYNC of the 
border nodes and enters directly in another virtual cluster, it will perform the schedule 
adaptation to the new schedule. Although AM-MAC outperforms S-MAC, it will 
energetically exhaust the border router like in the MS-MAC solution.  
MD-SMAC [Hameed et al. 2009], Mobility aware Dynamic S-MAC, is the combination 
between MS-MAC and DS-MAC, in an attempt to obtain a mobility aware solution 
capable to handle delay-sensitive applications, maintaining the low energy 
consumption. MD-SMAC basically proposes changes to the respective protocols. In the 
case of DS-MAC, MD-SMAC proposes that once achieved a pre-defined energy level 
threshold, the MN must revert the duty cycle to its initial format (remember that DS-
MAC doubles the duty cycle to improve latencies), improving lifetime. Regarding MS-
MAC, MD-SMAC removes the old schedule when the MN is in the next cluster (MS-
MAC keeps both schedules by default, which consumes more energy). Besides, MD-
SMAC also reverts the Neighbor Discovery frequency to its initial form as soon as the 
MN is in the new cluster with the new schedule.  In MS-MAC this frequency changes 
from 5 minutes to 30 seconds, when the MN is moving between clusters, but it never 
reverts to the original form. Although this solution will improve the network lifetime, 
latency will be affected and therefore the support for delay-sensitive applications 
questioned. 
MMH-MAC [Bernardo et al. 2010], Mobile Multimode Hybrid MAC protocol aims to 
be a protocol capable to maintain the low energy of the asynchronous solutions and the 
high throughput of the synchronous, even in the presence of mobile nodes. Hence, this 
solution implements both, synchronous mode for stationary nodes and asynchronous 
mode for mobile nodes. In the asynchronous mode it uses preambles and a frame called 

 25 
SHUT-UP used to stop the preambles. The synchronous mode is used by neighborhood. 
SYNC messages are sent to synchronize the internal watches and to operate as a beacon 
to detect neighborhood changes. In other words, when a mobile node arrives to a new 
cluster it starts to send preambles until it gets a SHUT-UP message. After the SHUT-
UP the mobile node receives a SYNC message and synchronizes with the 
neighborhood. This solution was implemented in a simulation and in a real 
environment.  
 
CFMA [Khan and Ali 2011], Collision Free Mobility Adaptive MAC for WSNs, is a 
new MAC protocol capable to outperform S-MAC, MOB-MAC, AM-MAC, MS-MAC, 
MD-MAC and DS-MAC by introducing a novelty concerning the way that the backoff 
values are allocated. Usually, MAC protocols compute a random value to decide the 
access period of each node. However, CFMA-MAC proposes the use of pre-defined 
delays, assigned by the Cluster Coordinator (which forces to use a cluster based 
network), implementing different levels of priority for each mote, based on the delay 
values. For instance, when a new node enters in the cluster it must has the maximum 
priority, which means the shortest delay, to assure its quick inclusion in the network. At 
each transmission the node asks to the coordinator the delay for the next transmission. 
In the case of a MN, it must also monitor the signal strength of the adjacent clusters. 
When that signal strength is increasing, the MN asks to the Coordinator for a delay to 
access the adjacent cluster. Thus, when arriving into that cluster the MN can 
communicate as quickly as the delay allowed, which is must faster than requiring the 
delay only after the movement. CFMA-MAC was analyzed and compared with the 
mentioned solution only via MATLAB. Although it shows to be superior, there is not 
any further implementation to evaluate it in real platforms. Besides, all the compared 
protocols are based on S-MAC, which does not make clear how good this solution is 
when compared with other types of solutions. Furthermore, forcing a cluster-based 
network could not fit in all the scenarios and thus limit its applicability. 
 
MoX-MAC [Dame et al. 2011], Mobility X-MAC, is a non-synchronous MAC protocol 
similar to X-MAC, but optimized for mobility support. In X-MAC whenever a node 
wants to transmit it sends a set of short preambles until it receives an Acknowledge 
notifying that the target is awake and the transmission can start. Applying X-MAC to 

 26 
MN would affect a wide number of nodes, because the range would be different along 
the MN’s path. Therefore, with MoX-MAC the MN is capable of listening to any ACK 
first to check whether there is a transmission happening. By learning the schedule 
through the ACK the MN knows when it can start sending data. MoX-MAC is 
compared with X-MAC, but the results show a slightly advantage only in some cases, 
not being clear how significant that advantage is. Table 2 resumes the described 
protocols.

Table 2 shows that more than mobility support, the approached algorithms remain 
under-evaluation and most of them are still based on simulation. Therefore, there are 
some questions that must be evaluated. S-MAC is well established but limited. B-MAC 
and X-MAC are the most used but were not designed considering mobility. 
Nevertheless, X-MAC with short preambles can also support mobility avoiding the 
complexity of MS-MAC and MAMAC, by a simple adaptation as proposed by MoX-

 28 
MAC. Moreover, as it is possible to conclude, mobile aware algorithms at the MAC 
layer are heavier and more difficult to implement. Consequently, it is not desirable to 
support complex handoff mechanisms at this level. Furthermore, looking at the whole 
frame we can conclude that most efficient protocols are specific for one type of solution 
or network and not for global application, which limits their applicability and 
interoperability.  
 
Considering this panorama we defend that mobility must be fully implemented at layer 
3, using only data from layer 2 for mobility estimation. Therefore, in the next section 
we analyze mobility at the network layer, considering not only the WSNs based 
solution, but also conventional solutions designed for non-constrained networks. 
 
 
22.4. Mobility at the Network Layer  
 
Although mobility is possible to be supported at the MAC Layer, there are several 
inconveniences to do it. On the one hand supporting mobility at the MAC layer requires 
high complex algorithms to match efficient duty cycle schemes reducing energy 
consumption. On the other hand there are critical situations where high performance and 
reliability is required, which means that MAC protocols must be strict and efficient 
enough to guarantee such reliability levels. Adding mobility support to those protocols, 
such as LL-MAC, will affect their performance. Hence, it is important to evaluate the 
possibility of guaranteeing the mobility support at the network layer, in order to leave 
the MAC free of complexity. The appearance of IPv6 in WSNs, through the 6lowPAN 
concept, has contributed to a series of new possibilities aiming to achieve the global 4G 
and Internet of Things (IoT) concept. 6lowPAN allows the extension of the Internet to 
the real things, allowing running conventional protocols on constrained networks, such 
as ICMP, UDP or even HTTP. This also opens the possibility to explore the potential of 
MIPv6 and MIPv6 based solutions to handle mobility in WSNs, since we already 
concluded that MAC Layer solutions are not as effective as required.  
Hence, in this section we first introduce 6lowPAN evaluating then existent IPv6 based 
solutions to support mobility.  

 29 
22.4.1. 6LowPAN 
 
6LowPAN is an IETF Working Group (WG) aiming to adapt IP technology into 
802.15.4-based networks. This group aims to design the support of IPv6 over the 
standard IEEE802.15.4, in order to import the IPv6 well known capabilities to WSNs 
(such as Neighbor Discovery (ND) or Mobile IP (MIPv6)). 6lowPAN aims also to make 
possible the use of upper layer protocols from conventional networks in lowPANs 
(UDP, TCP, HTTP; etc.). At the moment we are writing this thesis, 6lowPAN is 
concluding its work, guaranteeing the basis for an efficient interoperability. 
 
6lowPAN [Bormann and Mulligan 2009] WG proposed a special layer to provide the 
integration of IPv6 over IEEE802.15.4 MAC Layer.  
6lowPAN target items are: 
➡ IP adaptation/Packet Formats and interoperability. 
➡ Addressing schemes and address management. 
➡ Network management. 
➡ Routing in dynamically adaptive topologies. 
➡ Security, including set-up and maintenance. 
➡ Application programming interface. 
➡ Discovery (of devices, services, etc). 
➡ Implementation considerations. 
 
From this list, this WG has now three released RFCs, namely RFC 4919 [Kushalnagar 
et al. 2007], RFC 4944 [Montenegro et al. 2007] and RFC 6282 [Hui and Thubert  
2011]. The first presents an overview, defining assumption, identifying problems and 
establishing goals to improve IPv6 over lowPANs. The second defines how the 
transmission of IPv6 packets over IEEE 802.15.4 must be done, specifying the frame 
format and the adaptation layer. Meanwhile, the newest RFC 6282 replaced this RFC. 
This last is responsible for packet fragmentation and reassembly, providing the 
connectivity between the MAC frames and the IP packets, following an optimal mode.  
 
The 6lowPAN middle layer provides an interface to allow the transmission of IPv6 
packets over IEEE802.15.4, compressing/uncompressing the headers and 

 30 
fragmenting/defragmenting the packets/frames. Even being the maximum size of IPv6 
packets longer than the maximum size of IEEE802.15.4 MAC the applications working 
over IEEE802.15.4 MAC, should not send those packets. However, when necessary the 
designed intermediate layer can perform not only the normal header compression but 
also the packet fragmentation and reassembly.  This layer also provides support for 
mesh network and broadcast messages. 
 
The main advantages of IPv6 in lowPANs, are: 
 
➡ The stateless address auto-configuration. 
➡ The Neighbor Discovery, allowing a better routing manages.   
➡ The anycast, which allows requesting a specific area or set of nodes instead a 
specific node. 
➡ The native multicast, allowing a request to a specific group of users [Silva et 
al. 2008]. 
➡ The native support of node mobility. 
 
The disadvantages of IPv6 application in lowPANs are completely related to integration 
with IEEE802.15.4, forcing this middle layer (6lowPAN) to make both protocols 
interoperable.  
 
IPv6 provides a mechanism called Neighbor Discovery (ND) (RFC2461) [Narten et al. 
1998], which among other functionalities, implements a node and service discovery 
mechanism. However, due to the several broadcasts presented in ND, the original 
version is not suitable to apply in lowPANs. 
 
Within the 6lowPAN WG, some interesting ideas to adapt ND to WSNs have appeared. 
“Load adhoc routing” [Yoo  and Kushalnagar 2007] defined a method called LOAD to 
provide route discovery, managing data structure and maintaining local connections. 
”Hierarchical-routing” [Kim et. al. 2007] introduced the issue of dynamic address 
assignment for hierarchical routing. According to which, motes are programmed to 
discover the existing lowPAN and perform self-configuration. “LowPAN Neighbor 
Discovery Extension” [Chakrabarti and Nordmark 2008], proposes optimizations to 

 31 
Neighbor Discovery minimizing the multicast of Router Solicitations and 
Advertisements, while [Toutain et al. 2008] proposes the suppressing of the Neighbor 
Discovery Router Advertisements. This draft applies to the unnecessary knowledge of 
the global address by the sensor node. In this case, nodes should be configured with a 
previously defined anycast L2 address, which would be substituted if the node received 
any Router Advertisement (RA). Finally, in [Shelby et al. 2011] a completely adapted 
version of Neighbor Discovery was achieved and is waiting to become an RFC. This 
draft proposes new addressing mechanisms and duplicates address detection for 
lowPANs. 
It allows too the use of stateless address assignment, Neighbor Discovery proxy (to 
allow the interoperability among all lowPANs) and optimization of the Router 
Advertisements. This draft introduces the concept of a Router Edge (RE) per network. 
 
MIPv6 are under the same constraints, but in an initial stage. [Mulligan et al. 2008] 
identified the mobility scenarios, as previously identified in this section ‚(e.g. network 
coverage, changing connectivity, etc.), the main challenges and security issues. It also 
approaches the need of mobile networks, referencing NEMO [Devarapalli et al. 2005], 
an extension of Mobile IP that considers the movement of the entire network. In [Shin 
et al. 2007] authors also proposed a series of possible scenarios and solutions to support 
mobility in 6lowPANs. Authors considered not only intra and inter mobility but also 
network mobility, basing their solution in NEMO.  
Also within 6lowPAN and developed in the scope of this thesis, we proposed an 
adaptation for MIPv6 application in 6lowPAN [Silva and Silva 2009]. This draft 
proposed the compression and adaptation of all MIPv6 headers and messages in an 
attempt to reduce the overhead and thus to save energy. In [Custódio et al. 2011] this 
draft was evaluated and compared with the original MIPv6. This evaluation is presented 
later on this document (Chapter 4 – section 4.3). 
 
Currently, there are three well-known implementation of 6lowPAN. Two of them were 
developed based on the operating system TinyOS-2.x and one third was developed for 
ContikiOS.  
The first implementation, also known as 6lowpancli, was released in 2007 as a result of 
an MSc thesis [Harvan and Schönwälder 2007], being now integrated as a native 

 32 
application in TinyOS-2.x (6lowpancli). At the moment, it supports header compression 
(accomplishing with the HC1 format), fragmentation and addressing, as well as IPv6 
Stateless Configuration. Such Stateless Configuration requires the previous manual 
setting of the network address prefix. Regarding high-level protocols, 6lowpancli 
supports UDP, accomplishing with the HC_UDP format and ICMPv6 messages. Hence, 
it is possible to ping motes and use UDP sockets from any Internet point to a small 
sensor node. UDP supports send, receive and notification. 
  
To implement the bridge between the WSN and the conventional network, the same 
implementation provides a daemon to emulate an IPv6 tunnel over IEEE802.15.4, 
allowing the communication, through, for instance, the USB port of any laptop.   
Therefore, 6lowpancli is completely static requiring manual configuration. It does not 
support any type of Node discovery mechanism like Neighbor Discovery, neither 
support for mobility like MIPv6. Besides, the support for mesh network is not provided 
and when a packet with a different destination address is received, it is just dropped.  
 
B6lowpan [Hui and Culler 2008], then renamed to Blip, was the second released 
implementation to TinyOS-2.x. The code is not included in the TinyOS-2.x core, but 
can be found in the contributions from Berkeley. Implementing the basic features 
defined in RFC4944, namely header compression, fragmentation and addressing, 
b6lowpan also supports ICMPv6 and UDP packet, but without acknowledge of send 
done. Besides that, the last version introduced the first prototype of a TCP stack. Blip 
implements also Neighbor Discovery, in a light version, configuring a link local address 
on nodes boot and a global address if received any Router Advertisement. The current 
version, Blip 2, follows the header compression format of the draft HC-06 and supports 
RPL and CoAP. Until now there is not a solution supporting the newest RFC 6268. 
 
As 6lowpancli, Blip includes a daemon to create an IPv6 tunnel to connect conventional 
networks and WSNs. As part of the Sink Node application, the daemon can run in any 
Unix based machine, including ND in the daemon. Unlike 6lowpancli, b6lowpan 
supports mesh networks, which as defined by RFC4944 are known as “mesh under”. 
Mesh-under means that from the IP level all nodes are one-hop distance, being that 
multi-hop is only performed by IEEE802.15.4 (MAC layer). 

 33 
 
SICSlowPAN [Durvy et al. 2008] is the first implementation released for ContikiOS. 
SICSlowPAN is based on RFC4944, implementing header compression, fragmentation 
and addressing. Besides that, it goes further and implements interoperability support 
based on  [Hui et al. 2008] and new header compression mechanisms defined in [Hui 
and Thubert 2009]. This solution also does not support the latest RFC6268 yet. 
SICSlowPAN does not implement mesh under. Over SICSlowPAN, there is a uIPv6 
layer implemented and, under SICSlowPAN the MAC Layer. Therefore, on one hand, 
when the MAC layer receives an IPv6 packet, it calls the SICSlowPAN to adapt the 
packet from the MAC to the IP layer. On the other hand, when the uIPv6 needs to send 
a packet, it also calls the SICSlowPAN but now, to adapt the packet in the other way 
round, i. e., from the IP to the MAC layer.   
ContikiOS implements per default a non-IP protocol called RIME at the MAC layer, a 
uIP stack implementing IPv4 and now a uIPv6 stack. Theoretically, the uIPv6 is 
independent of any above layer [Durvy et al. 2008], being possible to run over 
IEEE802.15.4, IEEE802.11 or 6lowPAN. However, over IEEE802.15.4, to full support 
the IPv6 integration, SICSlowPAN is required. The uIPv6 supports ICMPv6, 
implements Neighbor Discovery and supports UDP and TCP. Currently in version 2.5, 
RPL is also supported. 
Although ContikiOS implements IPv6 and 6lowPAN as completely different platforms, 
the 6lowPAN WG is currently working on issues that are included in the uIPv6 (eg. 
ND) and not in the SICSlowPAN. By the same reason, b6lowPAN changed its name to  
blip, justifying that it is more than 6lowPAN. The next table summarizes the three 
implementations. 
 
Table 3: Summary of the thr ee analyzed implementations. 
 6lowpancli Blip-2.0 SICSlowpan 
OS TinyOS-2.x TinyOS-2.x ContikiOS 
ICMPv6 YES YES YES 
UDP YES YES YES 

 34 
TCP NO Prototype YES 
ND NO YES YES 
RPL NO YES YES 
Mesh under NO YES NO 
Route over NO YES NO 
Mobility  NO NO NO 
 
  
As it is possible to conclude, 6lowpancli is the simplest implementation supporting only 
the basic ICMPv6 and UDP. On one hand it means that for developers it can be a good 
platform to work in. However, on the other hand, it can be really poor for final users, 
and/or a quite difficult to be optimized. Blip is the most complete solution, 
implementing not only the RFC4944 but also a light Neighbor Discovery mechanism to 
automate the network. SICSlowPAN has the advantage of supporting TCP connection. 
However, TCP is an undefined issue, considered to be complex and heavy for WSNs. 
6lowPAN WG has not approached TCP support. Besides, SICSlowPAN does not 
support mesh under. But according to the authors, new route over techniques will 
overcome the issues of routing/forwarding. In the scope of this analysis we evaluated 
the three solutions in a real platform. The details and obtained results are presented in 
the Annex A of this thesis.  
The next section presents possible IP Mobility solution to apply over 6lowPAN. 
22.4.2. IP Mobility 
 
If mobility is hard to support at the MAC layer, bringing it to the network layer will 
imply the participation of IP solutions. Although in WSNs IP support remains to a 
restrict set of applications, the truth is that conventional and industrial solutions such as 
ZigBee or WirelessHART, do not offer mobility support. Therefore, we aim to analyze 
the potential of IP based mobility solutions to apply in WSNs, not only to support the 
mechanism itself but also to assure the required reliability level in critical scenarios. In 

 35 
this section we survey the most popular Mobile IP solutions, with the objective of 
finding the most suitable approach for WSNs. 
 
The main objective of Mobile IP is to guarantee the connectivity of a mobile device, 
moving between heterogeneous networks, in a completely transparent mode to the 
higher layers. This protocol was initially developed to IPv4 (MIPv4) [Perkins 2002], 
and later adapted and included in the IPv6 (MIPv6)[Johnson et al. 2004]. 
MIPv4 introduced the concepts of Home Agent (HA), Foreign Agent (FA), 
Correspondent Node (CN), Mobile Node (MN), Care of Address (CoA) and Mobility 
Binding Tables. Home Agent consists in the local entity responsible by the management 
of local Mobile Nodes (MN) keeping their mobility information in the Mobility  Binding 
Table (MBT). The Foreign Agent, in turn, consists in the external entity, located at the 
foreign network to which the Mobile Node (MN) moved. Whenever a MN arrives to a 
foreign network, the FA assigns it a Care of Address (CoA), registers that information in 
its MBT and notifies the HA, which in turn updates the corresponding entry in the local 
MBT.  
The Correspondent Node (CN) is a static or mobile node, located or not in the same 
network of the MN, with which is in communication. When the HA receives a message 
to the MN it checks in the MBT whether it is in the local network. If so, the HA just 
delivers the message. Otherwise, the HA gets the CoA, encapsulates the message and 
forwards it to the FA, creating an IP Tunnel. When the FA receives the message it 
checks the CoA in the MBT and delivers the same to the MN. In the opposite direction 
the FA may forward the message from the MN to the CN through the conventional 
routing mechanisms or using the tunnel through the HA. 
HA and FA are periodically broadcasting Agent Advertisement messages to the 
respective networks in order to verify changes in the existent devices. Whenever the 
MN moves to a new network it can wait for an Agent Advertisement or, instead, it can 
send an Agent Solicitation, to quickly announce itself to the new FA.  
 
The MIPv6 introduced significant changes in the previous version, based on the native 
capabilities of IPv6. Implemented has a specific message type of IPv6, MIPv6 becomes 
more flexible and easy to use. On the one hand, the use of more than one address, global 
and link-local, associated with the address configuration methods of IPv6 made it 

 36 
possible to remove the FA entity, becoming the own MN responsible for generating its 
own addresses. On the other hand each MN (and CN) has its own Binding Cache, which 
is used to keep information about other MN. It is the responsibility of each MN to 
maintain their information updated in their HA agent and in the Binding Caches of the 
MN with which they are in communication, using the Binding Updates messages.  
In the case where the CN wants to communicate with a MN, it first checks whether CoA 
exists in the Binding Cache.  If so, the CN directly sends the message to that CoA, using 
the IPv6 Routing Header Option. Otherwise, it will send the message using the 
conventional mechanism, which will force the HA to encapsulate the packet and create 
the IP Tunnel to forward that message, like in MIPv4. When the MN receives the 
encapsulated packet it will then notify the CN about the new CoA, in order to guarantee 
the direct communication in the next transmission and performing route optimization, 
avoiding the triangulation effect. 
 
Besides the MIP protocol, there are many other solutions to support mobility in 
conventional networks. In the following description we choose the most important 
solutions considering their singularity and reliability in the manner the handoff is 
processed. The Hierarchical Mobile IPv6 (HMIPv6) [Soliman et al. 2005] was 
specifically designed to minimize the handoff time in the cases of micro or intra 
mobility. The Mobile IP Fast Authentication (MIFA) [Diab et al. 2004] allows the local 
authentication reducing the handoff load in the MN, moving it to the network side. The 
Fast Handovers (FMIPv6) [Koodli 2009]  is based on MIP but it assures a minor delay 
in the handoff time through the pre-configuration of the CoA in the MN, before the 
movement. The Mobile SCTP [Riegel et al. 2007] uses several IP addresses to identify 
the same MN. To conclude, the PMIPv6 [Gundavelli et al. 2008] presents a solution 
based on additional entities in the networks capable of handling the handoff on behalf of 
the MN. A detailed analysis of these solutions is presented in the following paragraphs.   
 
HMIPv6 
 
This handoff mechanism was developed to improve the performance of MIPv6, by 
splitting local and global mobility. The objective of this mechanism consists in reducing 

 37 
the time needed to register the MN in the network, by locally processing the handoff 
and thus supporting intra-mobility with higher efficiency.  
This mechanism defines the existence of a new entity, named Mobility Anchor Point 
(MAP). This entity is basically a local HA capable of supporting several Access Routers 
(AR), which define the range limits of the MAP network and are responsible for 
continually broadcasting information about the MAP. In this solution, the MN has two 
types of CoA: the Regional Care of Address (RCoA) and the On-link Care of Address 
(LCoA). While the first is the address used to identify the MN out of the visited 
network, the second is used to identify the MN in the visited network. This mechanism 
allows the MN to freely move within the visited network without informing the HA or 
any CNs in contact. 
The MAP keeps a Mobility Binding Table  containing the information about the MN, 
associating the RCoA to the LCoA. The ARs continually announce the MAP in a way 
that whenever a MN enters in the network it can use the information contained in those 
announcements to self-configure its RCoA. Each time the MN moves within the MAP 
network it will only handoff between ARs, which will only force to an LCoA update, 
remaining the MN reachable through the RCoA. 
 
MIFA 
 
The Mobile IP Fast Authentication (MIFA) protocol has as main objective to improve 
the performance of the registration process between the MN and the HA, without 
introducing secondary devices in the middle. The method developed to achieve that 
improvement consists in the delegation of the registration process of the MN in the FA, 
allowing the MN authentication locally. Thus, the MN does the registration in the FA 
and in the HA, being that those operations are both executed only by the FA. When the 
MN sends a RegRqst to the FA, this in turn replies with a Registration Reply message, 
acknowledging the success of the process. Then it is recursively created a tunnel 
between the FA and the previous FA until reaching the HA, originating a unique tunnel 
from the HA to the actual FA. 
 
In order to guarantee the local authenticity of the local MN, the FA must be a member 
of a recognized Fas group, known as Layer 3 Frequent Handoff Region (L3-FHR). This 

 38 
group can be statically constituted through specific algorithms or dynamically through 
the own network. The L3-FHR is generally constituted by a shorter number of FAs 
when compared with the whole network. Each FA belongs to its own L3-FHR, which in 
turn is not obligated to be constituted by all the adjacent FA, which will depend on the 
physical conditions and scenario. 
 
The security among FAs can be statically defined, for instance by the network 
administrator, or dynamically by the network. The MN local authentication is 
guaranteed through the notification messages sent by the FA to its L3-FHR group while 
the MN is connected to it. Those messages contain security data between the MN and 
the FAs of the L3-FHR group and the security data between the FAs and the HA of the 
MN. This security information is kept by each FA of the group, and is eliminated when 
used by one of them. Additionally, those messages can contain information regarding 
HA attributes and/or authentication data needed by the MN in its next FA registration.  
 
The MN sends the registration request to the new FA, which in turn verifies the local 
authenticity of the MN through the security information sent by the previous FA to the 
L3-FHR group. Furthermore, the FA uses the extra information about the HA to check 
whether it corresponds to its requirements. Once the authenticity is confirmed, the new 
FA sends a notification to the previous FA notifying this one to forward all packets 
whose destination is the MN. Afterwards, the FA replies to the MN registration request. 
Guaranteed the local registration, the FA sends a notification message to the HA 
informing about the new registration. In response to such notification the HA 
establishes an IP tunnel to the new FA. This type of mechanism allows the support of 
global mobility, like MIP, and also local mobility, like HMIP. 
 
FMIPv6 
 
This handoff mechanism follows the same concept of MIPv6 regarding the avoidance of 
the triangle routing, with the difference that in this case it is possible to reduce the 
overall latency.  The method found to achieve such reduction consists in removing to 
the handoff process, the CoA configuration. The MN configures the new CoA before 
starting the handoff, keeping always the connectivity avoiding data losses. Hence, once 

 39 
the handoff process is initialized, this mechanism also reduces the packet losses, 
through two methods. The first method consists in the use of an IP tunneling between 
the router of the source network (PAR) and the router of the destination network 
(NAR). The second method consists in the use of a buffer of packets in the NAR. When 
the MN changes the network, the packets addressed to it are not arranged by the PAR, 
but instead forwarded to the NAR, which keep them. Once the MN is in the new 
network it notifies the NAR that in turn sends all the packets kept in the buffer during 
the handoff.  
 
Mobile SCTP 
 
This handoff mechanism is an extension of the SCTP protocol, which provides mobility 
support. The main characteristic of the SCTP protocol is to allow that a single node can 
be reached through several IP address. One of those addresses is used as the main 
identifier, while the remaining addresses are used as a backup, to activate whenever the 
main addresses fails. The handoff process was thus designed for the case where the MN, 
supporting several address, is communicating with a static host. During the handoff 
process the main address is dynamically changed. When the MN arrives to a foreign 
network it first obtains the new IP address and then, using the mSCTP control 
messages, adds that address to the host association table, making it the main address. 
 
Proxy MIPv6 
 
Proxy MIPv6 (PMIPv6) was standardized in RFC5213 and describes a method to 
support nodes mobility using a network-based procedure. To do so, PMIPv6 defines 
two extra elements, namely the Local Mobility Anchor (LMA) and the Mobility Access 
Gateway (MAG). All the traffic to/from the MN is forwarded via the LMA, which 
creates a tunnel with the current MAG. Upon arriving in a new network the MN sends a 
Router Solicitation that is received by the local MAG. After receiving the solicitation 
the MAG will send a Proxy Binding Update (PBU) to the MN’s LMA, containing the 
MN ID and the profile, which includes, among others, the MAG address (proxy-CoA). 
The MN’s LMA, in turn, will create a Binding Cache Entry (BCE), which includes the 
MN-ID, the MAG address and the new MN prefix, and send a Proxy Binding 

 40 
Acknowledge (PBA) to the MAG. When PBA is received, the MAG creates the bi-
directional tunnel to the LMA and sends a Router Advertisement to MN, containing its 
Home Network Prefix (HNP). Hence, almost all the process runs in the networks side, 
operating the MAG on behalf of the MN. Nevertheless, when moving from one MAG to 
another, the MN needs first to detach from the previous one before attaching to the new 
MAG, which might lead to packet losses.  
 
Based on the handoff mechanisms presented above, there are some conclusions we can 
achieve considering packet losses, packet reordering, blackout period and the addition 
of extra elements. Although a packet loss is a factor directly dependent on the 
application type, by analyzing the handoff mechanisms we can conclude that MIPv6 
and HMIPv6 are susceptible to provoke losses. On the other hand, to avoid packet 
losses and guarantee a soft-handoff mSCTP makes use of multiple IP addresses and 
Fast Handoff uses different paths. Both situations might provoke the occurrences of 
packet reordering affecting the transmission rate. Nevertheless, the same does not occur 
with MIFA, because in this case the HA continues to forward data using the old path 
until receiving a new MN registration and define the new path. The blackout period in 
the handoff mechanisms is only avoided by the mSCTP protocol, which uses, as 
previously mentioned, several addresses to identify the same MN. Another difference 
between the mechanisms approached regards to the use of additional devices in the 
network. In this case, the HMIPv6 solution makes use of additional devices, as well as 
the PMIPv6 solution. 
While the base, MIPv6, provides the mobility procedure control needed in common 
networks, the remaining solutions, built on the first, were designed to improve the 
procedure in some defined aspects. Some of them were designed to speed up the 
handoff (FMIPv6), while others to add more reliability (MIFA, PMIPv6). Table 4 
summarizes this comparison.
