As we specified during this comparison, these mechanisms were all designed and 
developed specifically for non-constrained networks. Therefore, we must evaluate 
whether they can be applied in WSNs considering their impact in energy consumption 
and handoff time. Besides, it is not clear how reliable enough are these solutions for 
application in critical scenarios, such as GINSENG. 
As we could learn so far, the support of mobility in WSNs is not a straightforward 
mechanism, involving and affecting all the network components. We have analyzed 
several proposals but none of them demonstrated an efficient mobility support 
associated with a reliable service. Most of them considerably affect the overall 
performance decreasing the general quality of service (QoS). Nevertheless, maybe QoS 
mechanisms could be used on top of mobility support, to assure performance. Next 
section surveys Reliability QoS in WSNs and analyzes whether these features could 
effectively help to improve mobility performance. 

 42 
22.5. Mobility and QoS in WSNs 
 
Reliability in conventional networks is commonly seen as a problem overcome by 
transport protocols and sometimes by Quality of Service mechanism. Transport 
Protocols like TCP could be used to guarantee reliability in WSNs, however it would 
introduce large overheads, forcing compressing and complex mechanism, not suitable 
for constrained networks. Besides that, to guarantee reliability in WSNs it is necessary 
to control the transport not only from the Transport layer, but also considering a cross-
layer solution [Pereira et al. 2007]. 
 
2.5.1. Reliable Mobility 
 
Reliability in WSNs is classified in packet reliability and event-reliability. Packet 
reliability forces that all packets are well-transmitted and received, without losses, while 
event-reliability requires only that at least one packet informing about the event is 
successfully transmitted.  
 
Reliability regards two different modes: hop-by-hop reliability or end-to-end reliability. 
Hop-by-hop control is more energetically efficient than end-to-end solution, being here 
where TCP fails. TCP is an end-to-end based protocol, and in a multi-hop scenario 
forces that all acknowledged and synchronization messages run the entire path, i. e., all 
nodes several times [Willing and Karl 2005]. For that reason, several hop-by-hop 
transport protocols, approaching reliability in WSNs, have appeared. A transport 
protocol must provide an orderly transmission, flow and congestion control, loss 
recovery and in some cases QoS, regarding timing and fairness [Wang et al . 2006].  
 
[Wang et al. 2006] also surveys the existent transport protocols for WSNs, classifying 
them into three main areas: congestion control protocol, reliability guarantees and both. 
Congestion control reflects also in reliability because if a link is congested packets will 
be dropped and therefore reliability affected. Congestion control protocols are 
responsible for detecting, notifying and performing rate-adjustments. For instance, the 
protocols Fusion [Hull et al. 2004] and CODA (Congestion Detection and Avoidance) 
[Wan et al. 2003] implements congestion detection based on the queue length, while 

 43 
CCF (Congestion Control and Fairness) [Ee and Bajcsy 2004] and PCCP (Priority-
based Congestion Control Protocol) [Wang et al. 2006a] use packet service time.  
Congestion control, reliability guarantees or both can be used for mobility estimation, 
serving as metrics to decide the handoff. 
 
Protocols regarding reliability are usually split into upstream and downstream, 
considering the data flow direction. Well-known upstream reliable protocols are ESRT 
(Event-to-Sink Reliable Transport) [Sankarasubramaniam et al. 2003], RMST (Reliable 
Multi Segment Transport) [Stann and Heidemann 2003], RBC (Reliable Bursty 
Convergecast) [Zhang et al. 2005] while well-known downstream protocols are 
GARUDA [Park et al. 2004] and PSFQ [Wan and Campbell 2002]. ESRT is event-
reliable and does not implement loss detection and notification, neither loss recovery. 
RMST and RBC implement packet-reliability, loss detection and notification using 
NACK and IACK respectively and hop-by-hop loss recovery.   
Within the downstream reliability protocols, PSFQ, a packet reliability protocol, 
implements loss detection and notification through NACK and hop-by-hop loss 
recovery, while GARUDA is quite more complex. In order to be more scalable 
GARUDA implements what is called a two-tier two-stage loss recovery, remaining the 
NACK for loss detection and notification. A deep analysis of these protocols and a few 
more can be found in [Wang et al . 2006].  
 
Considering both, congestion control and reliability, STCP (Sensor Transport Protocol) 
[Lyer et al. 2005] is the most well-known protocol. STCP is announced as a generic 
transport layer protocol for WSNs, considering end-to-end upstream control. STCP uses 
the queue length to detect congestion on the intermediate nodes. These, in turn, notify 
the Sink Node about the congestion, delegating all responsibility on it. For loss 
detection and notification both ACK and NACK messages are used. Loss recovery is 
performed from end-to-end. STCP is an event and packet reliability protocol.  
 
Excluding STCP, all the remaining presented protocols cannot achieve the desired 
performance while looking just to one side. It is necessary to consider and deal at the 
same time with congestion control and reliability, in order to better control the first and 
better guarantee the second. Besides that, all the protocols only considered one-direction 

 44 
streams, up or down, and neither one considered congestion control and reliability in 
both directions. The distinction between end-to-end and hop-by-hop is in the literature 
also identified as a problem [Wang et al. 2005], as well as the distinction between 
packet-reliability and event-reliability. 
 
Concluding this short summary about reliability in WSNs, it is possible to affirm that 
there are as many as different Transport Control Protocols trying to guarantee 
reliability. However, all of them are focused on specific network characteristics or 
applications, remaining the lack of a really generic solution. Transport Control 
Protocols even capable to guarantee reliability, are not ready to work over mobility 
protocols. Besides that, regarding mobility, reliability must be controlled from the 
handoff mechanism, independently of the transport control mechanisms chosen.  
 
Related with reliability is the supported or required Quality of Service. In WSNs QoS 
has been maidenly approached. The next section presents a brief summary. 
 
22.5.2. Quality of Service 
 
In [Kim et al. 2008] the use cases of 6lowPAN networks are described in the format of 
IETF draft. Parallel with the last evolution of Neighbor Discovery for 6lowPAN 
[Shelby et al. 2011], the possible applications have become better defined. The same 
document identifies the need of Mobility and QoS, among others, as dimensions for 
scenarios categorization. Mobility is a natural ability since nodes are completely self-
powered and wireless. Although not regularly approached, the support of mobility has 
been recognized from the beginning. However, Quality of Service has been hidden or 
avoided. Under such specific characteristics, like resources constrained nodes, data 
redundancy, limited communication protocols, complex duty cycle schemes, high 
number of network elements or highly dynamic scenarios, Quality of Service is as much 
complex as demanded. 
 
[Chen and Varshney 2004] and [Younis et al. 2004] identified the necessitation of QoS 
in WSNs. Both recognize that through the lasts years many researchers have been 
working on WSNs, however all of them have followed the same topics, namely: 

 45 
optimized duty cycle schemes, energy-aware routing algorithms or communication 
protocols. Since many of the WSNs applications are intended for critical scenarios, as 
for indication as for control, QoS is highly mandatory.  
 
[Chen and Varshney 2004] surveys the QoS in WSNs describing two different 
perspectives: Application QoS and Network QoS. Whereas, Application QoS is not 
really approached since it is based on external factors, Network QoS is supported by 
three basic delivery models: event-driven, query-driven and continuous delivery 
models, each one characterized by some factors like: end-to-end communication, 
interactivity, delay tolerance or critical level. For the authors, most event-driven 
applications in WSNs are interactive, delay intolerant, mission critical and non-end-to-
end application. Most of the query-driven applications in WSNs are interactive, delay 
tolerant, mission critical and non-end-to-end application. Lastly, the continuous mode, 
as the name suggest is when nodes send continuously data at a specific rate, which can 
be real-time and non-real-time data. Real-time requires bandwidth and is delay 
constrained, while non-real-time does not. The authors also assumed the possibility of 
the coexistence of more than one factor, forcing a mix of QoS characteristics.  
 
[Younis et al. 2004] identified the requirements of QoS in WSNs presenting them by 
layer. Starting with the Application Layer, the authors defined the system lifetime, 
response time, data novelty, detection probability, and data reliability and data 
resolution as the QoS requirements at this level. At the Transport Layer, authors 
identified reliability, bandwidth, and latency and cost, while at the Network Layer path 
latency, routing maintenance, congestion probability, routing robustness and energy 
efficiency were the main identified requirements. In [Younis et al. 2004] lower levels 
were also approached, defining middle-layers like Connectivity Maintenance and 
Coverage Maintenance. The QoS requirements defined for Connectivity Maintenance 
were: Network Diameter, Network Capacity, Average Path Cost, Connectivity 
Robustness and Connectivity Maintenance. For Coverage Maintenance the main issues 
are Coverage Percentage, Coverage Reliability, Coverage Robustness and Coverage 
Maintenance. Since WSNs are very MAC Layer dependent, the importance of QoS at 
this level is crucial to the support of all of the previous described requirements. 
Properties like Communication Range, Throughput, Transmission Reliability and 

 46 
Energy Efficiency are mandatory for the network properly operation.  Also in Physical 
Layer Channel Speed, Coding and RF Power are important for QoS support. 
 
[Chen and Varshney 2004]  and [Younis et al. 2004] pointed the main issues of Quality 
of Service in WSNs. However, even recognizing the existence of mobile nodes, the 
presented solutions did not take them in consideration. 
 
[Sohrabi et al. 2000] proposes one of the first algorithms for WSN that considers QoS. 
Sequential Assignment Routing (SAR) was proposed for routing decision in multi-hop 
multi-path WSNs. With SAR routes were decided based on three parameters: energy, 
QoS supported by each path and the type of traffic. Thus, it also introduced the traffic 
differentiation in WSNs. Another approach, SPEED, is presented in [He et al. 2003] 
and it is a real-time QoS aware routing protocol, for end-to-end guarantees. The 
application estimates the necessary speed for a considered delay taking into 
consideration the distance to the Sink Node. Based on SPEED, MMSPEED [Felemban 
et al. 2006] allows several traffic classes and services differentiation. Each type of 
traffic is dynamically connected to a required deadline which means that paths are 
dynamically assigned according to their capability/congestion in order to accomplish 
with the assumed timeliness. To assure the reliability a probabilistic multi-path routing 
mechanism is provided. 
 
This brief summary presented the main advances on QoS in WSNs. The integration of 
QoS in mobility, supporting mobility estimation and assuring the required reliability 
and performance level, are extremely important to determine, in the future, which are 
the main aspects to consider and what is the best way to assure QoS and to manage 
mobility based on QoS parameters.    
 
Having been presented the most relevant work in mobility, reliability and QoS, the next 
section presents some related project developed mainly in the academic field while 
section 2.7 briefs the WSNs role in the industrial automation systems of today.  
 
 

 47 
22.6. Related Projects 
 
This section presents concluded and ongoing projects that have been approaching 
subjects related with this thesis.  
 
AWARE [Ollero et al. 2009] is a European project whose main objective is to develop a 
middleware for the functionalities required to allow the communication between aero 
vehicles, like helicopters, and ground sensor networks. Those networks comprise 
mobile nodes, carried by ground vehicles or people, which means that both Sink and 
Nodes can be mobile bodies. Moreover, nodes are heterogeneous, which means that the 
middleware must support several different devices and operate as an abstraction layer to 
the application level.  
AWARE is now in the third year; presenting a considerable list of publication, as a 
result of all work done. Regarding mobility, this project does not approach handoff 
techniques. Instead, aware project consider that mobile node must connect to new points 
as fast as possible, and then the middle layer deals with the connectivity questions. An 
adaptable routing protocol was developed to assure nodes reachability [Erman-Tüysüz 
et al. 2008].  
 
City Sense [Holloway et al. 2009] project is a project from Harvard University and 
BBN Technologies, whose main objective is to deploy about 100 sensor nodes 
dispersed in the city of Cambridge, MA. Static and vehicular sensor nodes must collect 
data about weather and pollution conditions. This project aims also to provide an open 
urban large-scale platform for worldwide researchers, allowing them to test their own 
application there. Microsoft and the American Nation Science Foundation sponsor 
CitySense.  CitySense is the example of project where mobility could improve coverage 
and therefore the efficiency, through the deployment of mobile nodes in vehicles with 
pre-defined and controlled routes, such as city buses. CitySense does not handle 
mobility itself but aims to interface with mobile and low power sensors, obtaining data 
from heterogeneous mobile devices, such as cellular phones or the mentioned vehicular 
sensors.  
 

 48 
WISEBED – Wireless Sensor Network Testbeds [Fischer et al. 2009], is an FP7 
European Project whose main objective is also to provide a large-scale but well-
organize structure considering heterogeneous nodes. This project aims to deal with 
hardware, software, algorithms and data, and also to provide, in the future, the final 
structure for other research groups. Since WISEBED is considering dynamic and 
heterogeneous nodes, mobility should be a supported feature and therefore the project 
outcome can be a useful platform for mobility evaluation. 
 
CHOSeN is also a European Project [Rodig et al. 2009], launched to develop specific 
applications for real large-scales Wireless Sensor Networks, in critical scenarios such as 
automotive and aeronautic. CHOSeN considers these WSNs as Smart Wireless Sensor 
Networks, in such a view that it assumes the heterogeneity of nodes, regarding 
individual requirements. CHOSeN aims that the resultant Smart Network becomes 
interoperable with other types of networks, such as vehicular networks. Therefore, 
mobility is an intrinsic feature to the objective of this project; even until the current date 
no outcomes about it have appeared. CHOSeN is composed by several partners and 
comprises from chips design to application software development, including also MAC, 
Network and Transport protocols design.  
 
e-Sense [Presser et al. 2009] is a project that aims at the exploration of Ambient 
Intelligence through Wireless Sensor Networks. The idea is to create a context-
capturing framework that accepts and converges different inputs, thereby focusing on 
energy efficient operation of the network. The assumed scenarios are equipped with 
multiple sensors and use heterogeneous, partly mobile nodes in highly variable 
numbers. Besides, e-Sense consortium has presented some related work, mainly 
regarding sink mobility [Basagni et al. 2008]. The e-Sense framework also specifies 
middleware components and the integration with beyond 3G mobile communication 
systems. e-Sense uses a pre-planned deployment approach and even has a tool for 
simple network planning. In addition to that, e-Sense uses a TDMA based network 
setup. 
 
RUBICON [O’Hare et al. 2011], an FP7 European Project started in April 1 st 2011, 
aims to develop a self-learning robotic network, constituted by sensors, effectors and 

 49 
robots. The objective is to develop robots capable to adapt to the environment, learning 
through the different interaction. This robot ecology can be used in several targets such 
as assisted living and security among others. By using their mobility and sensing 
capability robots can intelligently adapt to each situation. Although, mobility support 
(from the communication perspective) is an active mechanism in this project, it is still 
in its early days and therefore there are not scientific contributions regarding mobility 
support. 
 
Aiming to deliver a global WSAN (Wireless Sensor and Actuators Network) framework 
capable of providing services and applications through universal interfaces, SENSEI  - 
an FP7 European Project [SENSEI 2007], was released in January 1 st 2008. This project 
is focused on providing a highly scalable architectural framework, an open service 
interface and corresponding semantic specification, an efficient WSAN island solution 
and finally a Pan European test platform for long term evaluation of WSANs in the 
Future Internet. Although SENSEI main objective does not target mobility directly, 
some related work has been presented in the scope of this project, targeting mobility of 
multiple sink nodes [Basagni et al. 2009], routing of mobile elements [Conti et al.  
2008] and studying the impact of mobility in the SENSEI traffic [Tomic et al. 2010]. 
 
HOBNET [Nikoletseas et al. 2010], another FP7 European Project, targets the 
development of solutions for Future Internet applications focused on energy efficient 
for smart buildings. This project bases on large scale and heterogeneous wireless sensor 
networks, deployed to efficiently monitor and control entire buildings, maximizing their 
efficiency while reducing the energy requirements. HOBNET is designed to use all-
IPv6 through 6lowPAN. Besides, the work package 2 of the project is specific to 
network protocols and architectures, including heterogeneous and mobile devices. This 
project has been producing scientific contribution regarding mobile sinks, mobile data 
propagation and indoor tracking based on mobile nodes.  
The next table summarizes the approached projects.

As it is possible to conclude from Table 5, there are some projects where mobility is 
already assumed, even from different perspectives. Although node mobility support is 

 51 
the most crucial feature to assure reliability in mobility-aware scenarios, sink mobility is 
the most approached solution. Nevertheless, from all projects where sink mobility is 
addressed, included in scientific publications, the majority only approaches it from the 
theoretical side.  
In summary, this section reviews the most important projects in the area that are aware 
of mobility in WSNs. As we can conclude, none of them directly approaches node 
mobility, which from our point of view is the main lack in WSNs. The efficient support 
of node mobility is demanding to assure the reliability level required for critical and 
also non-critical applications.  
 
Apart from these academic projects, parallel research has been done within the 
industrial scope. Since this work is integrated in a real industrial case study – 
GINSENG – whose objective is to deploy controlled performance WSNs in an oil 
refinery, it is necessary to study existent alternatives and if possible to analyze their 
support of mobile elements. Next section presents the most important contributions. 
 
22.7. Industrial Automation 
 
Industrial Automation systems are highly demanding, requiring the maximum 
reliability, scalability, security and adaptation to different and critical environments. 
Considering wireless communication, signals can suffer from different problems when 
operating under special industrial conditions. Temperature or hazard environments can 
change drastically the communication signals, which means that Sensor Networks must 
be able to deal with that. Zigbee Alliance was born in this scope aiming to guarantee the 
interoperability between different manufactures. Zigbee Alliance was designed to 
operate on IEEE802.15.4, standardizing the higher layers. The work on Zigbee started 
before the definition of IEEE802.15.4, but after its release, both parts joined together. 
IEEE802.15.4 is responsible for PHY and MAC Layers while Zigbee is responsible for 
the upper layers, namely: routing, interoperability data networking, security and control. 
Zigbee ensures the interoperability among products from different manufactures, being 
nowadays widely used in monitoring systems, standardizing entire applications such as 
buildings automation, remote control, smart energy, health care and home automation 

 52 
among others [Zigbee 2012].  Although many of these solutions comprise mobility 
Zigbee does not support the same, as demonstrated in [Sun et al. 2007]. 
 
Specifically launched aiming to wirelessly support industrial automation, ISA100 
Committee [ISA100 2012] scopes wireless and control system manufactures. Also 
constituted by several partners, ISA100 comprises a set of standards, such as: The  
Automation Engineer’s Guide to Wireless Technology, in which part 1 refers to the 
radio technology and part 2 refers to a review of technology for tracking of active 
elements.  ISA100 also defines “ Wireless User Requirements for Factory Automation”, 
where a set of use cases, description of factory topologies and recommendations are 
presented. This committee also released another standard comprising trustable wireless 
system in industrial environments. Although considering the use of WSNs, the 
committee has not approached mobility so far. Besides, the released standards are not 
freely open to the community, forcing to be purchased. 
 
Another well-known example is WirelessHart, the Wireless version of the massively 
used Hart protocol [Hart 2012].    
WirelessHart is a Wireless Mesh Network Communication Protocol designed 
specifically for process automation applications. Created from Hart protocol, a wired 
protocol used for more than 20 years, WirelessHart aims to allow the easy deployment 
of new or additional nodes to new or existent networks, guaranteeing the same 
reliability and interoperability of the wired Hart based solutions.   
WirelessHart is IEEE802.15.4 based protocol, using Time Division Multiple Access 
(TDMA) to establish links based on timeslots and frequency. The links can be dedicated 
to provide minimal latency services, or shared to optimize bandwidth rationalization. 
Security is achieved through AES-128 block ciphers in conjunction with Session and 
Data-Link level Network keys.  
 
In WirelessHart messages have different priorities, implementing a simple QoS 
mechanism. Split in 4 categories, data packets can be categorized as Command (high 
priority), Process Data, Normal and Alarm (lowest priority). For example, high priority 
is implemented at MAC layer by assigning a dedicated link, guaranteeing minimal 
latencies. WirelessHart, even based on IEEE802.15.4, introduced a modeled protocol 

 53 
stack in order to achieve the most reliable solution. The biggest advantage of this 
version is the interoperability with the conventional solution, Hart, which is already 
massively used. However, WirelessHart is not considering the dynamic nature of a 
traditional Wireless Sensor Network, neither the possibility of mobile nodes. Therefore, 
the unique advantage it really brings is that it is easier to deploy than the wired well-
established solution. Regarding new applications, exploring network dynamic and/or 
mobility, WirelessHart does not bring new contributions at the moment. 
 
Behind WirelessHart, lives the Time Synchronized Mesh Protocol, also known as, 
TSMP [Pister et al. 2008], already introduced in this document. Dust Networks [Pister 
and Kristofer 2009], a company specialized in Industrial Wireless Sensor/Actuators 
Networks developed TSMP, IEEE802.15.4-PHY based protocol. Besides that, TSMP is 
being adopted for ISA100 protocols, since it proved to be as reliable and efficient as the 
desired.  
TSMP requires a centralized network with an entity responsible for schedule and 
coordinate the communication. Such require is crucial because TSMP uses time synch 
to schedule the communication channels, in order to avoid collisions. This technique is 
the basis for the already explained WirelessHart shared or dedicated links.  
TSMP has shown a great performance, as announced, with reliability greater than 
99.9%. However, even supporting large networks with thousands of nodes, it shows a 
considerable latency in such cases. Nevertheless, we must have in consideration that, 
even supporting high scalability, TSMP and all the derived protocols, do not support 
mobility. Without mobility, even within the Industrial Automation scope, the possible 
applications of TSMP are not global, and therefore, the WSNs role within the scenario 
will never be as important as possible.  
 
Millennial Net Mesh Scape [Hanssmann et al. 2009] is another protocol designed for 
Industrial Automation. Mesh Scape is a closed platform based on IEEE802.15.4-PHY. 
At layer 2 it uses the Millennial Net Protocol, which they announce as the industry’s 
longest battery life provider at sensor nodes, guaranteeing data delivering over fault-
tolerant links, using end-to-end redundancy. At layer 3, Mesh Scape uses a protocol 
called Persistent Dynamic Routing. The Persistent Dynamic Routing is a protocol 
capable of finding the best route for packet delivery on fly, and thus, to be more 

 54 
scalable and power efficient than the existent solutions.  
Millennial Net Mesh Scape supports three types of network topologies, namely, star, 
mesh and star-mesh hybrid. Star-mesh hybrid is announced as the optimal solution for 
high dynamic networks, considering mobility. Mobility is thus majorly supported by the 
Persistent Dynamic Routing, which detects and adapts to network changes. 
Nevertheless, it is not explained in detail how mobility is supported and in specific, how 
handoffs are performed.  
 
Industrial Automation is led by close platforms, controlled by the respective companies. 
Consequently, the detailed information about the used technology is mostly hidden. 
Nevertheless, WirelessHart is slightly different because it is based on TSMP, a well-
known and published protocol. Regardless of those questions, we would like to stress 
out with this brief summary about WSN in Industrial Automation, that mobility has not 
been approached, even when considered highly required by several applications. 
Regarding the high level of reliability and efficiency, demanded by Industrial 
Automation, mobility in this field has been avoided due to the complexity it might 
impose. However, once ready for Industrial environments, mobility is ready for the 
World. Therefore, a reliable mobility support for WSNs is thus the main objective of 
this thesis proposal. Next section concludes this chapter with related considerations. 
22.8. Considerations 
 
As we could observe during this chapter mobility in WSNs can be approached from 
several perspectives. Therefore we started by characterizing those approaches based on 
the mobile object and the objective of the mobility use, considering end-to-end 
reliability. Solutions designed to decrease power consumption by providing mobile 
base-station or to achieve more accurate indoor localization method, mobility in WSNs 
has been seen as a potential not only because of application requirements but also as a 
network improvement. However, most of those proposals ignore how this capacity will 
be handled from the communication point of view. Despite this diversity, we focused 
our research to just one case, namely the reliable node mobility. We believe that before 
using mobility to enhance the network we must assure an efficient and reliable mobility 
support to turn the application real.  

 55 
Regarding node mobility several approaches could be considered. Many solutions tried 
to efficiently support mobility at the MAC Layer. However, as we could observe these 
solutions become truly complex because of the existent requirements of the duty cycle 
mechanisms in use. Saving energy makes the MAC Layer too complex to add more 
features on it. Besides, adopting a MAC Layer solution will limit the duty cycle 
algorithm in use and therefore will make any solution dependent on a specific MAC 
protocol and/or application type.  
Hence, we targeted the use of Network Layer solutions capable of being used on top of 
any MAC Layer protocol. Based on the possibilities brought by 6lowPAN we analyze 
MIPv6 and based solutions to assure a reliable mobility support in WSNs. However, 
since these protocols were not developed for such constrained networks, their feasibility 
is not known in such environments. 
In addition we have analyzed the reliability and QoS in WSNs, in order to determine 
whether or not these existent mechanisms could be also considered toward a reliable 
mobility support. 
In the scope of the GINSENG project, we also study the existent industrial solutions 
and concluded that, currently, none of them support mobility with reliability and 
performance guarantees and thus GINSENG will be the first solution applied into 
industrial and critical environments to do it.  
 
Hence, after this deep analysis we believe that to achieve a reliable support of mobility 
in WSNs we need to consider the MIPv6 solutions. However, before evaluating the 
feasibility of MIPv6 and based protocols, to serve critical applications, we need to 
provide reliable mobility estimation mechanisms. Next chapter introduces a deep 
analysis to mobility estimation, including three new methods developed to trigger and 
enhance the handoff procedure in WSNs. 
  

 56 
  

 57 
Chapter 3  
Mobility Estimation 
 
Regardless of the method used to support mobility, there are some aspects that must be 
considered in order to efficiently detect and deal with the procedure. Mobility 
estimation is the most important aspect to be considered when the objective is to assure 
a controlled and reliable handoff. To achieve such, we present in this chapter a set of 
procedures proposed not only to detect the need to handoff on time, but also to adapt the 
handoff regarding the application requirements and also to predict the direction of the 
mobile node. Next section surveys the existent mobility estimation methods, section 3.2 
presents the feasibility of using GPS as a valid mobility estimation method in WSNs, 
while sections from 3.3 to 3.5 present new RSSI based mobility estimation methods 
developed specifically to enhance the mobility experience in WSNs. Section 3.6 
concludes this chapter by presenting final considerations. 
33.1. Handling Mobility 
 
In order to efficiently support mobility there must be mechanisms to detect that a 
specific node moved or is in motion. In cellular networks different approaches have 
been used to detect movement. For instance, 2G/3G uses a routing area constituted by 
adjacent cells of one Base Station (NodeB). Here the MN is responsible for periodically 
sending information about its current routing area. Recently, LTE controls mobility by 
constituting Tracking Areas set up by a set of adjacent cells, which may belong to the 
same or different Base Stations (eNB – Evolved Node B). Tracking Areas may also 

 58 
overlap each other increasing accuracy and allowing soft-handoff. Nevertheless, there is 
a drawback in this method, because the mobility detection in the latest cellular 
technologies is obtained through periodical signaling messages, included in the 
neighboring periodic list updates. In this list each MN periodically measures its link 
quality to each neighbor cell, monitoring its relative position. This behavior provokes 
high energy consumption and could never be applied to such constrained networks, like 
WSNs.  
In non-3GPP networks, such as Wi-Fi, mobility detection is basically provided at the IP 
level through MIP or MIPv6 mechanisms. In MIP there are different possible modes to 
detect that the MN is in a different network. One is based on the network prefix 
received. The second is via the lifetime field of the ICMP Router Advertisement 
message. If the lifetime expires the MN sends an Agent Solicitation messages, 
presuming that it is in a new network. In addition, in MIPv6 Neighbor Discovery 
optimizes this mechanism allowing the MN to periodically know its network based on 
Router Advertisement and Solicitations. Although efficient and totally independent of 
other levels, this solution provokes a considerable delay to trigger the handoff, which 
can drastically affect the application performance. Therefore, some alternatives 
appeared trying to speed up the triggering by using Link Layer information. The most 
popular based solution is the Fast Handover [Koodli et al. 2004] mentioned in the 
previous chapter. Fast Handover uses information from Link Layer to trigger the 
handoff, based on a specific pre-defined threshold. [Shin et al. 2004] proposed a similar 
solution but only focused on decreasing the latency by optimizing the AP scanning 
process, performed by the MN. Anticipated Handover [Montavont and Noel 2005], in 
turn, proposed not only a mechanism to get and analyze Link Layer information, 
detecting not only the next access point, but also the next network level configuration 
before the handoff. Once in possession of the new configuration the MN could quickly 
load the new configuration. If the original MIPv6 presents a high latency caused by the 
triggering method, using Link Layer information may limit the interoperability of the 
system. Besides, in all these optimizations the MN is responsible, like in the 3GPP 
solutions, by detecting its own movement, which leads to extra and periodical 
transmissions. 
  

 59 
Considering the WSN constraints some adaptations must be considered. First it is 
necessary to achieve an energy-aware method and second it is necessary to define a 
dynamic method to efficiently trigger the handoff. Due to the own technology 
constraints, handling mobility in WSNs poses several challenges, leading to the use of 
specific movement estimation methods. Using cellular based solutions or native MIPv6 
discovery methods would on the one hand rapidly consume the MN’s energy resources 
and on the other hand limit the MAC Layer duty cycle algorithm. Thence, an optimal 
solution for WSNs should combine Fast Handover - using Link-Layer information and 
Anticipated Handover – getting next configuration in advance, without interfering with 
the normal operation and adapting to the application requirements.   
In [Dong and Dargie 2012] the authors present several mechanisms for mobility 
estimation in WSNs, namely: pedometers to count steps, Radio Frequency Identifiers 
(RFID) with readers strategically deployed, anchor nodes with known position, Time of 
Arrival (ToA) and Angle of Arrival (AoA) methods, triangulation and trilateration, 
Received Signal Strength Indicator (RSSI), Sinal-to-Noise ratio (SNR), ultrasounds or 
GPS. Most of these mechanisms are related with localization methods and some of them 
require specific deployments and hardware, which may not be appropriate for WSNs. 
From this list, RSSI is the most popular solutions, while ToA, RFID and GPS have also 
been approached.  
 
RSSI is natively supported by IEEE802.15.4 being thus the most used metric for 
mobility estimation. Recognized by its short accuracy in wireless networks, RSSI was 
proved to be an efficient metric in WSNS [Srinivasan and Levis 2006]. RSSI can be 
obtained from the own communication process and does not require any additional cost, 
regarding new hardware or additional software modules. In [Parker and Langendoen 
2007] an RSSI based solution to detect mobility is proposed. This proposal 
demonstrated the feasibility of using this metric but did not specify any adaptive or 
dynamic method capable of efficiently enhancing the procedure.  
Time of Arrival (ToA) is also a useful and simple metric widely used. Although it is not 
natively included in IEEE802.15.4, it can be easily computed without the usage of extra 
equipment. In [Zaidi and Mark 2005] while the authors proposed an AR-1 (Auto 
Regressive) based mobility estimation scheme for WSNs, they also conclude that RSSI 
and ToA have similar behaviors. This study was performed on real GPS trace files, 

 60 
useful to validate the veracity of the estimation. As an alternative to ToA, which 
requires nodes synchronization, [Saeed et al. 2007] proposes a Round Trip Time (RTT) 
solution which is shown to be more flexible and accurate. Nevertheless, from these non-
invasive solutions, RSSI, ToA and RTT, RSSI is the one that requires less computation 
since it is natively integrated in the communication IEEE802.15.4 protocol. 
 
Unlike RSSI and ToA, using RFID or GPS solutions will require adding specific 
hardware to each mote or creating a dedicated infrastructure.  In [Zhang and Wang 
2006] RFID was integrated with WSNs, originating the concept of smart node, useful to 
track elements mainly in indoor situations. This solution requires additional 
infrastructure needed to read the RFID tags. Besides, it is not extensible to existent 
deployments, because RFID tags need to be integrated with the existent sensor nodes, 
which may not be possible.  
On the other hand, GPS is natively supported in some WSNs’ based hardware, such as 
the XBow MTS420 board for micaZ motes [XBow 2006]. Although GPS is not possible 
to use in indoor scenarios, its accuracy is attractive in any wireless solution. For 
instance, in the case of GINSENG, it would be useful to monitor the employees while 
working within the factory area (open space).  However, the power consumption may be 
too high to be supported on constrained networks, such as WSNs. Since there were not 
previous studies in this field we present in the next section an evaluation of the GPS 
feasibility in WSNs, to decide whether or not this solution can be considered for 
mobility estimation. 
33.2. Feasibility of GPS in WSNs 
 
Up to some years ago, the energy requirements of GPS systems were not compatible 
with their use in WSNs. However, current technology makes the integration of GPS in 
sensor nodes possible. Although the use of GPS in WSNs is not new, little or no 
evaluation of its impact on energy consumption has been made. With this in mind, we 
performed some experiments with the aim of assessing the energy requirements of using 
a commercial GPS solution in sensor nodes. 
We used Crossbow equipment: Micaz and mib520 modules. For the GPS support we 
used MTS420/400CC [XBow 2006]. We began the experiments by comparing the 
energy required by a sensor that was periodically monitoring its battery level with and 

 61 
without the GPS system. The MTS420CC board is composed of several digital sensors, 
which allow the measurement of temperature, humidity, pressure and light. It also 
includes an analog accelerometer and a GPS receiver provided by uBlox, the LEA-4A. 
MTS420CC is the newer version of this board family. The older versions, like 
MTS420CA/CB, also provide the same features. However, in the GPS case, they use a 
different receiver – the LeadTek GPS9546. 
The Crossbow platforms only support TinyOS version 1. Neither of these boards have 
an official driver for TinyOS version 2. In general, there is no guarantee of its 
functionality over TinyOS-2.x. However, for versions CA/CB, i. e., using the GPS 
receiver from Leadtek, there was a contribution provided by Rincon Research 
Corporation, which can be adapted. 
After having studied and analyzed all implementation code for versions CA/CB, we 
started the adaptation task. Hence, as the next step, we studied both datasheets, in order 
to adapt the Rincon code to the MTS420CC board. This was a very “low-level” task and 
required the adaptation of connection settings, including adaptation of the baud rate to 
9600 bps instead of the 4800bps of Leadtek and Nmea. After that, the driver for 
MTS420CC, adapted from the Rincon driver for versions CA/CB, was installed and 
worked successfully. The GPS receiver could then read the position and send an Nmea 
packet toward the base station. The objective of this study was to turn on the GPS 
receiver and to test both its feasibility and its impact on the battery consumption of the 
mote. To evaluate such parameter, one mote was programmed to read the battery level 
and the GPS values, and send them to the base station at each 5 seconds. All battery 
values were measured in mV, according to the sensor datasheet and using the voltageC 
component of TinyOS. To calculate energy consumption we used the Credit-Point 
(CREP) system [Younis and Fahmy 2005], which allows calculating the residual energy 
of batteries (Emax) in Joules. By following this method it is possible to calculate the total 
energy consumed during the experiment, in contrast with traditional methods in WSNs 
that allow measuring the time spent in listening, transmitting, processing and sleeping, 
applying then the respective current.  
The experiments were split in two sessions, the first using only IEEE802.15.4 and the 
second using 6lowPAN on top. In each session we measured the base system (without 
GPS) and with GPS. Although the impact of 6LowPAN is expected to be low, the fact 
is that the integration of IP in WSNs has already motivated heated discussions and 

 62 
debates. If, on the one hand, WSNs are energy limited and the TCP/IP stack is too 
complex, on the other hand the use of a thin version of IP can have several advantages. 
The integration of IP and sensors can be the basis for transparent communication 
between nodes and for providing Internet connectivity, thus eliminating the need for 
translation gateways.  
Figure 4 and figure 5 belong to the first session, corresponding respectively to the base 
and GPS setups. Both figures present the residual energy (J) in the tested mote during 
one hour.  
 
Figure 4: MTS420CC + MicaZ  + 802.15.4 without GPS. 
 
Figure 5: MTS420CC + MicaZ + 802.15.4 with GPS. 
 


 63 
The same experiment was then performed using the second session, in which 6lowPAN 
was introduced. Figure 6 presents the 6lowPAN base results, while Figure 7 presents 
the 6lowPAN GPS results.  
 
 
Figure 6: MTS420CC + MicaZ + 6lowPAN without GPS. 
 
Figure 7: MTS420CC + MicaZ + 6lowPAN with GPS 
 
As we expected, running on 6lowPAN would require more energy and therefore, GPS 
usage will create a greater impact as well. To have an overview on power consumption 
for each case, Figure 8 depicts the final results. As we can observe in Figure 8 using 
GPS significantly increases the energy consumption, mainly when applying this 
technology with more complete solutions such as 6lowPAN. While over raw 


 64 
IEEE802.15.4 GPS required an average of ~52J, when applied to a more complex 
network protocol, the consumption raised to almost ~215J, which cannot be accepted in 
such constrained networks. In addition, during our experiments the GPS module did not 
answer when the voltage was under 2.7V, which lead us to conclude that some kind of 
“cutoff voltage” is implemented. 
 
 
Figure 8: Summary of power consump tion under the different situations. 
 
Since the impact of GPS is considerably high, other mobility estimation methods must 
be considered. Because our main target is the GINSENG case study, using RFID to 
cover the whole scenario is not possible. Therefore, considering the remaining options, 
RSSI is the one that represents the most appropriate solution. It can be used in both 
indoor and outdoor solutions and it does not require any additional hardware. However, 
to be efficiently used some additional mechanisms must be developed to achieve not 
only similar results in WSNs like the proposals Fast Handover [Koodli et al. 2004] and 
Anticipated Handover [Montavont and Noel 2005], but also to provide an adaptive and 
dynamic solution capable of granting high performance even in critical solutions, such 
as the GINSENG scenarios. Hence, in order to achieve such, next section presents a 
new method to use RSSI as a mobility estimator using a dynamic threshold, while 
section 3.4 presents an evolution of this method using an adaptive threshold. Section 3.5 
adds a mechanism to predict nodes direction in order to allow the pre-load of the next 
network configuration.  


 67 
10 0xD8 -85dBm 
12 0xD6 -87dBm 
14 0xD5 -88dBm 
15 0xD5 -88dBm 
16 0xD2 Connection lost -91dBm Connection lost 
 
From these two tables it is possible to observe the impact of the environment conditions 
on the link quality. Apparently, in the open space without noise, the node achieves a 
higher distance. However, distance is not important. The important result of this 
evaluation is that in both cases, with completely different environments, the node 
achieves the R point at the same link quality level, around the 0xD5 hexadecimal value, 
equivalent to -88dbm. 
This evaluation helped to determine the R-value, and proved that it is not affected by 
the environment or application. In fact it is limited more by the environment conditions  
(Ei) than by the distance (D). Environment conditions (Ei) regards to all variables that 
can affect link quality, such as, distance, physical structures and cellular networks 
among others. 
If a node moves fast, passing through a set of Sink Nodes between a short period of 
time, it should have a longer period between C point and R point, called Δc, in order to 
give time for the handover, before it loses the connection. Hence, 
 
Δc   V x Ei 
 
V is the velocity and Ei the Environment condition. For instance, if the observed RSSI 
is -80dbm and knowing that the optimal is -46dbm, Ei equals to 34. As the velocity 
increases the Δc should also increase.  
 
 
Figure 9: RSSI values with handoff defined points represented. 


 68 
 
As we can observe in Figure 9, we have the representation of the optimal point and the 
Rupture point at the edges. Then, there is a variable Critical point, calculated based on 
the MN average speed and the Environment noise. This is the difference between the 
current point and the optimal point. Once in the Δc region, the MN, or any entity 
monitoring its behavior, must trigger the handoff procedure, guaranteeing the fastest 
and the most efficient mobility support possible, independently of the used protocol.  
Our presented solution is a method to guarantee an efficient and early initiation of the 
handoff procedure.  
However, considering that each application can have different requirements we present 
another alternative in the next section, which even considering the application is based 
on this initial proposal. 
 
33.4. Application-based threshold 
 
Our concept intends to guarantee the reliability of links between MN and parents by 
monitoring in real time the connection RSSI. As mentioned before, although RSSI was 
considered a non-reliable metric in conventional networks, in WSNs it is widely used 
and scientifically supported [Srinavasan and Levis 2006] 
In the presence of mobility, link quality metrics are crucial to control the mobile process 
and to assure a reliable connectivity across the path. Based on them, MN or parents 
must recognize what is a good link and what is not. Furthermore, both must recognize 
when the handoff must take place and how fast it must be. Thus, while in the previous 
section we presented an adaptive solution based on speed and environment conditions, 
in this section we present an alternative based on the application requirements, when 
considering the Packet Received Ratio (PRR). Although following different 
requirements, the objective of both solutions is the same, i.e., obtaining the most 
appropriate threshold from which the MN, or any responsible entity, must trigger the 
handoff procedure. While the first solution can be widely applied and does not 
guarantee any specific requirement, this solution is specific for critical applications, 
such as GINSENG, in which a specific PRR value is mandatory.  
To achieve the appropriate formula, several experiments were performed and the 
resultant data analyzed using MATLAB. Due to the characteristics of the output values, 
an interpolation was necessary to achieve the final result. 

 69 
Thus, in our evaluation we aimed to achieve a relation between PRR and RSSI, capable 
of assuring a certain percentage of packet delivery at a certain RSSI threshold. To 
achieve such, we ran an experimental setup in which two TelosB nodes programmed 
with ContikiOS were used. One node was configured as the mobile node (MN) and sent 
200 packets with an inter-packet interval of 100ms to the Sink Node. At each evaluation 
we increased the distance 1 meter between the sending and receiving nodes. This test 
was run in a controlled environment with both nodes in line-of-sight communication 
which assured us the best possible performance. 
From each received packet we saved the RSSI value. To control whether a packet 
arrived or not we stamped each one with a sequence id in the sender and controlled it at 
the receiver. 
Figure 10 depicts the obtained results. The RSSI values are the average of the received 
packets. 
 
Figure 10: RSSI vs PRR 
 
As we can see it is possible to assure a 90% packet delivery until the -80dBm. We can 
also observe that the number of packet losses was stable until the -70dBm with one or 
two losses caused by transient interference. Those losses slightly increased until the -
80dBm and started dropping after that.

 70 
In order to establish a relationship between the desired PRR and the RSSI threshold, the 
following 6th order polynomial formula was obtained: 
y = p1* z^6 + p2*z^5 + p3*z^4 + p4*z^3 + p5*z^2 + p6*z + p7 
 
In the equation above z is centered and scaled with z=(x - mu)/sigma , where mu = -
69.258 and sigma = 10.898. The norm of residuals is equal to 0.58771 and the obtained 
coefficients are:  
 
 %.#$"$$%(**,
 &.$"$%&*+&

 ).#$"$$'&-*
 *.#$"$$(*,+'
 +.$"--(%-
 
 
With this formula it is possible to determine the best RSSI threshold for the desired 
PRR, between the given interval, i.e., [-30;-90]. Hence, if for instance a critical 
application requires a PRR of 0.95 the chosen threshold should be approximately -78 
dBm. 
Using this formula we can guarantee that by using the same type of hardware, under 
similar conditions, we can assure the required application performance by triggering the 
handoff procedure in the exact time.  
 
However, triggering the handoff at the exact time may be insufficient in certain cases 
and therefore further mechanisms can be adopted. One of those mechanisms may be the 
pre-load of the next radio configuration, assuring that once in the new network, the MN 
already owns the new configuration avoiding extra-dealings. Following this idea, the 
following section presents a proposal based on this concept, whose main idea is to 
predict the movement direction. This additional feature is constrained by application 
and can only be applied to specific deployments. Although it cannot be widely applied 
we consider it an added value to speed up the handoff procedure. 
 
33.5. Direction prediction with pre-configuration 
 
 

 71 
The objective of this solution is to previously detect the MN direction in order to 
previously obtain the next configuration, providing it to the MN before the handoff. 
Once in the possession of the new configuration the MN only needs to load that 
information when detecting the need to handoff, using the methods presented in the 
previous sections. However, to efficiently detect the direction it is necessary to use 
localization methods and to know deployment details in advance. 
Localization methods in wireless networks have played an important role within the 
scientific community. Recently, with the popularity of Wireless Sensor Networks, 
wireless localization becomes even more desired. Designed to detect events while 
monitoring specific parameters, nodes’ localization is a requirement to accurately detect 
where the event occurred.   
Inheriting the concept from the conventional wireless networks, the localization 
methods in WSN operate, generally, by the same manner. In wireless networks, there 
are three common types to get a node localization [Chaczko et al. 2007], namely:  
• By knowing the distance and direction from a known point (x,y);  
• By previously knowing the direction from two known points (x1,y1) and (x2,y2);  
• By knowing the three known points (x1,y1), (x2,y2) and (x3,y3).  
 
From these three methods, the two first require directional antenna. Otherwise, it would 
not be possible to get directions. The most well-known example is the Angle of Arrival 
(AoA)[Chen et al. 2003] algorithm. The third method, based on the three well-known 
points can be easily used with ordinary omni-directional antennas and therefore is more 
suitable for common wireless networks, including WSNs. 
 
The most popular localization algorithms are based on the third method, assuming thus 
the previous knowledge of three existent points. Based on distance, those algorithms get 
such parameters from signal propagation. Some calculate the distance based on signal 
delay, while others calculate it based on signal strength. Time of Arrival (ToA)[Chen et 
al. 2003] and Time Difference of Arrival (TDoA) [Gustafsson and Gunnarsson 2003] 
are popular examples of signal delay based solutions. The first requires nodes 
synchronization in order to accurately achieve the three distances. The second does not 
require synchronization since it uses a signal correlation to compute distances.  

 72 
Knowing the signal strength [Patwari et al. 2001] in the sender and comparing it in the 
receiver, makes signal strength also a valuable and widely used tool to compute 
distances. Due to its simplicity and since RSSI is natively integrated in the standard for 
WSNs, avoiding further calculations, signal strength is the most used method to get 
nodes’ localization in such networks. 
 
For our direction prediction solution we do not need to get the accurate node position, 
but we do need to get the node movement direction. To do so with the common omni-
directional antennas present in wireless sensor nodes, we aim to estimate distances 
between existent fixed nodes with well-known positions, accomplishing thus with the 
described method 3. As a result, we are able to predict in which network the MN will be 
in the next time and thus speed up the handoff and registration process.   
 
33.5.1. Detection of Node direction 
 
In order to pre-configure the MN before the handoff process, it is necessary to predict in 
which network the node is going to enter. Hence, an accurate method to detect the MN 
direction is demanded to assure the assignment of the pre-configuration for the right 
network. 
Based on the GINSENG case study, the network is previously configured and deployed 
following a tree topology with the maximum of 3 levels and no more than 25 nodes. 
Although the leaf nodes can be mobile nodes, the position of all parents is known. 
Hence, based on the position of the immediate parent and the neighbor parent, it is 
possible to obtain the mote direction. The method we propose in this section is based on 
angles between relative vectors constituted by the parents and MN locations. Figure 11 
depicts the scenario.  
 

 73 
 
Figure 11: Direction detection scenario. 
 
Being the two Parents, P1 and P2 and the leaf node MN, our goal is to establish a virtual 
triangle with those 3 points and obtain the angles of P1MN^P1P2 ( ∂) and P2MN^P2P1 
(ß) processing the difference across the time. 
The first step is to get the coordinates of MN. Based on RSSI it is possible to obtain the 
distance between two nodes [Wang et al. 2009], which means that we need to 
periodically assure communication among the 3 participant nodes if we want to 
accurately monitor direction. Since we know that P1 and P2 are static we can assign 
relative xy coordinates. For instance, P1(1,1) and P2( d+1,1), where d is the distance 
between P1 and P2.  
Also knowing the distance between P1-MN and P2-MN we can assume that these 
distances are circle radius (r and q) in which MN is one intersection point (as shown in 
Figure 11). 
In the presence of the xy coordinates, the vectors P1MN, P1P2 and P2MN are computed 
and, using arccosine, the angles ∂ and ß are obtained. 
Through ∂ and ß the movement direction of MN relative to P1 and P2 is accurately 
obtained. By comparing the previous measurement with the actual measurement is 
sufficient to obtain MN direction, relative to P1 and P2, in degrees. 
This method is theoretically more efficient than to simply compare coordinates, because 
it requires less computational work and allows quickly establishing relationships with 


 74 
the existent objects. Next section presents the practical evaluation of the described 
method. 
33.5.2. Simulated Evaluation 
 
To test our method we implemented it in the ContikiOS simulator, Cooja [Osterlind et 
al. 2006]. We used one sink node with ID = 4, two parents with ID=2 and ID=3 and one 
MN with ID=1 (light blue). To implement mobility we used boonmotion [Bonn 2011] 
with the RandomWaypoint algorithm. The simulated motes were based on the sky 
platform, which is equipped with the MSP430 processor. We chose these motes because 
they are the same used in the Petrogal refinery, in the scope of the GINSENG Project. 
To implement our direction detection algorithm for MSP430 we lost some accuracy due 
to the lack of floating points support and also to the restriction size of integers. 
However, it was possible to achieve interesting results during our tests, proving the 
accuracy of our method. Figure 12 depicts the Cooja scenario, in which we evaluate the 
method. 
  
  


 75 
  
 
Figure 12: Tested scenario in Cooja. 
 
As we can observe in Figure 12, there are 4 motes presented in our evaluation. The one 
localized at the top is the sink node, while the two in the middle are parent nodes and 
the one above (ligh blue) is the MN. During the simulation time, 1 hour, the MN was 
moving between the two parent nodes. During that period we recorded 20 movements 
of the MN and consequently the values for ∂ and ß, which are presented in Table 8. 
 
Table 8: Angles obtaine d during the evaluation. 
 
Iteration ∂ ß 
1 66.4º 25.8º 
2 53.1º 25.8º 
3 25.8º 25.8º 
4 25.8º 45.6º 
5 25.8º 60.0º 
6 25.8º 70.1º 
7 25.8º 74.3º 
8 24.6º 74.3º 
9 24.6º 80.6º 
10 20.2º 80.6º 
11 17.7º 80.6º 


 76 
12 17.7º 70.1º 
13 17.7º 63.3º 
14 17.7º 50.0º 
15 17.7º 44.8º 
16 20.3º 47.6º 
17 46.9º 25.8º 
18 66.4º 25.8º 
19 74.3º 25.8º 
20 80.6º 24.6º 
 
 
Analyzing the values of Table 8 we can obtain the movement direction, through the 
angle difference at each movement. For instance, by calculating the difference between 
the first and the second movement, we know that the MN moved 13.3º to the right, 
maintaining the 25.8º of ß, which means that it was moving in direction to P2. Then, in 
movement 3 the MN continued straight to P2 and in movement 4 it suddenly changed 
direction, continuing moving to the right, but now in the opposite direction to P1.  
Following this method, direction can be lightly obtained and the next network predicted 
on time, which will be useful to pre-send the next configuration for MN. 
Since in the simulator Cooja each element has its own position transmitted in xy, it was 
possible to compare the results obtained through our method (which uses relative 
coordinates) and thus obtained, theoretically, using the Cooja position system. In the 20 
presented simulations, in different positions, we concluded that our method was 95.4% 
accurate when compared with the theoretical results. This difference is mainly caused 
by the implementation of our method without floating points, which are not supported 
by mspgcc. However, 4.6% error in the achievement of the MN direction should not be 
critical in a real scenario, where the number of existent adjacent networks is not so big. 
 
Thus, adding this feature to the proposal presented in the previous sections, we can 
trigger the handoff procedure on time, based on physical aspects, and also predict the 
nodes’ direction in order to get the new configuration of its radio in advance. 
 

 77 
33.6. Considerations 
 
To efficiently support mobility in WSNs it is important to provide an efficient 
mechanism and to assure that it is triggered at the right moment and, when possible, that 
all the necessary information to do it is previously known. 
In this chapter we presented a set of mechanisms to provide a reliable and efficient 
mobility estimation. Supporting mobility is not only to provide an efficient protocol to 
deal connections or to keep the trace of the MN. Before that, it is important to develop 
mechanisms to detect the movement and, if possible, to support the protocol, 
independently of its nature, with useful information in advance, in order to improve its 
performance and decrease the handoff time. Several methods may be used to perform 
mobility estimation. RFID solution, GPS or RSSI based solutions are the most popular 
examples for WSNs.  
In this chapter we started by analyzing the most popular solutions, such as GPS and 
evaluate their feasibility in WSNs. Then, concluding that a simpler solution could be 
better we developed two distinct mechanisms to detect mobility based on RSSI and to 
decide when the MN should handoff, based on a specific threshold. From our point of 
view, this threshold is mandatory for the application performance. Therefore, we 
developed two mechanisms considering a) the movement and environment 
characteristics and b) considering the application requirements. Besides, we also aim to 
provide mechanisms able to provide new configurations in advance. To do so, we need 
to get mechanisms to predict the next step of MNs and therefore we proposed also a 
solution capable of doing it. Nevertheless, we assume that many other possibilities may 
be used in the same context and we are not going to detail them further. 
Instead, our goal is now to achieve the best method to complete the handoff procedure, 
guaranteeing the fastest and most efficient mobility support. As we concluded in chapter 
II, based on the state of the art analysis, supporting mobility at the MAC Layer is highly 
demanded, interfering directly with the complex duty cycle schemes. Besides, 
supporting mobility directly at the MAC level would make it specific to one solution 
and not interoperable with other systems. Therefore, we aim to raise the mobility 
support to the Network Level, in which some solutions already exist for conventional 
wireless networks. Those solutions are mostly based on IPv6 and have not been applied 
to WSNs yet. Hence, in the next chapter we will study the feasibility of using IPv6 

 78 
based solutions to support mobility in WSNs. From this point forward all evaluations 
were performed using the mobility estimation procedure presented in section 3.4, with a 
required PRR of 90%, which means to a threshold of -80dBm. 

Chapter 4  
MIPv6 in WSNs 
 
Inability to meet the key requirement of efficient mobility support is becoming a 
major impairment of wireless sensor network (WSN). Many critical WSN 
applications need not only reliability, but also the ability to adequately cope with the 
movement of nodes between different sub-networks. Despite the work of IETF’s 
6lowPAN WG and work on the use of MIPv6 (and many of its variants) in WSNs, no 
practical mobility support solution exists for this type of networks. In this chapter we 
start by assessing the use of MIPv6 in WSNs, considering soft and hard handoff, 
showing that, although feasible in small networks, MIPv6 complexity leads to long 
handoff time and high-energy consumption. 
 
The use of IPv6 in WSNs is nowadays clearly identified as an added value. The 
capacity to establish communication with any single mote from any remote point 
extends the applicability of such networks and substantiates the concept of the 
Internet of Things (IoT), reducing the gap between the real and virtual worlds. 
Nevertheless, in order to effectively use IPv6 in WSNs, some adaptation is needed, so 
that the scarce energy and processing power resources of sensor nodes are not rapidly 
depleted. Although the work of IETF's 6lowPANWorking Group has led to clear 
advances in the area, mobility is still an open issue in WSNs, despite the fact that the 
use of IPv6 opens up the possibility of resorting to MIPv6 [Johnson et al. 2004]. 
The fact is that current standards do not efficiently support mobility, and this poses 
considerable obstacles to their use in WSNs, especially if they are being used for 

 80 
critical applications requiring high levels of reliability and performance. If achieved, 
adequate mobility support would be fundamental for fulfilling the long-made promise 
of WSN deployment in scenarios as critical and important as military, health or 
transport scenarios, for which the number of real implementations is still very low. 
 
44.1. Prototype-Based Evaluation 
 
While moving along a specific path, a mobile sensor node can cross several networks. 
Some of them might operate in the same channel and domain, while others might 
operate in different domains or even channels.  From an application perspective, such 
details are irrelevant and only connectivity is important. Therefore, the handoff 
process must be completely seamless to applications, which requires a mobility 
management abstraction. This abstraction can be provided by well-known protocols, 
such as MIPv6 or any of its variants, e.g., FMIPv6, MIFAv6 or PMIPv6. Although 
MIPv6 is a well-known protocol that provides standard mobility support, its 
application to WSNs has not been sufficiently explored, mainly due to the fact that 
MIPv6 was not engineered for use in resource-constrained networks. 
In this section we start by evaluating the use of MIPv6 in WSNs, measuring the 
required time to handoff between two networks and the energy spent by mobile nodes 
in order to perform this task. We chose not to evaluate packet losses since this is an 
application-dependent metric.  
In order to assess MIPv6 in WSNs, we implemented core MIPv6 functionality over 
the Contiki WSN operating system [Dunkels et al. 2004]. By core MIPv6 
functionality we mean Router Advertisements, with the Home Agent flag activated, 
Binding Update, Binding Acknowledge, Home Agents and respective Mobility 
Binding Tables. This implementation was done in C language and adapted to the 
ContikiOS net structure. To trigger the handoff we used Router Solicitation messages, 
as defined in Neighbor Discovery [Narten et al. 1998]. At this stage we did not 
implement the Return Routability procedure, which was left for future work.  
The main goal of the first two rounds of tests was to evaluate the feasibility of node-
based MIPv6 while performing soft-handoff and hard-handoff. In the first round we 
assessed MIPv6 while a single mobile node (MN) was moving between two networks 
belonging to different domains, although operating in the same channel. In the second 

 81 
round, we implemented, in a lab environment, a setup equal to the one used in the 
case study, where adjacent networks operated in different domains and channels. This 
means that the handoff process required that the mobile nodes load the new 
configuration in a new channel, which led to a disconnection period.  
All evaluations presented in this section were implemented and tested in a real 
platform, constituted by TelosB motes [XBow 2011], MSP430-based  [Texas 2011], 
using the CC2420 transceiver  [ChipCon 2011]. In all tests, handoff was triggered 
when the RSSI parameter dropped below the -80dBm threshold. 
44.1.1. MIPv6 implementation details 
 
This section provides details on the developed WSN MIPv6 implementation, used in 
all of the prototype-based evaluation experiments described in the chapter.  
Several MIPv6 implementations are available to the research community. However, 
none of them was specifically developed for WSNs, which means that it is not 
possible to run the existing code directly in motes due to their inherent constraints and 
limitations. Hence, in order to address the problems at hand, a ContikiOS-adapted 
MIPv6 implementation had to be developed.  
We focused our implementation on the strictly necessary, basic MIPv6 features, 
following RFC 3775 and 6lowPAN specifications as closely as possible, using 
stateless configuration whenever possible. As it was not essential for the tests, the 
Return Routability procedure was not implemented at this stage. Figure 13 graphically 
lists the implemented RFC 3775 functionality. This is briefly explained below. 
 

 82 
 
Figure 13: Implemented RFC 3775 functionality. 
Concerning section 6 of RFC 3775, we implemented the mobility header and the basic 
messages, such as the Binding Update, Acknowledges and Error messages (Table 9 a, 
b and c). The implementation contemplated not only the message structure but also all 
the necessary code to send and receive messages, integrating it with the ContikiOS 
UIP6 stack. 


 83 
 
Table 9: Implemented MIPv6 data structures. 
 
struct uip_mip6_hdr { 
    u8_t payload_proto; 
    u8_t header_len; 
    u8_t type; 
    u8_t reserved; 
    u8_t checksum; 
}; 
a) MIPv6 Header 
 
struct uip_mip6_bu { 
    u16_t seqno; 
    u16_t flagsreserved; 
    u16_t lifetime; 
}; 
 
b) Binding Update 
 
struct uip_mip6_back { 
    u8_t status; 
    u8_t flagsreserved; 
    u16_t seqno; 
    u16_t lifetime; 
}; 
 
c) Binding Acknowledge 
 
struct binding_cache { 
    uip_ipaddr_t haddr; 
    uip_ipaddr_t coaddr; 
    u16_t lifetime; 
    u8_t flags; 
    u16_t seqno; 
}; 
d) Mobility Binding Table 
 
In this case we based our implementation on the existing Neighbour Discovery (ND) 
implementation in ContikiOS.  
The implementation contemplated a Home Agent capable of performing the basic 
MIPv6 operations and dealing with the conceptual data structures specified in section 
10 of RFC 3775, including the mobility binding table (Table 9 d).  
The Mobile Node implementation (RFC 3775 section 11) followed 6lowPAN and the 
concept of stateless configuration, in which the mote constructs a global address 
based on its 64-bit MAC address and on the network prefix, obtained via the received 
Router Advertisement.  In order to allow for soft handoff, we also implemented 
section 11.5.3 (Using Multiple Care-of Addresses). Dealing with multiple addresses 
has been simplified in ContikiOS version 2.4, through uip-netif. 
 
In what concerns movement detection (RFC 3775 section 11.5.1), we did not use 

 84 
Layer 3 mechanisms in order to avoid energy-expensive Layer 3 broadcasts. Instead, 
Layer 2 RSSI-based mechanisms were used which, in addition to leading to lower 
energy expenditure, have the added benefit of being quicker. As explained in the 
previous chapter, the RSSI threshold chosen was -80 dBm, to guarantee a PRR of at 
least 90%.  
As an example of the developed implementation, Figure 14 presents the construction 
of the Binding Update message, while Figure 15 shows the construction of the 
Binding Acknowledge. 
In this implementation several macros were specifically created for MIPv6, following 
the style and approach of the remaining ContikiOS code. 
 
 
Figure 14: Building a Binding Update message in ContikiOS.  
 


 85 
 
Figure 15: Building a Binding Acknowledge message in ContikiOS.  
44.1.2. MIPv6 node-based soft-handoff 
 
The first round of tests, consisting of 40 test runs, was intended to assess the behavior 
of MIPv6 in WSNs, by measuring the time and energy a mobile node took to handoff 
between two networks, operating in the same channel (in this case – channel 11), but 
in different IPv6 domains.  
The mobile node was programmed to send an ICMP request message per second to 
simulate the application, to which the HA responded with an ICMP reply.  
Figure 16 depicts the network scenario, while Figure 17 and Figure 18 present the 
results for the handoff time and spent energy, respectively. The ContikiOS provides 
two alternatives for getting time values: clock time and rtimer. Although similar, 
rtimer has higher accuracy. 
 
 
Figure 16: Soft-Handoff between different IPv6 domains.

 86 
 
As we can observe in Figure 17 in a graphical way and in Table 10 in a numerical 
way, the obtained average handoff time between two WSNs operating in different 
IPv6 domains and in the same channel is approximately 772ms. Note that in this case, 
and due to the IPv6 capabilities, the handoff is simplified, as the node just adds 
another global address to its list of addresses. No transceiver reboots are needed and, 
therefore, the process is relatively fast and soft-handoff is possible.  
 
 
Figure 17: MIPv6 node-based soft handoff time. 
 
 
Table 10: Summary of MIPv6 node-based soft-handoff time values 
 
 


 87 
 
Figure 18: Energy required by MIPv6 node-based soft handoff. 
 
Table 11: Summary of MIPv6 node-based soft-handoff energy values 
 
 
Figure 18 and Table 11 present the energy required to perform the handoff, in 
graphical and numerical terms, respectively. The energy values were obtained through 
the ContikiOS Energest module. Energest measures the time used by each component, 
which is then converted into energy based on the used hardware and using a reference 
of 3V (the maximum, since motes were powered by USB). TelosB motes are 
composed of an MSP430 CPU, which consumes 1.8mA, a CC2420 transceiver that 
requires 18.8mA to receive and 17.4mA to transmit, and a Low Power Mode (LPM), 
which consumes 0.426 mA. 
As we can observe, while performing the handoff between two networks operating in 
the same channel, using MIPv6, the average energy expenditure was ~8.73mJ.  
The time and energy values presented above were obtained considering that the 
handoff process started at the moment the mobile node detected the RSSI level had 
reached the threshold value and ended when the Binding Acknowledge message had 


 88 
been received. Figure 19 illustrates the handoff protocol.  
 
 
Figure 19: Node-based soft handoff protocol. 
 
From the first round of tests we can conclude that MIPv6 is not only feasible in 
WSNs, but also that MIPv6 allows relatively fast soft-handoffs when both networks 
operate in the same channel, as the handoff time is much lower than the usual data 
rate for continuous data monitoring applications, which is normally in the order of 
seconds.  
 
44.1.3. MIPv6 node-based hard-handoff 
 
The second round of tests was intended to study the performance of node-based 
MIPv6 when dealing with hard-handoff, i.e., when the mobile node moved between 
two networks operating in different channels, forcing a connection disruption. In this 
test suite we used the same application, protocol and methods to trigger the handoff. 
However, since each mote was only equipped with one transceiver, it was necessary 
to disconnect from the previous network, load the transceiver with the new 
configuration and connect to the new one. In addition, once operating in a different 
channel, the MN could not directly send a BU to the HA. Therefore, we implemented 
a routing mechanism between the two networks, in order to forward the Binding 
Update and Binding Acknowledge messages. Figure 20 depicts this mechanism. 
Note that in these tests we assumed the best case scenario, in which the mobile node 
	 	 	









 89 
knew the channel of the next network. In controlled deployments – such as the one 
corresponding to the case study, or in any scenario where there is the need to provide 
performance and reliability guarantees – such information can be previously provided 
to the sensor nodes. However, in other cases alternative methods must be 
implemented, in order to determine the next network channel. This, of course, will 
require additional time and energy expenditure. 
 
 
Figure 20: Node-based hard handoff protocol exchanges. 
 
In this scenario, network 1 was operating at channel 11 while network 2 was 
operating at channel 12. Both channels were randomly chosen. MN movement was 
performed from network 1 to network 2, and handoff was performed using the 
protocol depicted in Figure 20, running on XMAC. Both clock time and rtimer were 
measured for 40 handoffs. The time to perform the complete handoff procedure, since 
the node detected that it had to handoff until it received the Binding Acknowledge and 
the energy spent while performing such procedure, were measured and are presented 
in Figure 21 and Figure 22, respectively. Numerical values are presented in Table 12 
and Table 13, respectively. 
 
	 	 	
