 90 
 
Figure 21: MIPv6 node-based hard handoff time. 
 
Table 12: Summary of MIPv6 node-based hard-handoff time values 
 
 
 
Figure 22: Energy required by MIPv6 node-based hard handoff. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 91 
Table 13: Summary of MIPv6 node-based hard-handoff energy values 
 
 
In Table 12 we can observe that the average of ~961ms to handoff between different 
networks operating in different channels is approximately 25% higher than the 
average obtained to handoff between different networks operating in the same channel 
and different domains. However, depending on the application, this value might be as 
acceptable as the one in the previous case. For instance, considering the GINSENG 
case study, in which a worker in the refinery carries a sensor node that monitors 
his/her vital signs in real-time, at speeds lower than 10m/s and networks with 100m 
range each node stays a minimum of 10 seconds in each network, which is more than 
enough to handoff. 
Analyzing the data in Table 13, we can observe that to perform a hard handoff, which 
includes a transceiver reconfiguration, mobile nodes required an average energy 
expenditure of approximately 11.76mJ. We can also observe that the majority of the 
energy was spent on transmitting and processing tasks. Noting the total range of 
~4.3mJ, and comparing it with the value in the previous round of tests (i.e., ~0.97mJ), 
we can conclude that, due to the routing mechanisms needed to complete the 
procedure, the time spent by the MN to perform handoff varies significantly. This 
variation might mean slightly higher or slightly lower values, occurred during normal 
operation, leading to a maximum energy expenditure of ~14.43mJ or a minimum of 
~10.12mJ, which, considering a AA battery powered mote, are within an acceptable 
range and prove the feasibility of the implemented MIPv6 version. 
 
44.2. Simulation-Based Evaluation 
 
The results presented in the previous section were obtained in a real platform, using 
controlled mobility and a limited number of sensor nodes, namely two sink nodes and 


 92 
one mobile node.  
In this section our objective is to present the results of similar experiments, this time 
performed through simulation, now considering there are additional sink nodes, a 
varying number of static, interfering nodes, and the mobile nodes use one of three 
possible mobility models. 
 
44.2.1. Simulation Overview 
 
WSN mobility is classified into three different strategies [Stevanovic and Vlajic 
2008]: random, predictable and controlled. In the first case, MNs move freely and 
randomly in a specific area. In the second case, the MNs paths are known in advance, 
while in the third case the MNs’ movement is controlled in real-time. The simulation-
based evaluation of MIPv6 was performed using these three different strategies.  
To simulate the mobility cases with the same code used in the implementation-based 
evaluation we chose the Cooja simulator/emulator [Osterlind et al. 2006], adapted 
with the mobility plugin. The main reasons for this choice were, on one hand, the fact 
that Cooja allowed us to use the same code developed for the real experiment because 
it is also based on ContikiOS and, on the other hand, the fact that Cooja, along with 
the Castalia WSN simulator, are widely recognized as the best simulators/emulators 
for WSNs [Abdolrazaghi 2009].  
The mobility trace files were generated based on Bonnmotion [Bonn 2011]. For the 
random strategy we chose to use the Manhattan model, according to which a 
destination was randomly chosen from the various possible destinations in a grid 
representing the roads of our real scenario, i.e., the refinery in which employees and 
vehicles can freely move along the defined roads. For the predictable mobility 
strategy case, we generated a trace file in order to guarantee that the MN moved in a 
continuous fashion between two distant points (x1,y1) (x2,y2), like a ping-pong ball 
obliquely crossing the field, whereas for the controlled strategy we manually placed 
the MN in the handoff area. The experiment area was limited to 10,000 m 2 and the 
MN speed was programmed to vary between 0.5m/s and 2m/s. To trigger the handoff 
we used layer 2 mechanisms, namely the RSSI value. The defined threshold was -
80dBm.  
For all three mobility strategies, we performed simulations with 2 and 5 sink nodes, 

 93 
and with 0, 2, 5, 10 and 15 additional static neighbor nodes, which generated 
background traffic. Applying these configurations to the two mobility approaches 
under evaluation - namely MIPv6 soft handoff and MIPv6 hard handoff - we obtained 
a total of 60 different scenarios.  For each one we collected the results of 20 handoffs. 
Additional simulation details are provided in next section. 
44.2.2. Simulation Details 
 
In order to support mobility in Cooja, through the described plugin, the tracefile 
generated by bonnmotion, described in the previous section, must be converted to the 
Interval Format. Figure 23 presents an extract of an Interval Format file that defines 
the first 10 seconds of events for a Mobile Node 0. 
 
Figure 23: Sample Interval Format file. 
As previously mentioned, the simulations were performed using three different 
mobility models, namely, random, predictable and controlled. Even though the 
controlled mobility model is simple to implement, random and predictable mobility 
models require substantially more complex settings. With Cooja, all three mobility 
models can easily be simulated and evaluated under different conditions, varying the 
number of sink nodes and static nodes per network.  
In the case of the random mobility model, the respective trace files were obtained 
using Bonnmotion, with random movement limited to 10,000 m 2 and following the 
Manhattan grid model. Figure 24 shows the generating command and associated 
output. 
 
Figure 24: Generation of random mobility trace file. 


 94 
 
Figure 25: Converting to the Interval Format. 
As it can be seen in the figure, to generate the trace-file using Bonnmotion, we first 
must specify the name of the file, followed by the mobility model and then the 
specific attributes. In this case, the –u and –v attributes are specific to the 
ManhattanGrid model and are used to define the size of each grid square, the –n 
parameter specifies the number of mobile nodes, the –d parameter establishes the 
simulation time in seconds, and the –x and –y parameters specify the simulation area 
in meters. Once the mobility file is created, it is necessary to convert it to the Interval 
Format, in order for it to be readable by Cooja. Figure 25 depicts this command. 
After conversion, a trace file with extension .if is created. Figure 26 presents an 
extract of the beginning of this file. For the predictable mobility model simulations, as 
Bonnmotion does not provide a suitable model for this case we decided to write our 
own mobility trace file, necessarily complying with the Interval Format.  
 
 
Figure 26: Resulting interval format file, cr eated according to the random mobility model. 
In this case our mobile node was programmed to move in a ping-pong fashion 
between the (x1,y1) point, located in network 1, and the (x2,y2) point, located in 
network 2. 
 


 95 
 
Figure 27: Predictable mobility scenario. 
Figure 27 depicts four stages of this predictable movement scenario (from left to right 
and from top to bottom), in which a mobile node is moving from position 
(180.0,132.0), within range of sink node 2, to position (145.9,160.5), within range of 
sink node 3. During its path, the mobile node crosses an area in which both networks 
are reachable, representing the handoff area. This predictable mobility scenario can 
represent several real-life cases, such as lifts, trams, trains or buses, in which the 
various positions can easily be anticipated. 
Controlled mobility is the type of mobility that, in real scenarios, can correspond to 
robotic systems in which there is a manual or automatic movement controller. For 
simulating this type of mobility no trace file was needed. Instead, we manually 
positioned the mobile node in the handoff zone, like we did in the real evaluation. The 
scenario is depicted in Figure 28. 
Using the three types of mobility models described above, several simulations were 
performed. In addition to the use of different mobility models, the number of sink 
nodes and the number of sensor nodes per networks varied.  
Varying the number of sink nodes has direct impact on coverage, increasing the 
density and therefore increasing the number of possible attachment points during the 
handoff. Varying the number of nodes in the network impacts the network traffic, and 
therefore influences the handoff process. 


 96 
 
 
Figure 28: Handoff stage of a controlled mobility scenario. 
 
It should be noted that the number of simulated sink nodes and sensor nodes in the 
network was limited by the processing capacity of the machine used for the 
simulations, this being the reason for not extending the tests to wider and denser 
networks. 
 
44.2.3. Simulation Results 
 
 
Figure 29: Mean handoff time for each simulated scenario. 
Figure 29 presents the overall results concerning the time required to handoff in 
each case. Starting by analyzing the controlled strategy section, we can observe that in 


 97 
the first situation – equivalent to the one used in the implementation-based evaluation, 
in which there were just two sink nodes (one in each domain) and no additional nodes 
– we obtained time values greater than 1.5 seconds, which were higher than the values 
obtained in the real platform using the same configuration. This difference was 
mainly caused by factors not present in the simple prototype scenario, such as sink 
nodes deployment, distances and noise. The difference is, in addition, a reminder that, 
even when using the same code – as we did – in the simulated and in the real 
environment, WSN simulations cannot yet replace implementation due to the 
influence of the great variety of factors (including device drivers and hardware types) 
that are present in wireless sensor networks. This is in line with [ Abdolrazaghi 2009], 
in which the author concluded that simulation results can vary extraordinarily based 
not only on the simulator but also on the used parameters.  
One important observation is that, regardless the situation, the MIPv6 hard handoff 
solution always took more time than the soft handoff solutions, as expected. Besides, 
we can also conclude that the MIPv6 traditional solutions were significantly affected 
by increasing the number of nodes, the number of sinks and also by the mobility 
strategy.  
Another interesting result was the non-linear behavior when only 2 sink nodes were 
used. With the exception of the controlled strategy, in which the values increase 
proportionally to the number of nodes, in the random and predictable strategies the 
obtained values did not change linearly with the number of nodes present in the 
network. The constant change of positioning affected the collision pattern and, 
consequently, retransmissions, which in turn affected the time to handoff 
independently of the number of neighbors present in the network. Hence, in general 
we can conclude that for the case of random and predictable mobility strategies, in 
which the MN was continuously moving, an increase in the number of sink nodes per 
domain led to a more predictable handoff time. 
 
Figure 30 and Figure 31 present the energy expenditure results for each of the two 
types of handoff. The simulations confirmed that MIPv6 Soft Handoff requires less 
time in general and also that the performance of both solutions is correlated with the 
number of nodes, as previously mentioned. 
 

 98 
 
Figure 30: Simulation results for the energy required by MIPv6 node-based soft handoff. 
 
Looking at the base case - which is the soft handoff, controlled strategy, 2 sink nodes 
and no neighbors - we can see that the MN spent an average of ~25mJ to handoff, 
while in the implementation-based evaluation it only reached ~8mJ. On a closer look, 
we can see that the MN spent roughly the same time in each task (receiving, 
transmitting, sleeping and processing). However, in the simulation more 
retransmissions occurred, namely of Router Solicitation and Binding Update 
messages.  
Looking at the remaining results, we can observe the same type of behavior explained 
before in the context of the time analysis. In general, hard handoff required more 
energy, independently of the scenario. We can also conclude that the increase in the 
number of nodes directly affected not only the time (as previously observed) but also 
energy. Collisions and retransmissions are the main justifications for this. 
 


 99 
 
Figure 31: Simulation results for the energy required by MIPv6 node-based hard handoff. 
 
44.3. Lightweight MIPv6 – A 6lowPAN-based compressed MIPv6 
 
Although possible to support mobility in WSNs, MIPv6, in its original form, requires 
a significant amount of time and energy to handoff, when considering the 
requirements of critical and real time applications. Following the concept of 
6lowPAN, compressing, coding and fragmenting not only IPv6 headers but also entire 
packets, we assumed the possibility to apply the same in all MIPv6 messages, 
originating a lowMIPv6 version, constituted by shorter messages. This work was 
presented in a draft form submitted to IETF 6lowPAN WG [Silva and Silva 2009], 
which can be viewed in Annex B  of this thesis. In section 4.3.1 we detail the 
lowMIPv6, while in section 4.3.2 we evaluate and compare it with the original 
MIPv6. 
4.3.1. μMIPv6 – the draft 
 
MIPv6 is an extension of IPv6, constituted by a specific header, eight main messages 
and several mobility options. Each component has a specific format, considered too 
large for use in lowPANs. The original and the compressed header that we proposed 
are presented, respectively, in Figure 32 and Figure 33 [Silva and Silva 2009]. 


 100 
 
Figure 32: Original MIPv6 message header. 
 
Figure 33: Compressed MIPv6 message header for lowPANs. 
 
We propose the compression of the 6 bytes MIPv6 header into just 1 byte. Payload 
Proto identifies the next header. Being equal to the original IPv6 header, we propose 
to compress this field in the same way that RFC 4944 [Montenegro et al. 2007] 
compressed it for 6lowPAN, reducing it to only 2 bits. Bit L represents the header 
length and can assume two values: 0 means that the length is obtained via the MAC 
Layer, and 1 means that it is carried in line. Since currently there are only 8 known 
types of mobility messages, we propose to reduce the MH Type field from 8 bits to 4 
bits, allowing the maximum of 16 types. The last bit, C, signals if the checksum is 
carried in line or is included in the packet checksum. 
The submitted draft [Silva and Silva 2009] proposes a set of compression rules for all 
MIPv6 messages and mobility options. For instance, in order to analyze the real 
impact, let us consider the original Binding Update (BU) Message.  
Without considering mobility options, the original BU is composed of 6 bytes, plus 6 
bytes of the Mobility Header, amounting to a total of 12 bytes. Our proposal reduced 


 101 
the BU from 6 bytes to 2 bytes, which means that with the 1-byte Compressed 
Mobility Header we get a BU with just 3 bytes.  
Our proposal also includes advanced features, as the return routeability mechanism. 
To support that, the Mobile Node (MN) notifies both the Home Agent (HA) and the 
Correspondent Node (CN) about its new Care-of Address (CoA) during the handoff. 
This mechanism is composed of several mobility messages used to guarantee the 
integrity of the Mobility Binding Update.  
Performed at the same time as the handoff process, the return routeability procedure 
starts with the Mobile Node sending a Home-Test Init (HoTI) to the CN via HA and a 
Care-of Test Init (CoTI) directly to the CN.  When the CN receives both messages, 
HoTI and CoTI, it generates two keygen tokens based on a pre-generated key (random 
number of 20 octets) and a pre-generated nonce (random octet string with any length), 
namely: home keygentoken and care-of keygentoken, respectively.  Then, via the 
same paths, the CN sends back the generated keygen tokens in Home Test (HoT) and 
Care-of Test (CoT) messages, respectively.  When the MN receives both HoT and 
CoT, it computes a binding message key (bmK) to sign the Binding Update (BU) 
message which it then sends directly to the CN.  Finally, the CN checks the signature, 
updates the Mobility Binding Table and sends back a Binding Acknowledgement 
(BA). 
Our proposal, at this stage, does not intend to alter this procedure. However, as 
mentioned before, we proposed in [Silva and Silva 2009] the compression of all 
mobility messages: HoTI, CoTI, HoT, CoT, BU and BA. Figure 34 illustrates the 
entire handoff process proposal. 
 
 

 102 
 
Figure 34: HandOff process considering Return Routeability for lowPANs. 
 
44.3.2. Evaluation of μMIPv6 
 
To evaluate the µMIPv6 proposal we assumed only the basic features in order to 
guarantee the same level of complexity used in the previous MIPv6 evaluation, 
allowing a direct comparison between both solutions. 
An analytical analysis, considering the values of standard motes, showed us a slightly 
superiority of µMIPv6. To measure it, we considered 22,6mA to transmit (value 
obtained from the telosB datasheet [XBow 2011]) and 2.6V in the circuitry. Table 14 
shows the time and energy required to send a BU message in its original and 
compressed version. 
 
Table 14: Average time to send a regular BU and a compressed BU message

The difference of 9 bytes in the message length is visible at a millisecond scale. 
Although both ranges are slightly overlapping, the obtained average stresses out the 
difference. Looking at the energy consumption, in just one message we saved about 
0.23mJ as a result of the 9 bytes suppression. Analytically, considering that in the 
entire Return Routeability procedure we proposed to save 62 bytes [Silva and Silva 
2009], we can conclude that in this process we are able to save, under the same 
conditions, 1.58mJ.  
To better assess this solution we implemented it in ContikiOS and run several 
experiments in parallel comparing µMIPv6 with MIPv6. In addition to time and 
energy consumption, in this evaluation we have also considered the number of 
successful request to handoff. We will start by presenting the cost and efficiency of 
the initial request (Router Solicitation) and then the cost of the entire process, 
considering soft and hard handoff. Note that the following experiments were carried 
on ContikiOS-2.5 using Z1 motes from Zolertia [Zolertia 2012], in contrast to the 
previous MIPv6 experiments, in which we used ContikiOS-2.4 and TelosB motes 
from CrossBow. 
 
To evaluate the successful requests (Router Solicitations) when MN is running MIPv6 
or µMIPv6, we used several thresholds from 2 to 10 sec as the time limit of each 
request. For example, a 5 sec threshold means that each request should be performed 
within 5 seconds to be considered successful. After that, it is considered lost and a 
new request should be sent. The result shown in Figure 35 is the average number of 
successful requests and their standard deviation.

Figure 36: Detailed time spent on each request in HN 
As can be observed, each mobility request needs less than 2 seconds to be completed, 
but for most of this time (more than 1s) the mote is idle. When comparing 
transmission and reception times, we notice a significant difference between them, 
due to the fact that before transmitting a packet, the radio sends beacons to the 
destination to know when both motes are awake. If this is the case, the 
communication is performed. 
The difference between MIPv6’s and µMIPv6’s request times corresponds to only 
18ms, which is not significant when considering the requirements of critical and real 
time applications. 
Looking at Figure 37 we can observe the respective energy consumption. 
 
 
Figure 37: Detailed energy spent on each request in HN 
In terms of consumption LPM is irrelevant, because in idle mode the mote almost 
does not consume energy. CPU consumes about 50% of the energy, but it is very 
difficult to reduce this value because the extra code does not have algorithms, only the 
necessary instructions to process messages. Once again, the difference between 
µMIPv6 and MIPv6 is not significant.

44.4. Considerations 
 
As wireless sensor networks broaden their field of application, it becomes apparent 
that the need for effective solutions for sensor node mobility is increasing.  
In order to investigate the consequences and possible issues of using MIPv6 for 
mobility support in WSNs, we performed a set of tests with the objective of 
determining the handoff time and the energy expenditure incurred by MIPv6 node-
based mobility. These tests were performed both by prototyping and by simulation, 
having as guidelines 
 the requirements of a critical, real-life scenario, deployed in an oil refinery in the 
context of the GINSENG European project. 
Although the test results have shown that deploying and using MIPv6 in sensor nodes 
is feasible, they also showed that this leads to non-optimal handoff time and to 
significant energy expenditure, even when using a simplified, lightweight MIPv6 
implementation. Increasing the number of nodes and sink nodes in the network, 
demonstrated that the time and energy required by MIPv6 could vary considerably, 
which make this type of solution unlikely for the support of reliable mobility in 
critical scenarios. This is mainly due to the complexity of the MIPv6 protocol, which 
is not adapted to the scarce resources generally found in WSN motes. To overcome 
this issue a new type of solution must be adopted. In the next chapter we present a 
network-based alternative to assist mobility support in WSNs. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

 108 
 
 
 
  

 109 
 
Chapter 5  
 
Network of Proxies 
 
 
Targeting an increasing number of potential application domains, Wireless Sensor 
Networks (WSN) have been the subject of intense research, in an attempt to optimize 
their performance while guaranteeing reliability in highly demanding scenarios. 
However, hardware restrictions have limited their application, and real deployments 
have demonstrated that WSNs are not able to cope with complex communication 
tasks (e.g., routing and mobility) in addition to the application-related tasks. In this 
line, we propose and present the Network of Proxies solution, which relieves 
resource-constrained WSN nodes from heavy procedures such as mobility 
management. We implemented and evaluated our solution in a real platform, 
demonstrating not only its superiority over conventional solutions in terms of mobility 
support, but also its very good performance in the simultaneously handling of several 
mobile nodes, leading to high handoff success rate and maintaining low handoff time. 
 
Lately, WSN research has intensely addressed performance, reliability and capacity 
optimization, in an attempt to shorten the gap that separates them from conventional 
networks. However, resource-constrained devices, whose characteristics are still far 
from those required by most applications, largely constitute WSNs. Advanced routing 
algorithms, neighbor and service discovery mechanisms, security, mobility and 
debugging, among others, are just examples of features that researchers are trying to 

 110 
implement in WSNs. Even though it is possible to install and evaluate them 
individually, the integration of all these features with the aim of developing a reliable, 
complete system will, on one hand, limit the algorithms’ complexity due to ROM and 
RAM restrictions and, on the other hand, contribute to a decrease in the lifetime of 
each mote due to added energy requirements. 
While working on the GINSENG project [Sreenan et al. 2011], a European Project 
whose main objective is the deployment of performance controlled WSNs in critical 
scenarios, we faced this problem when we tried to include all features we considered 
fundamental in a real, deployed WSN, whose target was Petrogal’s Oil Refinery in 
Sines, Portugal. The solution we found was to remove some features and simplify the 
software installed in each mote, in order to still achieve the necessary performance 
without compromising the network lifetime. 
In [Schmid et al. 2010] the authors also arrive to a similar conclusion, namely that 
motes must be relieved from the routing process and must become as simple as 
possible, acting just as end nodes and delegating routing procedures on more powerful 
entities. Basically, the authors defend the separation of the sensing activity from the 
network operation activity. A similar line was taken in [Banerjee et al. 2008], in 
which the authors study the enhancement of mobile networks by adding infrastructure 
support, concluding that, in general, this kind of support is highly beneficial when 
mobility is concerned.  
 
As presented in Chapter 2, some approaches appeared in the literature trying to deal 
with mobility by using conventional solutions running directly on motes. Researchers 
have proposed MAC Layer solutions to support mobility in WSNs, but none of those 
proposals materialized in a real experiment to demonstrate its capacities and limits. 
Some other solutions proposed the use of mobility protocols designed for 
conventional networks, such as MIPv6, in WSNs. In the last Chapter we implemented 
and evaluated the use of a light version of MIPv6 in WSNs, concluding that even 
though it is feasible, MIPv6 can rapidly deplete the motes’ resources, mainly when 
the number of nodes in the network increases. On the other hand, in many cases, 
complete solutions are using ROM space to the limit and it is almost impossible to 
add additional features, such as the support of complex mobility protocols.  
 

 111 
Given the above, in this Chapter we propose, present and evaluate a WSN support 
infrastructure, called Network of Proxies (NoP), designed to perform complex, time-
consuming, processor-intensive and energy-demanding operations, such as mobility 
management operations, on behalf of WSN nodes. The NoP objective is to simplify 
the sensor network, moving the complexity from the motes to local proxies. Proxies 
are machines without the stringent energy restrictions of sensor nodes, and with 
capacity to operate alone or to be part of a mesh network. They should be capable of 
monitoring each mote’s link quality and determining when handoff should be done, 
taking care of it on behalf of mobile nodes. This way, it is possible to maintain mobile 
nodes as simple as possible, focusing their activity on sensing, and saving energy. 
Although we are mainly dealing with reliable mobility support in this thesis, the NoP 
concept can be used to support any other activity whose complexity level requires 
more powerful mechanisms. 
 
This chapter is organized as follows. The next section presents the concept of 
Network of Proxies and its application to mobility support. Section 5.2 presents 
implementation details, starting with the proxy overall architecture, and continuing 
with the description of the implementation of each of its modules. This is followed by 
the presentation and discussion of the NoP’s evaluation results, in Section 5.3. Section 
5.4 presents the application to the case study – GINSENG and Section 5.5 the 
extension of this concept to a wider perspective, using the NoP to also support routing 
and QoS among others. Section 5.6 concludes the section with some considerations. 
 
55.1. Network of Proxies 
 
Typically, WSNs are made of several resource-constrained nodes and one sink node. 
The sink node is not subject to the same energy, memory or processing power 
restrictions of sensor nodes and, thus, it is used as activity coordinator and central 
communications hub, providing connectivity to the outside world.  
 
5.1.1. Overview 
 
The Network of Proxies (NoP) is a wireless mesh network overlay constituted by 

 112 
resource-unconstrained nodes (i.e., nodes not having the stringent limitations of 
normal sensor nodes), capable of handling energy/processing/time-consuming 
operations on behalf of sensor nodes. NoP was proposed in the scope of the 
GINSENG project and was designed to assist constrained networks in critical 
scenarios, such as healthcare systems, oil refineries or power plants, in which 
reliability and performance are essential.  
 
In its early phases, the concept relied on the existence of a shared backbone [Silva et 
al. 2010]. Then, the idea evolved and in [Silva et al. 2012], along with an evaluation 
of MIPv6 in WSNs, we presented the first version of the Network of Proxies and 
assessed its positive impact on the WSN side. Later, the concept was optimized, and 
an implementation was developed, leading to the results and evaluation presented in 
this chapter.  
 
In Figure 41 we can see a scenario in which an NoP is superimposed on a dense 
WSN. The figure separately depicts the WSN and the NoP in the upper part, and the 
two networks after superimposition, in the bottom part.  
Several configurations are possible. A single NoP can be installed to cover the entire 
WSN (standalone mode), several WSNs (extended mode) or just a specific section of 
one or more WSNs (standalone or extended mode), depending on the requirements.  
As previously mentioned, NoPs are transparent overlays, which means that these 
networks do not take part in or interact with the WSNs at the application level, and 
that the WSNs operation is unaffected by them. 
Nodes supervised by NoPs are named Assisted Motes (AMs), while WSNs supervised 
by NoPs are named Assisted WSNs or just AWSNs. NoPs can be installed in both, 
new and/or existing WSNs.  

 113 
 
 
Figure 41: WSN partial assisted by a Network of Proxies. 
 
Typically, a NoP is deployed following the Manhattan style, assuring that adjacent 
covered areas overlap. This leads to different AWSN areas being covered by different 
proxies and also in a different way. For instance, considering Figure 41 we can 
observe that there are motes covered by one proxy only, which means that they are 
located at the edge of the NoP, while there are other motes covered by four proxies, 


 114 
which means that they are located somewhere in the middle of the NoP.  This may be 
explored by the NoP model in order to determine the areas in which the motes are, 
which can, in turn, be used for mobility support as explained in the next section. 
It should be emphasized that the NoP is an overlay structure that does not interfere 
with the WSN itself. As already mentioned, it can be used for assisting motes while 
they move through a WSN, but this does not mean that the WSN topology and/or 
multi-hop structure is affected. This is illustrated in Figure 42, where we can see a 
proxy assisting a mobile node in choosing its parent node while it roams through the 
WSN. The following sub-section further discusses this example, as it is dedicated to 
explaining the use of NoP for mobility support. 
 
 
Figure 42: A proxy assisting a mobile node in choosing its parent node. This scenario was 
taken from GINSENG project. 
 
This complementary wireless mesh network can use standard IEEE802.11 [IEEE 
2007], thus facilitating its constitution and operation. IEEE 802.11 is a well-known 
technology, ease to use, already supported for most mobile devices. By using a 
different technology in the right RF channels we avoid, on one hand, interferences 
with the WSN side, which is running IEEE802.15.4 by default and, on the other hand, 
we can support higher transmission rates. NoP is in line with and an evolution of the 
concepts presented in  [Banerjee et al. 2008], [Bouckaert et al. 2010]  and   [Schmid 
et al. 2010], adapted to WSNs. 
 


 115 
Before achieving the NoP version presented above, other similar approaches were 
developed. NoP is thus the result of a continuous work that has evolved. The first NoP 
version do not considered the existence of a mesh network between proxies. Instead, 
our first network-based version was published using a shared backbone between 
proxies, as presented in Figure 43. 
 
 
Figure 43: First NoP version. 
In our initial solution proxies were named Sensor Mobility Proxy (SMP) and designed 
exclusively to assist handoffs through the shared backbone. In this first version, while 
the MN was disconnected, data will pass via the backbone assuring no packet losses.  
 
Figure 44: First NoP version with MN1 moving to a new network. 


 116 
 
Figure 45: First NoP version with the MN1 after complete the handoff. 
Observing the sequence of Figure 43, Figure 44 and Figure 45 it is possible to see the 
link between the Control Center and the MN1 updating, using the shared backbone, 
during the movement. Like in the last version, the shared backbone, replaced now by 
the mesh network, was used not only to assure connectivity but also to share 
information about Mobile Nodes in the area. However, this initial solution forced the 
existence of a shared backbone that is not possible in many scenarios, limiting the 
applicability of the concept, which was not our objective. Therefore, this first concept 
evolved to the NoP Mesh Network based, which is much more flexible, operating in a 
plug and play fashion. 
55.1.2. NoP for mobility support 
 
This subsection presents one possible mobility estimation approach, based on RSSI 
signatures. Other approaches were implemented as well, such as the threshold-based 
approaches described in Chapter 3. 
When used for mobility support, each NoP proxy is responsible for monitoring the 
WSN area within its range and take note of the various motes’ received signal strength 
indicators (RSSI). Depending on their location, motes are heard with different signal 
strengths by each proxy. By sharing the gathered information on each mote’s RSSI 
among the proxies, the NoP is able to built what we call a “signature” for each mote. By 
analyzing the signature of mobile nodes, it is then possible to dynamically decide which 


 117 
is the best parent node for each mobile node, manage the handoff processes, and instruct 
mobile nodes to change their configuration accordingly. 
Table 16 depicts an example of a table containing the signatures of various motes in an 
AWSN. 
Table 16: Example of data stored and shared by each proxy in an NoP. 
Mote Parent HA proxy 
ID 
HA RSSI 
(dBm) 
P8 RSSI 
(dBm) 
P4 RSSI 
(dBm) 
P5 RSSI 
(dBm) 
1 7  -50    
2 3 3 -55  -70  
3 4 3 -60 -60   
4 10 5 -65 -70 -68 -70 
5 4 1 -70 -65   
1 7  -52    
7 14 5 -46 -70 -80 -65 
…. … …. … … …. … 
 
As we can observe in Table 16, for each node in range, each proxy maintains 
information on the parent ID (the parent node of the mote in the WSN), the Home 
Agent proxy ID (the proxy responsible for the mote), the RSSI between the HA proxy 
and the mote (HA RSSI), and, in the case the mote is under more than one proxy, it also 
keeps the RSSI between each of the other proxies (Foreign Agent proxy, FA proxy) and 
the mote. This information is shared in an ad hoc fashion among proxies using the NoP 
overlay. While RSSI and parent information are obtained by sniffing the motes’ 
communications, the other items are obtained by sharing information among the NoP 
proxies. 
As an example, considering node 3, we know from the table that in the last 
communication from this mote was heard by the HA proxy with an RSSI of -60dBm, 
and also with an RSSI of -60dBm by FA proxy P8, which allows to draw the conclusion 
that this mote is somewhere between the HA proxy and FA proxy P8. Considering 
motes 4 and 7, we can observe that both are covered by the same proxies and also that 
both have similar RSSI values, which allows to conclude that both motes are in the 
same region.  

 118
Based on the signature information, proxies can easily detect when a node is moving, 
and manage the WSN connections along the path, either in intra-mobility or in inter-
mobility scenarios. Moreover, the proxies can choose the best parent node of each mote 
and execute all handoff procedures on behalf of the motes. The process is totally 
transparent to the motes, which, on completion of the handoff, simply receive a 
command to update their configuration (parent, address, channel, etc.), interpreting 
these actions as simple topology updates performed by request of topology control 
modules. 
In sparse WSNs, signature comparison is not viable or effective and, thus, RSSI 
thresholds are used to decide when to handoff, as defined in Chapter 3.  
 
An RSSI threshold is also used to define the optimal range of each proxy.  Motes out of 
this optimal range will not be assisted. Figure 46 exemplifies how this method is 
applied.  
 
Figure 46: Example of proxies' true range compared with the optimal range used by NoP 
solutions. 
As a final remark, although we are aware that RSSI might be unstable and that radio 
ranges are not symmetric, our experience shows that the described RSSI monitoring and 
decision method is effective, and that in the vast majority of cases the assisted motes are 
connected to the best parent. RSSI is not only considered a reliable metric in WSNs 
[Srinivasan and Levis 2006], but also a recent study concluded that we can use LQI to 
assess RSSI [Sharly et al. 2012], making this solution yet more robust. 


 119 
55.2. Implementation 
 
The NoP concept leads to several requirements that must be met by the proxies, which 
must be capable of: 
• establish IEEE 802.11 ad-hoc connections with other proxies; 
• detect and communicate with at least one IEEE 802.15.4 network; 
• store, manage and share data regarding all the proxy neighbors and all the sensor 
nodes in range; 
• process data, and to communicate between both sides (WSNs and NoP) in real 
time; 
• dynamically perform handoffs based on real time information. 
 
To address these requirements, several software modules were developed and 
interconnected. The next section provides an overview of the proxy architecture. 
Sections 5.2.1 to 5.2.5 detail each of its modules. 
5.2.1. Proxy Architecture 
 
An NoP is set up when two or more proxies establish a wireless mesh network among 
themselves, using their IEEE 802.11 interfaces for this purpose. Since proxies have all 
the necessary features, they can also act as sink nodes, which means that an NoP may 
contain both Proxies and Proxy-Sinks. Proxies can also act as Edge-Proxies when 
deployed between different WSNs.  
To be part of an NoP, each proxy must be, on one hand, capable of communicating with 
other proxies via IEEE 802.11 and, on the other hand, capable of monitoring one or 
more WSNs (in the case of Edge-Proxies), via IEEE802.15.4. This characteristic poses 
several challenges to the design and implementation of these devices, forcing the use of, 
at least, two different radios and a mechanisms to interconnect them (note: this is also 
the approach taken in [Bouckaert et al. 2010], for instance).  
Nevertheless, more than being a simple sniffer, a proxy needs intelligence, as it must be 
able to decide what actions should be taken based on real-time information. For 
instance, in the case of mobility, proxies must be capable of not only deciding when a 
specific mobile node (MN) should handoff but also of performing all the handoff 
procedures on behalf of that MN.  

 120
Hence, to support this, the proxy’s architecture (Figure 47) includes not only the 
mentioned radio interfaces but also a central unit ( middleware), connected to a local 
data backend, and accessible through a configuration frontend.  
 
 
Figure 47: Proxy software architecture. 
Proxies constantly monitor the networks to which they are attached – the NoP mesh 
network(s) and the WSN network. After real-time processing of all packets by the 
middleware module, the data obtained from these networks is recorded in the local 
database, as presented in Table 16. The middleware module uses the proxy’s 
communication interfaces to trigger or perform the necessary actions. The frontend 
module is the interface for configuration and management of the proxy software. This 
module is outside the scope of the current paper and will not be further described. The 
NoP architecture requires approximately 180MB of ROM and 60MB of RAM, when 
running in full operation. 
55.2.2. IEEE 802.11 ADHOC 
 
After booting, the first module to start is the IEEE802.11 ADHOC module. This module 
is responsible for announcing the device’s presence in the mesh network, for listening to 
the announcements of other devices, and for establishing an ad-hoc connection with 
each found neighbor. Each neighbor is recorded in the local database, assigning a Time-
To-Live (TTL) to each entry. This TTL is refreshed with each received announcement, 
and will lead to the deletion of the entry if it reaches zero.  


 121 
Then, the discovery process is started, in which each proxy advertises itself using 
ICMPv6 Router Advertising (RA) messages, sent to the broadcast router IPv6 address, 
and listens to other possible advertisements in the network. This process does not 
require any protocol, working instead in an ad-hoc fashion. Consequently, each proxy 
operates in NoP mode when at least one neighbor is active in the database.  
Hence, while one thread is periodically advertising the proxy (Figure 48), there is a 
second thread responsible for listening to and processing the received RA (Figure 49), 
inserting new neighbors or renewing the TTL of the existing ones. A third thread is used 
for updating the database (Figure 50), periodically decreasing the TTL and removing 
the neighbors whose TTL expired from the database. In addition to the address and 
TTL, the hop count is also recorded in the database, in order to be later used by the 
middleware module. 
 
 
Figure 48: Sending periodic advertisements.  
 
Figure 49: Listening to and processing new packets.  


 122
 
Figure 50: Updating the neighbors in the database. 
 
55.2.3. IEEE 802.15.4 SNIFFER 
 
Once the Network of Proxies (NoP) is established, the proxy needs to interact with the 
local WSN. To support this, at least one IEEE802.15.4 interface should be available and 
configured. However, since each interface usually has only one radio transceiver, for 
devices located between two or more distinct WSNs operating in different channels, two 
or more IEEE802.15.4 interfaces should be configured (Edge-Proxies). Obviously, these 
radio channels should be carefully chosen in order to avoid interference with the 
IEEE802.11 network [Yang et al. 2011]. 
The main objective of including an IEEE802.15.4 interface in the proxy is to enable it to 
work as a sniffer, allowing the detection of specific situations in real time and acting on 
behalf of motes without interfering with them. To do this, we adapted one of the tools 
provided by ContikiOS [Dunkels et al. 2004], namely tapslip6, to operate in 
promiscuous mode. Tapslip6 is a tool that establishes a bridge between the 
IEEE802.15.4 radio and a serial port, configuring the latter as a UNIX network interface 
(see Figure 51). 
 


 123 
 
Figure 51: tapslip6 interface created and configured.  
After configuration, it is then possible to receive packets from the WSN through a 
common UNIX network interface. However, with this out of the box  solution the 
interface does not operate in promiscuous mode and, therefore, it only receives data sent 
to its addresses or to the broadcast address. In our case we used as interface the TelosB 
mote [XBow 2011] (CC2420-based) running the uip-bridge6 application, with the 
sicslowMAC protocol, also included in ContikiOS. To make it running in promiscuous 
mode we changed the CC2420_MDMCTRL0 flag in the CC2420 ContikiOS 
implementation from 0x11 to 0x0. Additionally, we made some changes in the 
sicslowMAC implementation to avoid packet discarding when the received packet was 
not destined to the network or to the mote. 
In addition to configuring the interface in promiscuous mode, it was necessary to 
develop a module to receive and process all packets received through this interface. 
Therefore, we developed a module in C language called Sniffer.  
Since our immediate goal was to support mobility, Sniffer was made responsible for 
receiving all packets at the tap0 interface and storing, for each packet the mote’s address 
(the motes were running 6lowPAN [Bormann and Mulligan 2011]), the respective 
received signal strength indicator (RSSI) and the mote’s parent (the packet’s L2 
address) in the proxies’ local database, as shown in Table 16. 6lowPAN allows a direct 
communication to each mote and the use of well-known protocols over WSNs. By 
monitoring the RSSI of the various nodes, we can efficiently detect when a particular 
node is moving, supporting the handoff whenever required, as previously presented 
either in Chapter 3 and Section 5.1 of this chapter.  
However, to get the RSSI via tapslip6 some changes had to be done. Although RSSI 
comes by default in the IEEE802.15.4 packet, it belongs to the metadata and it is not 
passed through the tapslip6 bridge. To overcome this problem we changed the uip6-
bridge original code in order to get the RSSI from the metadata, including it at the end 


 124
of the packet payload. With this mechanism, we are able to maintain a record of RSSI 
values over time, for each node. 
The information obtained from this module is then processed in the middleware module 
to detect, in this case, the movement of the existing motes and the need to handoff.  
Other types of information can be recorded depending on the requirements, without 
forgetting that proxies are not intended to interfere with the application level.  
In addition to the sniffing activity, this module is also responsible for any 
communication with the WSN, as in the case where the proxy is also a sink node, and 
for notifying mobile nodes of their new configuration following a handoff operation. 
55.2.4. Backend – Database 
 
The proxy’s backend module is constituted by a PostgreSQL local database, designed to 
support Table 16 and its respective process. This database is shared among the 
IEEE802.11 ADHOC module, the IEEE802.15.4 SNIFFER module and the Middleware 
module. Figure 52 presents the database structure. 
 
 
Figure 52: Proxy postgresql database structure. 
As we can see in the figure, the database structure is simple and, in this implementation, 
oriented to the mobility case.  
The proxy table, mentioned in Figure 52, is completely managed by the IEEE802.11 
ADHOC module and used by the Middleware module. In this table we can find the 
entry id, the neighbor IPv6 address, the hop count and the TTL.  
The remaining tables refer to the WSN and are maintained by the IEEE802.15.4 
SNIFFER module. 


 125 
The mote table keeps the basic information of each detected sensor node, such as entry 
id, the IPv6 address, the parent’s id and an additional flag to identify whether or not that 
mote is under this proxy’s responsibility (HA Proxy). This flag is important to 
guarantee the organization among proxies. Each mote can be monitored by more than 
one proxy, but only one proxy can be its master. This flag should be set during the node 
and service discovery mechanism. 
The first time that the IEEE802.15.4 module receives a packet from a specific mote, it 
inserts that mote in the table and the first RSSI entry in the RSSI table. Then, when 
receiving the next packets from the same mote, a 1-N relationship is created between the 
mote table and the RSSI table, thus maintaining a log of the signal strength for each 
mote. In addition, when another proxy shares information regarding an existent mote 
entry, this proxy also stores that information, constituting the signature of that mote, as 
explained in section 5.1.1 of this chapter.  The RSSI table contains the entry id, the 
RSSI value, the time that the packet was received and two foreign keys: one to the 
respective mote table and the other to the respective proxy table.  
Finally, the raw table keeps each received packet for each mote.   
55.2.5. Middleware 
 
The middleware module of the proxy device was developed in Java, and it constitutes 
the intelligent part of the system. In the current implementation, the main goal of the 
middleware module is to monitor in real time the RSSI evolution of each mote included 
in the database and marked as home node (marked as “ mine” in database), in order to 
perform the mobility protocol on behalf of the sensor nodes . It is the responsibility of 
the middleware module to timely detect that a specific mote is changing signal strength, 
and to perform the necessary handoff procedure using the IEEE802.11 ADHOC mesh 
network, as explained in section 5.1.1. Figure 53 represents the structure of the 
middleware module. 
 

 126 
 
Figure 53: Structure of the middleware module. 
The Database Reader (DBReader) module is responsible for periodically checking the 
local database and starting the monitoring system for the existing motes. For motes not 
under its responsibility (not flagged as mine) DBReader only starts the Foreign Agent 
Receiver (FAReceiver) in order to listen to requests that might occur. For motes under 
its responsibility (flagged as mine) DBReader starts the Home Agent Receiver 
(HAReceiver), responsible for the communication regarding activities related with those 
motes. In addition to the HAReceiver thread executed for each existing mote, the 
DBReader starts a new Processor thread for each mote during each iteration. The 
Processor, in turn, is responsible for verifying whether or not the respective mote link is 
below the admissible threshold and whether or not it needs to handoff. This verification 
is done every 3-seconds interval, considering the maximum time of the worst case result 
presented in the next section and giving it a safety margin. Such detection is achieved 
by the comparison of the motes’ signatures, in which similar signatures will mean that 
the respective motes can connect to the same parent or that the MN can connect to the 
mote with the closest signature. To avoid wrong movement detections, in our 
experiments mobility estimation is done based on the average of the last ten RSSI 
measurements of a specific node. This value can be adapted depending on the 
requirements of the scenario.  
When the Processor detects that the average RSSI is changing significantly (varying +/-
10dBm it initiates the handoff process by calling the Gossiper. The Gossiper, in turn, is 
responsible for executing the handoff procedure, which follows the protocol presented 
in Figure 54.  
 

 	

 


 127 
 
Figure 54: Handoff protocol  performed via proxies. 
The protocol presented in Figure 54 uses UDP. In addition, to improve the performance 
we used two dedicated UDP ports (one for receiving and another for sending) per mote 
listed in the database.  
The protocol is started by the home agent proxy (HA proxy) sending a GET multicast 
request message to all proxy neighbors. The scope of this request can be limited by 
specifying the maximum number of hops it can travel.  
When a foreign agent proxy (FA proxy) receives a GET message through the 
FAReceiver module, it checks if the specified mote exists in its database. If the mote 
exists, the proxy will then compute the average of the previous ten RSSI values for that 
particular mote and compare it with the RSSI value in the received GET message. If the 
computed result is higher, then it will reply with a MOTE #ADDR #RSSI message, 
which contains the calculated RSSI average value. This method is fundamental to 
decide which proxy is in better position to deal with the handoff. 
The HAReceiver will receive the MOTE messages and store them in a temporary list. 
That list will be managed in real time by the ListManager module. After a short period 
equal to 25ms x N, where N is the number of neighbors and 25ms the time to receive 
8192bits (> than our average packet size) at 54Mbps (allowing ~10ms delay), the 


 128
ListManager will send a SET message to the Neighbor that reported the best value, 
which might be itself. That proxy will choose the next best parent to attach the MN 
based on the signatures’ comparison, responding then with an ACK message providing 
the care-of address (COA) that the mote should use from now on and the new parent id. 
When receiving the ACK, the home agent proxy (through HAReceiver) extracts the 
COA, assembles a Router Advertisement (RA) message and sends it to the mote via the 
IEEE802.15.4 interface. In turn, when the node receives the RA message, it loads the 
new configuration, thus completing the handoff procedure. In the case where the best 
proxy is located in a different WSN, the RA message also contains the channel to which 
the mote should move. When the best proxy to deal the handoff is the HA Proxy, SET 
#RSSI and ACK #COA messages are not used.  
After concluding the handoff, the HA proxy updates its local Sink-Proxy (the WSN 
Sink-Node), which in turn updates its internal tables or forwards the same information 
in case of inter-mobility. Topology control modules and routing protocols should be 
responsible for maintaining then the consistence of the entire network. 
Hence, the middleware module can operate in two distinct modes: as a HA proxy, when 
it starts the handoff procedure; or as a FA proxy, when it is acting in response to a HA 
proxy request. Figure 55 and Figure 56 present the state diagram for each proxy mode. 
As we can observe, in addition to the description given above, there exist several 
timeout mechanisms to avoid deadlock situations. 
 
Figure 55: HA Proxy state diagram. 


 129 
 
Figure 56: FA Proxy state diagram.  
 
 
55.3. Prototype-Based Evaluation 
 
The implementation described in the previous section was used to evaluate the Network 
of Proxies proposal, using various operating scenarios. Several experiments were done, 
not only to assess the intrinsic performance of the NoP concept, but also to compare this 
performance with non-NoP, node-based mobility solutions using MIPv6. Five scenarios 
were defined, differing in the number of proxies, number of MNs and movement 
pattern. These are presented in the next section, whereas sections 5.3.2 to 5.3.4 present 
and discuss the results. 
 
5.3.1. Scenarios 
 
The scenarios used for evaluation of the NoP concept are presented in Figure 57 to 
Figure 61. 
 
 
 
Figure 57: Scenario 1: 1 HAProxy, 1 FAProxy and N motes moving from one to the other.  


 130 
Scenario 1, presented in Figure 57, represents one or a group of mobile nodes (MNs) 
moving from one location under the control of their HA Proxy to another location near 
one FA Proxy. The objective of this scenario is to evaluate the basic configuration, 
when one HA Proxy deals with just one neighbor, performing the handoff of one or 
more MNs.  
 
 
 
 
Figure 58: Scenario 2: 1 HAProxy, 2 FAProxy and N/2  motes moving to one FAPRoxy while 
the other N/2  is moving to the other FAProxy. Each sub-group of MNs is only accessible from 
the closest FAProxy.  
Scenario 2 introduces two novel aspects when compared to scenario 1. In this scenario 
we have one additional FA Proxy and the MNs are split in two separate sub-groups. 
This means that an FA Proxy is not able to detect packets from MNs under the 
responsibility of the other FA Proxy and, thus, the HA Proxy will only receive one 
answer per MN, avoiding the need to choose the answer with the best RSSI. 
 
 
 
Figure 59: Scenario 3: 1 HAProxy, 2 FAProxy and N/2 motes moving to one FAPRoxy while 
the other N/2 are moving to the other FAPr oxy, although both sub-groups  are accessible from 
both FAProxies.   
Unlike scenario 2, in scenario 3 each FA Proxy can listen to all the MNs, although with 
different RSSIs. Consequently, when asking for each MN (GET message) the HA Proxy 
will receive two answers (MOTE messages) for each request, one from each FA Proxy. 


 131 
Therefore, it will have to choose the one with the best RSSI. In the case of using just 
one MN it will move toward one of the FA Proxies. 
 
 
 
Figure 60: Scenario 4: 1 HAProxy, 2 FAProxy and N motes moving to an area accessible from 
both FAProxies, with different RSSI values.  
Scenario 4 is similar to the scenario 3, with a simple difference: while in the scenario 3 
the MNs move in separate groups, leading to different RSSIs for each FA Proxy, in this 
scenario they move all to the same place, maintaining the same RSSI to each FA Proxy. 
While in scenario 3 the HA Proxy needs to randomly deal with one of the two FA 
Proxies, depending on the one reporting best answers, in this scenario it will analyze the 
two messages anyway, but reporting always to the same FA Proxy. 
 
 
 
Figure 61: Scenario 5: 1 HAProxy, 3 FAProxy and N motes moving to an area accessible from 
all FAProxies, but with different RSSI values.  
Finally, scenario 5 introduces one more FA Proxy maintaining the same setup of 
scenario 4. The MNs are moving together and all to the same area. The HA Proxy will 
have to choose between 3 answers per mote but all of them with different RSSI values, 
although equal for all MNs. 
 


 132 
These scenarios target the evaluation of the NoP load capacity and the time taken to 
perform the handoff since the middleware module determines that the MN needs to 
handoff until the MN receives the RA message carrying the new COA.  
In each scenario, we varied the number of MNs from 1 to 90, following intervals of 10, 
i.e., 1, 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80 and 90 MNs. All the MNs were automatically 
inserted in the database and all of them required to handoff at the same time.  
For each case we performed the handoff 100 times and all the results presented in the 
next sub-section are the average of those 100 runs. 
The proxies were laptops with different hardware capabilities, but all of them running 
Ubuntu 11.04. The used MNs were all TelosB (MSP430 and CC2420). 
In addition to the time taken by the NoP infrastructure to perform handoffs, we also 
present the complete handoff time, i.e., the total time including the MN’s processing of 
the RA message and loading of the new configuration.  
To conclude, we compare the NoP solution with the conventional solution (i.e., MIPv6-
enabled WSN nodes), using values determined in the previous Chapter. 
 
55.3.2. Handoff time and success rate 
 
For each experiment we measured the total time that the set of MNs took to handoff, the 
number of handoffs successfully completed, the number of unsuccessful handoffs, the 
success ratio and the average time per handoff. 
Figure 62 presents the total time taken by the proxies to complete the simultaneous 
handoff of all MNs, while Figure 63 presents the average time per MN to handoff in the 
different situations. 
 

 133 
 
Figure 62: Total time to handoff.  
Analyzing Figure 62 we can first conclude that, as expected, increasing the number of 
MNs simultaneously requiring handoff increases the total time needed to complete the 
task, independently of the scenario. However, looking in detail we can observe that 
scenario 1, the simplest, requires more time than the other scenarios to complete the 
handoffs when the number of MNs is higher than 40. As we mentioned before, more 
neighbors in the NoP means that the HA Proxy will increase the waiting time when 
requesting information. However, this increase, fundamental to guarantee the reception 
of all responses, is not meaningful when the number of MNs to handoff is high. 
Furthermore, through these results we can observe that increasing the number of proxies 
will help when the number of MNs to handoff is relatively high. We can also conclude 
that, in general, increasing the number of proxies (in the experiments, from two to three 
or four) leads to a decrease in the total time to handoff all MNs. 
Despite the number of proxies, the way the MNs moved also created some differences. 
We can observe the results of scenarios 4 and 5, where the MNs moved to the same 
area, and compare them with scenarios 2 and 3, where the MNs are split into two sub-
groups, and conclude that when the MNs move altogether the total time to handoff is 
more predictable than when they move in groups. The increase in scenario 5 is almost 
linear, and scenario 4 follows the same trend, with some small variations, as opposed to 
the remaining scenarios, for which the linear increase is not apparent.  
A similar trend can, naturally, be observed if we look at the average time to handoff per 
MN, as depicted in Figure 63. 
 


 134 
 
Figure 63: Average time per mote to handoff.  
Looking at this figure, it is important to note that not even in the worst and extreme case 
of the simultaneous handoff of 90 MNs did we have handoff times of 1 second or 
higher. Moreover, if we consider scenario 5, in which the number of proxies is higher, 
we can see that the average time per handoff when simultaneously performing the 
handoff of 90 MNs was below 600ms, which is a very good result when comparing the 
NoP solution with the conventional node-based approach, as we will see later in this 
chapter. 
 
Because the total time to handoff all MNs by all proxies and the average time per 
handoff and per MN are not enough to completely depict the success of the operation, 
we also measured the number of successful handoffs, losses and the respective ratio per 
experiment. When loading a system up to a point close to one hundred MNs, even when 
assigning individual sockets to the communication between each MN and the proxies, 
some losses can occur. Figure 64 presents the number of successful handoffs, which 
means the number of processes completed, for all MNs, since the proxy detects that an 
MN must handoff until that MN receives the RA message with the new configuration. 
On the other hand, Figure 65 presents the number of lost handoffs, and Figure 66 
combines the results in Figure 64 and Figure 65, showing the success ratio for each 
experiment. 
 


 135 
 
Figure 64: Total number of completed handoffs.  
Looking at Figure 64, we can observe that, in general, until 40 MNs, all handoff 
requests were successfully completed. Under this mark there is only a slight difference 
in the 30 MNs case for the scenario 3. From this point on, we can observe a small 
relative decrease in the number of completed handoffs, starting with the 50 MNs case 
until the 90 MNs case. Starting with the 80 MNs case, the relative decrease becomes 
distinctly noticeable. If, on one hand, we conclude from the handoff time analysis that 
increasing the number of Proxies (e.g., as in scenario 5) will increase the performance, 
on the other hand we can now observe that scenario 5 completed relatively less handoffs 
after the 70 MNs point, when compared to the other scenarios. However, under the 70 
MNs point this scenario outperformed all the others.  
 
 
Figure 65: Total number of lost handoffs.  


 136 
Analyzing Figure 65 we can confirm the conclusions presented above. The loss increase 
when increasing the number of simultaneous MNs handoffs is justified by the increase 
in the number of exchanged messages. This is corroborated by the peak in scenario 5, 
that can be justified by the increase in the number of responses (MOTE messages) 
caused by the increase in the number of proxies. Although more proxies can improve 
the handoff time, as demonstrated in Figure 63, this will also increase the probability of 
congestion and consequent losses. Looking at these results across all scenarios, we can 
see that the increase in unsuccessful handoffs is not linear, in general. Under the 60 
MNs point the average losses will not be higher than 4 handoffs and above the 60 MNs 
point we can observe a general upward trend, although the number of lost handoffs is 
always below 20. Nonetheless, we should have in mind that the simultaneous handoff of 
more than 60 MNs is a highly unlikely scenario for any real application. Even 
considering an extreme scenario in which a bus full of employees, each one with an 
active MN, is moving inside the oil refinery of GINSENG, we will not have more than 
50 MNs, which is the maximum of persons allowed to travel on a bus.  
 
 
Figure 66: Handoff success ratio per scenario and per number of MNs.  
 
The high handoff success ratio can be better observed in Figure 66, where it is apparent 
that until the 70 MNs point there is a success rate higher than 90% in the first handoff 
try, with the exception of the 30 MNs case in scenario 3.  


 137 
It is important to mention that, in the case of handoff failure, the NoP middleware 
module will repeat the handoff attempt after 3 seconds. 
 
In order to have a complementary perspective of the results, the following figures 
present the relation between the average time to handoff and the handoff success rate, 
per scenario. These are the two metrics that better depict the overall performance of the 
NoP proposal, as they convey latency and reliability information.  
 
 
 
Figure 67: Relationship between average time to  handoff and handoff success rate. Average of 
the five scenarios.  
Looking at Figure 67, it is clear that although there is no direct relation between the 
average time to handoff and the handoff success rate, increasing the number of MNs 
that simultaneously require handoff, affects both metrics as expected. 
 
55.3.3. Analysis of Variance 
 
In order to have a more accurate perspective of the obtained results, we used the one-
way analysis of variance (one-way ANOVA) technique, applying it to all scenarios, to 
check whether or not the impact of the varying number of MNs on the success rate and 
on the average handoff time was significant.  Figure 68 presents the results. 
 


 138 
 
 
Figure 68: One-way ANOVA for Average Time and Success Rate per number of MNs.  
Considering a significance level of 0.05, we can see in Figure 68 that both average time 
and success rate are affected by the number of MNs, their means being significantly 
different. The difference between the average time means is indeed very high, with a p-
value of 3.255x10-13, while for the success rate that difference is not so visible, with a p-
value of 0.001107. In order to see where the difference lies, we ran the Tukey’s 
Honestly Significant Difference (Tukey HSD) test for both cases. 
Analyzing Figure 69 we can conclude that, in general, considering a significance level 
of 0.05, the average handoff time is significantly affected after the 50 MNs point. This 
means that increasing the number of MNs simultaneously performing handoff over 50 
will significantly affect the average time of each operation. 
 
 
Figure 69: Tukey HSD results for the average time. 


 139 
 
Figure 70: Tukey HSD for the success rate.  
Regarding the success rate, we can see in Figure 70 that only the case of 90 MNs 
significantly affects the average.  
Hence, to conclude this analysis about the significance of the impact of the number of 
MNs to handoff at the same time, we can affirm that using the proposed NoP 
architecture, we can perform up to 50 simultaneous handoffs without significantly 
affecting the average time per handoff, and up to 90 simultaneous handoffs without 
significantly affecting the success rate, considering a level of significance of 0.05.   
In order to also analyze the impact of the different scenarios on the obtained results, we 
also used the one-way ANOVA test. In the previous section we did a visual analysis of 
the results and we could observe some differences between the scenarios. However, we 
did not know yet whether such differences were significant or not. 
 
 
 
Figure 71: One-way ANOVA to analyze the scen arios’ impact on the average time and 
success rate.  


 140 
As we can observe in Figure 71, the obtained p-values of 0.6996 for the average handoff 
time and 0.733 for the handoff success rate demonstrate that the different scenarios did 
not create a significant difference in the final results. Although we could visually 
observe some differences, as mentioned before, they do not significantly affect the 
overall performance. Hence, we can conclude that the performance of the proposed NoP 
solution is quite stable, independently of the used scenario. Nevertheless, we aim to 
extend this study in future work in order to increase the proxies’ density and study its 
impact. 
 
55.3.4. NoP versus Node-based handoff 
 
It is important to analyze how good the proposed NoP architecture is when compared 
with the conventional solution. In conventional WSN solutions there are no proxies and 
the MNs must execute the handoff process by themselves. To do so, we compared the 
results presented above with the results obtained in the previous chapter, in which we 
analyzed the impact of using standard MIPv6 over WSNs.  
To evaluate the NoP complete solution we must consider not only the time that the 
proxies need to handle the protocol between them, but also the time the MN needs to 
receive, process and load the new configuration. 
 
Considering the same setup used to evaluate MIPv6 in  chapter 4, we started by 
evaluating the NoP weight in the MN side. Although the previous experiments have 
shown that the use of a lightweight MIPv6 version in sensor nodes is feasible, if we 
extend it to include the return routeability procedure or any other MIPv6 
extension/improvement, the required time and energy will increase proportionally to the 
protocol complexity. Thus, in cases of frequent mobility, the use of MIPv6 in sensor 
nodes may lead to prohibitive energy expenditure, negatively impacting the nodes’ and 
network lifetime. 
In order to evaluate the performance of the proposed Network of Proxies approach and, 
consequently, confirm its expected benefits in terms of handoff time and energy, a third 
round of tests was made, over the setup presented in chapter 4. 

 141 
The obtained time and energy results are graphically presented in Figure 72 and Figure 
75, respectively. The corresponding numerical results are presented in Table 17 and 
Table 18. 
 
 
Figure 72: NoP-assisted hard handoff time. 
 
Table 17: Summary of NoP-assisted hard handoff time values. 
 
 
As we can observe in Figure 72 and in Table 17, the average time to perform a hard 
handoff was only 117ms. Once in possession of the new configuration, the mote readily 
and simply loads the new radio configuration, switching to the new channel and self-
configuring the new address. Naturally, this is much faster than in the traditional 
handoff case, presented in the previous chapter, in which the mobile node spent an 
average of ~961ms to handoff.  
 
However, we also need to consider the time required by the NoP to handle the protocol. 
Therefore, if we sum the mean time obtained above to the average handoff processing 
time by the NoP infrastructure, we achieve the total time to handoff (i.e., the time taken 
by the NoP since it detects the need to handoff until it sends the RA message to the 
mobile node, plus the time spent by the mobile node to receive this message and load
