 142 
the newly assigned configuration). Figure 73 presents these values, for a varying 
number of MNs simultaneously performing handoff. 
 
 
 
Figure 73: Average time needed to comple te the handoff until the MN loads the new 
configuration.  
In Chapter 4  we also determined the average time taken by a single node running 
MIPv6 soft handoff to perform one handoff: 777.937 ms. Figure 74 depicts this result 
along with the best and worst NoP cases. We consider the best NoP case when only one 
MN is in handoff while the worst NoP case occurs when the MN is in handoff 
simultaneously with other 89 MNs.  
 
 
Figure 74: NoP versus the conventional solution MIPv6.  


 143 
As we can observe, even considering the worst NoP case (i.e., 90 MNs performing 
handoff at the same time), we can achieve better results than MIPv6 doing just one 
handoff. 
 
This dramatic difference can be easily understood when we realize that in the case of 
NoP-assisted mobility the mobile node only changes network when all mobility 
management tasks have been performed on its behalf by the NoP, while in the 
traditional mode it is the mobile node that has to deal with and complete all the 
procedure. As proxies use IEEE 802.11 to communicate between themselves (i.e., they 
benefit from much higher transmission rate than the one available to individual motes) 
and are not subject to energy and processing restrictions, they can perform the mobility 
procedures much faster than the mote-based mobility case. 
Figure 75, complemented by Table 18, present the energy expenditure results. 
 
 
Figure 75: Energy required by NoP-assisted hard handoff. 
 
Table 18: Summary of NoP-assisted hard handoff energy values. 
 
 


 144 
We conclude that in the case of NoP-assisted handoff the mobile node only spent an 
average of ~1.1mJ to handoff between networks operating in different channels. This 
value sharply contrasts with the ~11.75mJ spent in the previous test suite, in which the 
mobile node performed all the mobility tasks (Chapter 4).  
 
 
Figure 76: Overview of time and energy expenditure for the three approaches. 
Figure 76 depicts the difference between MIPv6 soft handoff and NoP-assisted handoff, 
regarding energy consumption in the MN. The obtained results clearly show the 
advantages of the NoP approach. 
Shorter handoff time decreases the disconnection period and reduces the loss 
probability. Concurrently, reduction in energy consumption reduces the number of dead 
nodes and, therefore, increases the network lifetime, reducing its maintenance and cost 
of ownership. 
 
55.3.5. Results of NoP Simulation 
 
To confirm the superiority of the NoP solution we extended the simulation performed in 
Chapter 4, to include the NoP solution. The objective was to study how the NoP 
solution behaves in front of different number of sink nodes, network size and mobility 
patterns. To do so, we only simulated the MN process participation, which includes the 
RA reception and processing, followed by the radio configuration. 
Figure 77 presents the results of integrating the NoP solution in the evaluation 
performed by simulation in Chapter 4.  
 


 145 
  
 
Figure 77: Mean handoff time for each simulated scenario. 
As expected, regardless the situation, we can conclude that the proposed NoP solution 
was not affected neither by the number of nodes and sinks or by the mobility strategy. 
In contrast, the MIPv6 traditional solutions were highly affected by the number of 
nodes, sinks and also by the mobility strategy, as we had previously concluded in 
chapter 4.   
The simulations also confirmed that the NoP-assisted handoff led to a dramatic 
reduction in the energy required for mobility support, in addition to showing that this 
was not affected by any other variable, such as the number of nodes, sinks or mobility 
strategy. Figure 78 depicts the obtained results.  
 
 
 
Figure 78: Simulation results for the ener gy required by the NoP-assisted handoff. 


 146 
As we can observe, the NoP solution required in the worst case an energy consumption 
approximately of 4mJ to complete the handoff. Although this result is considerable 
higher than the 1.1mJ obtained through the real platform evaluation, it stills much lower 
than the values obtained while using MIPv6 in chapter 4, depicted in Figure 30 and 
Figure 31. 
 
55.4. Application to GINSENG 
 
In the scope of the GINSENG project, one of the scenarios required in the Petrogal’s 
refinery, located in Sines, Portugal, was the personnel security scenario. Most of the 
times, when working in the factory side of the refinery, employees are exposed to a very 
hazardous environment. Although they are obligated to accomplish with the security 
rules, they are still in contact with dangerous conditions that can affect them seriously 
and in certain cases cause death.  
In front of such scenario, Petrogal was looking for a solution to monitor their 
employees’ health condition, notifying and triggering alarms when anomalies are 
detected. The objective is thus to use a mechanism able to monitor the basic vital 
signals, such as heart and breath rate and also to monitor the body position, to detect 
situations where employees are laying on the floor. Nevertheless, the refinery has 
specific rules and limitations regarding wireless communication and most of the 
available solutions cannot be used because they are based on GSM, a forbidden 
technology, or because they require the deployment of additional access points of 
different technologies that might interfere with the local equipment. 
 
The GINSENG Project has been introducing a novelty regarding monitoring and acting 
in critical scenarios, such as the Petrogal’s refinery. Using WSNs, the GINSENG 
project can guarantee the co-existence of reliable, easy to deploy wireless devices, 
having the total control over their performance without affecting the refinery systems. 
Currently, several WSNs are deployed throughout the refinery, monitoring pressure, 
levels, temperature and gases and actuating in real valves according to the values read, 
closing the loop. This infrastructure is prepared to support other scenarios, such as the 
personnel safety scenario. 
 

 147 
Hence, the personnel safety scenario aims to equip each employee working in hazardous 
areas with the VitalJacket smart shirt [BioDevices 2012] capable to read ECG and, once 
connected to a TelosB mote, able to send that information to a central control room, 
through the existent WSNs. This application should accomplish with the remaining 
application currently running in the refinery, which are sampling at each 3 seconds. 
Once in the control room, the middleware is responsible for data processing, triggering 
alarms when anomalies are detected. Figure 79 depicts an employee wearing the smart 
shirt under the special suit, required in the factory area. 
 
 
Figure 79: Employee wearing the smart shirt under the working suit. 
As we can observe, the VitalJacket is easy to wear and imperceptible under the suit. The 
TelosB mote is in a specific pocket located in the back, which was originally designed 
only to support the original VitalJacket gateway. This gateway was originally designed 
to send data via Bluetooth or to save it in a local SD Card. Therefore some adaptations 
were necessary to guarantee the interoperability between this gateway and the TelosB 
mote, in order to inject the signal from the gateway in the TelosB. 
 


 148
Employees wearing the VitalJacket can move freely throughout the factory area during 
their daily work. Some tasks are more dangerous than others, but in general, the risk is 
always present and the employees must be continuously under monitoring. To 
exemplify the scenario we are going to take into account two existent WSN deployed to 
monitor the water treatment zone of the refinery, monitoring an area of about 3.1 square 
kilometers. Figure 80 depicts the layout of those two networks. 
 
 
Figure 80: WSNs deployed in refinery. 
Both networks are located in critical areas, monitoring pressures, flows and gases, in 
addition to controlling various valves. An industrial control room is located in the 
overlap area, so that sensor nodes from any of the two networks can communicate with 
it.  
As real-time operation is a must, these networks use a TDMA MAC protocol, running 
on a tree topology [Suriyachai et al. 2010] specifically designed for this purpose. To 
avoid long epochs, each GINSENG network is limited to 25 nodes and 3 tree levels. 
With this configuration, epochs have a duration of 1 second. The 25-node per network 


 149 
limitation led to the deployment of two separate networks instead of using only one 
bigger network. Therefore, these networks must operate in different channels, in order 
to assure that no interference occurs between them. Figure 81 details the GINSNEG tree 
organization of network 1, as an example. 
 
Figure 81: GINSENG – Scheme of the Netw ork 1 deployed in the Sines refinery. 
 
Workers can move freely in the whole area, thus generating intra- and inter-mobility 
events. The underlying mobility protocol must be capable of performing handoff 
between parents located in the same network and between the two networks, operating 
in different radio channels. Consequently, mobile nodes need to switch between several 
configurations, which may also include new radio channels, forcing to disconnection 
periods. The main requirement is to perform this process as fast as possible, minimizing 
the duration of the disconnected period, guaranteeing the same latencies and data 
delivery rate guaranteed by static nodes under the same environment, i. e., maintaining 
the same reliability level.  
With such a scenario to support the personnel safety scenario, in which employees can 
move freely through the different networks, some requirements must be supported in 
order to guarantee the connectivity and service availability, regardless of the employee 
situation. Thus, one of the most important requirements to assure the operability of this 
scenario is the mobility support throughout the different deployed WSNs.  
 


 150 
In the refinery employees traditionally move on foot or by bicycle. Hence, these 
scenarios pose some challenges to the mobility support regarding the handoff not only 
of a single mobile node (MN), but also of several MN moving randomly and at different 
speeds. Besides, the deployed GINSENG WSNs, are, as mentioned before, TDMA 
based, with an epoch size medium of one 1 second and a limited number of free 
available slots. From the network perspective, supporting mobility in this scenario poses 
several challenges not only to get efficient handoffs but also to assure that the addition 
of MNs in the existent networks will not affect the performance of the other scenarios. 
The deployed WSNs were targeted to efficiently support static scenarios and all motes 
are connected to sensors or actuators. However, to support remote programming 
powerful Linux Boards were also deployed in the field and we can use them to help the 
mobility support. Next section describes the method that we designed and implemented 
to support mobility of motes in the refinery scenario. 
 
While in the NoP evaluation presented in the previous sections, our objective was first 
to demonstrate its feasibility and second to measure the handoff time and energy 
requirements, in this case study our objective is to evaluate the impact of the assisted 
handoffs in the application, through the impact in the packet received rate (PRR). 
Latency is also an interesting metric we should consider. However, since the network is 
a TDMA with a fixed epoch size, latency will basically depend on each slot. 
Nevertheless, both metrics are the mirror of the overall scenario and can demonstrate 
the reliability of our mobility support mechanism when applied to this specific case. 
55.4.1. Scenario description 
 
The scenario we are going to use will be based on one of the networks deployed in the 
water treatment zone of the Petrogal’s refinery. Using the GINSENG setup, this 
network is organized in a tree topology fashion with a structure of 3-3, i.e., the sink as 3 
children that are in turn parent of 3 children two, as detailed in Figure 81. As mentioned 
before, to achieve maximum performance the GINSENG setup is based on a TDMA 
protocol [Suriyachai et al. 2010], which for mobile scenarios are not the most adequate 
because of its lack of flexibility. Therefore, we consider this scenario one of the most 
particular and demanded cases to apply and evaluate the feasibility of the NoP solution.  
 

 151 
Considering the standard application of sending data from mote to sink at each 3 
seconds, we will measure the impact of the employees moving across the network in the 
application. Figure 82 depicts the scenario, exemplifying how one MN moves across the 
network. As we can observe, the MN is programmed to move along a pre-defined path 
in a loop mode. During that path, the MN will need to handoff between parents, 
connecting to a free TDMA slot in each one, performing handoff. In our evaluation we 
performed from one to three MNs, in order to study the impact of several MNs moving 
at the same time. 
 
Figure 82: Network scenario with MN 112 m oving in a ping-pong fashion along the red line.  
In GINSENG, parents are programmed to periodically announce their free slots. Once 
in scan mode (the initial state of every mote), the MN listens those announcements, 
choosing one slot to connect. This task takes time and depends on the announcements 
frequency.  
Currently, GINSENG allows two distinct configurations, static and dynamic topology 
control (DTC). In static topology motes have fixed positions and the network topology 
is previously configured without runtime changes support. In DTC the network tree is 
dynamically built and supports runtime changes [Zinonos et al. 2011]. In previous work 
DTC was also targeted to support mobility [Silva et al. 2011], but it presented a high 
handoff time, which would seriously affect the application reliability. In DTC there are 
not mechanisms to detect the need to handoff neither to process the same. In DTC, 
when an MN loses connection it enters in scan mode and waits for the parents’ 
announcements to obtain another slot.  Therefore, with our NoP proposal, initially 


 152 
designed to support IP Mobility, we can previously obtain the next free slot 
information, providing it to the MN before the movement. 
 
Due to the restrictions existent in the refinery we could not currently perform this 
evaluation in the real scenario. Therefore, in our evaluation we ran the GINSENG setup 
in the Cooja simulator [Osterlind et al. 2006], measuring the PRR and also latencies for 
the two most common cases existent in the refinery in regard to the way that the 
employees move, namely: walking (5Km/h) and riding a bike (15Km/h).   
We also considered three distinct configurations, regarding the topology construction 
and NoP support, namely: static topology, dynamic topology (DTC) and NoP. Finally, 
with NoP the tree construction and maintenance is based on the static topology but with 
the support of the extra proxies, capable of assisting runtime changes.  
 
To simulate the NoP we used telosB motes in Cooja programmed to sniff the 
GINSENG network and decide when a specific node must handoff. Those proxy-motes 
were then able to order the handoff through the Sink-Node using the existent 
downstream slots to reach the mobile node. In this evaluation we previously assigned 
free slots in each branch to assure that the MNs always have available slots wherever 
they move to. The same must occur in the real deployment, because otherwise the 
network will never support mobile nodes. To implement mobility in Cooja we used the 
mobility plugin for this simulator and to design the trace files we used bonnmotion 
[Bonn 2011]. As mentioned throughout this thesis, Cooja was chosen because it is the 
only simulator capable of running ContikiOS, which makes it possible to run directly 
the GINSENG code and setup. Besides, it was also considered one of the best WSNs 
simulators [Abdolrazaghi 2009].  
 
All evaluation had the duration of one hour. Latencies were measured in milliseconds 
(ms) and are presented as average, minimum and maximum latency, while PRR were 
calculated by the ratio between the total packets sent and received. From the PRR we 
can not only observe directly the packet received, but also look directly to the packet 
losses. As mentioned before, in the network constituted by twelve motes, we vary 
between one and three MNs asking for handoff at the same time. To support more MNs 
performing handoff at the same time in one network we would have to change the 3-3 

 153 
topology and increase the epoch size, which would significantly increase the latencies 
and affect the reliability of the application. Since these networks are also used to act we 
must guarantee the minimum latency also in downstream and therefore epochs’ size 
must be controlled. 
55.4.2. Walking scenario 
 
In our first scenario we considered the basic case where the employees are walking 
throughout the refinery. For our evaluation we considered in this case a constant 
velocity of 5 Km/h. Figure 83 presents the obtained PRR using the different topologies 
and NoP with different number of MNs requiring handoff at the same time. 
 
 
 


 154
 
Figure 83: Packet Received Rate using diffe rent topology control methods and NoP support 
while varying the number of MNs walking. 
Considering the results presented above we can clearly conclude that the NoP solution 
outperforms static and DTC solutions by increasing the PRR almost to the 100%. 
GINSENG was designed to reach at least 99.9% of PRR, which justifies why it is so 
restrict and TDMA based. Nevertheless, as we can observe, when applying mobility 
using the traditional topology modules, neither static or DTC are able to guarantee such 
reliability levels. In DTC we can in general obtain a significant improvement, however, 
it is not always true and depends on the dynamic slots assignment. For instance, looking 
at the 2 MNs scenario, we can see that the improvement over static topology is too 
short, while in the remaining cases it is a bit higher. Looking again at the NoP solution 
we can observe that in the 3MNs scenario, MNs 110 and 106 presents a PRR of 96.52% 
and 97.77%, respectively, which are values slightly under the 99% observed in the other 
cases. The own scenario and the mobility pattern, following the same trend of the static 
and DTC cases, in which the losses were also superior in these two cases then in the 
MN 112, cause these 3.48% and 2.23% of packet losses. Thus we can conclude that 
although NoP clearly improves the scenario to a very good result, due to its 
particularity, depending on the employees’ mobility pattern and slots assignment we 
cannot guarantee 99% in all situations. Nevertheless, we can assure >95% of PRR 
which is also a completely acceptable value in this restricted scenario.  
 


 155 
 
 
 
 
Figure 84: Latencies using different topology c ontrol methods and NoP support while varying 
the number of MNs walking. 


 156
Looking at Figure 84 we can observe the latencies (average, minimum and maximum) 
for all MNs in the different scenarios. As we mentioned before, since we are dealing 
with a TDMA and pre-configured network, latencies are directly dependent on the 
respective slot. Nevertheless, when we look at the figures above, we can see that there 
are some cases in which the maximum latency is much higher than the average, which 
means that the handoff process may also affect it. We can also observe that it only 
occurs with static topology and DTC and never with NoP. Although it does not affect 
the average latency it may affect a punctual situation and put the reliability of the 
application in cause.  
Despite that situation we can observe that in general DTC shows a better average 
latency than the remaining cases, which is justified by its algorithm of assign motes to 
different slots, while static and NoP experiments were run always using the same 
TDMA configuration.  
55.4.3.  Bike scenario 
 
An atypical scenario is when employees are riding a bicycle in their jobs. However, at 
the Petrogal’s refinery it is the most common method of mobility. To evaluate this 
special case we considered a constant velocity of 15Km/h, considering it the average 
speed that the employees move when riding the bicycle in the refinery.  
 
 
 


 157 
 
 
 
Figure 85: Packet Received Rate using diffe rent topology control methods and NoP support 
while varying the number of MNs riding a bicycle.  
Looking at Figure 85 we observe the same trend of the previous scenario in which 
employees move by walking. Nevertheless, considering the NoP case, we observe a 
slightly decrease in the PRR of all MNs in the case of 3 MNs requesting handoff at the 
same time. Like in the walking scenario, we have the MN 110 with higher losses. 
However, in this situation both 106 and 112 presented some losses too, between 2 and 
3%. Despites this difference we still demonstrating that even increasing the speed, 
applying NoP to a distinct scenario, we can guarantee an efficient mobility support 
outperforming the existent solutions, maintaining a very acceptable reliability level. 


 158
 
 
 
Figure 86: Latencies using different topology c ontrol methods and NoP support while varying 
the number of MNs riding a bicycle.  


 159 
Looking at Figure 86 we observe the same trend in latencies that we observed in the 
previous case. In summary, there are some cases in static and dynamic topology in 
which maximum latencies are considerably high. These cases are not meaningful in the 
average latency and are not controlled. Nevertheless, they may be harmful in the 
application performance and therefore not allowed in such scenarios, which main 
objective is the total control of the system performance. 
 
In conclusion, to evaluate the impact of mobility in the application and the performance 
of NoP, we ran several evaluations considering all the variables existent in the scenario. 
We simulated the movement of employees walking at 5km/h and riding a bicycle at 
15km/h. We also varied the number of MNs requiring handoff at the same time, from 
one to three. From those experiments we concluded that in spite of all the scenario 
restrictions, our solution is able to guarantee the same reliability levels for mobile nodes 
achieved by static nodes. We not only drastically reduced the packet losses but also 
eliminated the probability of spontaneous peaks in latencies, which occurred with the 
conventional solutions.  
If in previous work we proved that our NoP solution is capable of efficiently handling 
IP level mobility of several MNs at the same time and using different scenarios, in this 
section we demonstrated that the same solution can also be applied to one of the most 
restricted scenarios and efficiently handled mobility at the MAC level too. Using this 
solution we can justify the investment in the extra equipment by the clear improvement 
in the global performance, considering mobility support. However, based on our 
experience in real deployments we aim to extend this solution to more than mobility 
support and assist additional features that otherwise would be complicated to integrate 
in such small devices. Next section details the new concept of Assisted Wireless Sensor 
Networks (AWSN), based on NoP. 
  

 160 
 
55.5. Assisted WSNs 
 
 
Based on the concept presented in the previous sections, the objective is now to extend 
the paradigm and use the same infrastructure to support other demanding operations, 
such as routing, security and quality of service, among others. 
The Assisted Wireless Sensor Network (AWSN) is a WSN assisted by a Network of 
Proxies (NoP). As introduced in this thesis, a NoP is an IEEE802.11 ad-hoc mesh 
network, which provides higher bandwidth when compared to the traditional 
IEEE802.15.4 used in the WSN, constituted by more powerful devices without energy 
restrictions. Those devices can be of any type, the unique requirement being the 
presence of two network interfaces, one for each used technology. From our experience 
in real deployments we have been working with embedded solutions known as Linux 
Boards. These Linux Boards can be energetically fed through different solutions, 
providing all USB and network interfaces necessary. The price of each device is around 
the same price of one of the most used Motes, such as the Sky/TelosB mote. Our 
motivation to adopt this solution was based on our field experience on trying to assure a 
performance controlled WSN, during the GINSENG Project, in which several complex 
modules must be integrated in TelosB motes, but whose characteristics are not sufficient 
to hold all of them. In [Bouckaert et al. 2010] was presented a theoretical analysis of the 
integration between Wireless Mesh and Wireless Sensor Networks, in which the authors 
present a set of possible integration modes between both solutions. In all those 
described integration solutions, the Mesh Network is mandatory and makes part of the 
whole network architecture. In contrast, the Assisted WSNs is seamless and the WSN 
can operate with or without the support of the Wireless Mesh Network.  
 
Once deployed, the proxy devices automatically start to establish the NoP, using the 
IEEE802.11 interface. They maintain an updated list of all neighbors, in which the 
number of hops between each one is included. Then, through the IEEE802.15.4 
interface they can sniff and control the WSN in their range. Figure 87 shows an AWSN 
scenario. 

 161 
 
Figure 87: Assisted Wireless Sensor Networ k (AWSN) supported by a Network of Proxies 
(NoP).  
  As we can observe the assistance capacity depends on the deployment. In Figure 87, as 
an example, we deployed four new Proxies in addition to the Sink-Node, which can be 
considered as on Proxy as well, extending the network support for almost all WSN. 
Depending on the objective and also on the cost-benefit, the deployment can be done to 
cover all the WSN or just to cover the main area, where the density is higher. Hence, 
using this concept we propose now to extend the capabilities of this solution, assisting 
different areas. Next section explains how to assist the routing process while section 
5.5.2 explains the QoS support with using NoP. Section 5.5.3 exemplifies other 
processes capable to be assisted. 
55.5.1. Assisted Routing 
 
Once extra devices are deployed, we should study how to maximize their usage in order 
to justify the investment on reducing the cost on the deployment and maintenance of the 
WSN. Figure 88 depicts a common unassisted WSN, in which the paths from motes to 
sink are, as an example, already defined by an unspecified routing protocol. 
 


 162
 
Figure 88: Unassisted Wireless Sensor Network. 
As we can observe in Figure 88, in a common unassisted scenario, we can have leaf 
nodes at several hops distant from the sink, which will not only increase the energy 
spent by the other nodes to forward their packet along the path but also will increase the 
latency and the probability of packet losses. In Figure 88 we can see, as an example, 
motes at more than 4 hops. Nevertheless, using a NoP to assist this same WSN, we can 
drastically reduce the distance and therefore reduce the complexity of the routing 
protocol, which in turn will reduce the probability for packet losses and high latencies. 
Figure 89 depicts this scenario. 
 
Figure 89: Assisted Routing in  Wireless Sensor Networks.  
As we can observe in Figure 89, assisting the WSN will, in this case, remove almost all 
multi-hop cases and simplify the network. If on one hand we are increasing the 


 163 
deployment cost by including extra hardware, on the other hand we are saving money 
by reducing the energy consumption of the network and at the same time increasing the 
reliability and controlling its performance. 
 
To do such, our middleware intercepts the packets from the surrounding nodes, 
forwarding them through the NoP, instead of leaving them going through the WSN. On 
one hand we reduce the number of hops and on the other hand we increase the network 
capacity by using the NoP bandwidth. To support this statement we ran an experiment 
exploring the opposite situation, in which the sensor node is forwarding packets from 
the entire neighborhood. We considered for this evaluation one of the latest solutions, 
designed specifically for Low Power and Lossy Networks (LLN), such as WSNs. This 
solution was RPL, whose implementation is natively included in the latest version of the 
Contiki Operating System, 2.5. The experiment was carried by simulation in Cooja. 
Figure 90 depicts the scenario, while Figure 91 shows the connections. 
 
 
Figure 90: RPL running on a WSN, with mo te 2 doing the connection to the Sink.  
 As we can observe in Figure 90 we have a small network with 10 motes and 1 sink. We 
deployed this network to force the remaining 9 motes to send their data through mote 2, 
as we can prove in Figure 91, in order to load it and analyze the impact of such act.  


 164 
 
Figure 91: Connections created by RPL to forward packet to the sink.  
Using the collect view tool, provide by Cooja we analyzed the power consumption of 
the motes. Figure 92 presents the results. 
 
Figure 92: Average Power Consumption when running RPL on ContikiOS 2.5. 
As we can observe, by comparing the values from mote 2 with the remaining cases, the 
power consumption increases considerably. Taking into consideration the average of all 
the other motes, we conclude that by forwarding data from the 9 neighbors, mote 2 
consumes more ~30%, which will affect its lifetime. 
By placing a NoP in this scenario we remove this additional load from mote 2, making 
the power consumption more homogeneous through all motes, which turns it into a 
more controlled and predictable network. 
 
55.5.2. Assisted QoS 
 
By assisting the WSN we can also support QoS mechanisms using the higher bandwidth 
provided by the NoP to serve the WSN. For instance, in the real deployment we have 


 165 
been working on, it is mandatory to have a real time service, in order to detect 
anomalies on time and act on specific valves according to the values obtained. 
Therefore, in this critical system, failures or delays are not allowed and the QoS must be 
assured. By reducing the number of hops, which reduces the latency and increases the 
network lifetime, and by using the higher bandwidth of the NoP we can use traditional 
QoS mechanisms assure real-time activity. Besides, we can explore multiple connection 
points to extend the sensor node availability and to increase the bandwidth of by 
aggregating several IEEE802.15.4 connections. 
Although in the original version of NoP one specific Proxy exclusively assists each 
mote, to support Quality of Service we extend this concept, allowing packet forwarding 
from different paths, through different Proxies. Maintaining one proxy acting as parent 
(master), it is possible to use other reachable proxies to forward or receive data. Figure 
93 depicts this scenario. 
 
 
Figure 93: Sensor Node using more than one Proxy to increase the throughput assuring 
determined QoS level. 
Based on the scheme of Figure 93, the sensor node can send and receive that to/from 
several proxies simultaneously. This feature multiplies the available bandwidth in the 
WSN side, considering all reachable proxies, and will decrease the bottleneck existent 
in the original NoP solution. Thus, even though the throughput capacity remains 
considerably different when comparing one IEEE802.11 link with two or three 
IEEE802.15.4 links, the use of different paths will allow the establishment of different 
QoS levels. Otherwise, the support of packet differentiation would be only efficiently 


 166 
supported at the NoP side, where the capacity is enough to support such. At the WSN 
side, supporting QoS in just one link would seriously limit the overall performance. 
Nevertheless, applying this multiple connectivity in WSNs must be carefully 
implemented to avoid high-energy consumption.  
To evaluate this proposal, we ran an NS2 simulation, containing the scenario presented 
in Figure 93. In this simulation the Sink Node was configured to send UDP packet with 
1000 bytes at 1000 000 bps, using Constant Bit Rate (CBR) traffic. All links were 
configured with a queue size of 10 packets and a bandwidth capacity of 11Mbps for the 
IEEE802.11 networks and 250Kbps for the IEEE802.15.4 network. To analyze the 
impact of using multiple paths we measured the packet losses when using one, two and 
three different paths. We consider that having more than three proxies in the range is 
not a usual situation and therefore not considered in this evaluation. The following 
figures summarize the achieved results. 
 
 
Figure 94:Packet losses measured when us ing one single path to reach the MN. 
 
 
Figure 95: Packet losses measured when us ing two multiple paths to reach the MN. 


 167 
 
Figure 96: Packet losses measured when us ing three multiple paths to reach the MN. 
As we can observe, when using one single link to deliver data, the packet losses are in 
average over the 45 packets per second. However, when using the multiple path 
scenarios we see that this value is significantly reduced. Next figure compares the three 
averages. 
 
 
Figure 97: Average comparison of packet losses per second. 
Despite some picks, we can conclude that adding a new path avoided the loss of 10 
packets per second, in average. These results clearly demonstrate the benefits of using 
several paths to overcome the hardware constraints and assist the assurance of a 
determined QoS level. In this evaluation we also measured the bandwidth. Figure 98 
summarizes the results. 
  


 168 
 
 
Figure 98: Bandwidth evaluation for the three cases. 
As we can observe, the number of additional paths, provided by additional proxies, as 
expected, multiplies the bandwidth.  
To conclude, these results demonstrate how well we can support QoS in WSNs by 
assisting the network with a Network of Proxies, when using all the available resources. 
More than increasing the bandwidth between the mote and the NoP, by establishing 
multiple connection points we also increase the mote’s availability. 
 
 


 169 
55.5.3. Assisting other procedures 
 
In the previous section we could understand how NoP can be used to assist common 
WSNs in particular cases besides mobility. As example, we analyzed how to improve 
routing and how to add QoS by improving motes availability and throughput. 
Nevertheless, much more functionalities and features can be explored using the Assisted 
WSN concept.  
For instance, regarding security we can use the NoP to do the key management, for 
instance using an Internet level PKC, like trustedFleck does [Hu et al. 2010], and solve 
one of the most critical points when approaching security in WSNs. Besides, we can use 
IPSec mechanisms to guarantee security within the NoP and between proxies and 
motes, considering that motes are running 6lowPAN. 
Node Discovery can also be done and controlled by proxies. Instead of having advanced 
node discovery mechanisms, we can maintain a simple duty cycle solution only to 
assure that motes outside the NoP range can connect via other motes. Assisted motes, 
i.e. motes within the NoP range, do not need to establish any type of Node Discovery 
mechanism because Proxies will be responsible for detecting them in an ad-hoc fashion 
solution. Assuming that all motes are running a specific application, proxies can - by 
sniffing their communication or simply through the beacons used by the MAC Layer- 
detect the presence of them and cross that information among other proxies, not only to 
record the presence of each mote but also to estimate their location.  Once new motes 
are detected, the proxy asks its closer neighbors if they knew about the existence of that 
specific mote. If not, the proxy registers the new mote as its master. Otherwise, it just 
notifies its proxy master about the detection, which can mean a mobility situation. 
When registering new motes as master, the Proxy must contact a Public Key 
Infrastructure (PKI) in order to obtain a new certificate for the new mote.  
 
The biggest advantages of our proposal are not only the features we can provide, but 
also the flexibility of creating the AWSN. AWSNs can be applied to new WSNs or to 
existent WSNs, independently of their characteristics. With our proposal, WSN can run 
assisted or not. When running assisted we can assure certain levels of security, 
efficiency and QoS. Otherwise, those requirements cannot be globally assured without 
tuning the own WSN.  

 170 
 
 
Critical or not, many of the real applications require reliable systems, and to get them, 
researchers and engineers have overcome the problems by using specific or alternative 
solutions, such as adding extra hardware to enhance the network performance. 
Following this trend, which has also been supported in literature with the argument that 
we should keep motes doing just what they were designed to, and based on our 
experience in real deployments, we came up with a global solution of creating an 
Assisted Wireless Sensor Network (AWSN) based on a Network of Proxies (NoP). Our 
solution, initially designed only to support mobility of motes, is now intended to be 
extended to any other requirements such as routing, security, QoS and Node Discovery.  

 171 
 
55.6. Considerations 
 
Despite all the technological development witnessed in the last few years, hardware 
restrictions of wireless sensor nodes keep being largely incompatible with the 
paraphernalia that many researchers insist on including in WSNs, such as advanced 
routing algorithms, security mechanisms, data fusion methods, among many other 
features. The research community is starting to recognize this and is looking at 
complementary infrastructures that can relieve sensor nodes from tasks they were not 
designed for. 
In this chapter we proposed, presented and evaluated such an infrastructure, which 
relies on a self-configured wireless mesh network of proxies (NoP) capable of handling 
processor/memory/energy-intense operations on behalf of the sensor nodes. Using 
mobility as case study, and through implementation, we demonstrated that NoP is 
capable not only of triggering and performing the handoff of sensor nodes faster than 
conventional solutions (i.e., solutions in which the same operations are the sole 
responsibility of sensor nodes), but also that it is capable of handling several mobile 
sensor node handoffs at the same time, under different scenarios. Specifically, the 
experiments led to the conclusion that the implemented NoP, based on standard, off-the-
shelf hardware, allowed the handling of up to 50 simultaneous handoffs without 
significantly affecting the average per mote handoff time, and up to 90 simultaneous 
handoffs without significantly increasing the probability of failing the handoff. 
Moreover, when compared to node-based MIPv6 conventional handoff, the NoP 
solution is greatly beneficial in terms of handoff time and node energy consumption, 
which demonstrates that the use of an additional infrastructure is largely compensated 
by the savings in the overall WSN performance. 
  

 172 
  

 173 
Chapter 6  
 
Conclusions 
 
Mobility support in Wireless Sensor Networks is considered one of the most emergent 
and demanding mechanisms. Although recognized and used in several scientific 
contributions, the support of mobility in WSNs is still an unresolved issue. On the one 
hand mobility has been targeted to improve the network performance, while on the 
other hand it has been directly linked to the own application nature. While in the first 
situation mobility is usually considered an achieved and supported mechanism, in the 
second situation several and distinct solutions have appeared to solve particular 
problems. Analyzing those solutions we could conclude that part of them are too 
complex and were never implemented and used in real scenarios. In contrast, there were 
other solutions that were once implemented and used in real networks which were 
tremendously specific and not adaptable to other scenarios. 
We found the need to obtain a global solution able to be widely applied, regardless of 
the scenario and type of sensor network. After surveying the existent MAC Layer 
approaches, including the most relevant attempts to support mobility at this level, we 
concluded that the complexity of the duty cycle mechanisms used to rationalize energy, 
highly limits mobility support leading to specific and not global solutions. Therefore, 
we raised the level and started to analyze solution at the Network Layer. At this level 
well-known solution such as Zigbee and the most recent WirelessHart do not support 
mobility, but the idea of importing IPv6 to WSNs and all its features become a reality. 
Hence, following the Internet of Things concept and clearly believing in the advantages 

 174 
of directly reaching each tiny device, we aimed to bring MIPv6 to WSNs. However, this 
was something totally new and no one could know whether it was really supported or 
feasible in theses constrained networks. Hence, we definitely needed to demystify this. 
Once we could use MIPv6 in WSNs it would be possible to use it widely independently 
on the MAC protocol and network structure, becoming totally transparent to the 
application. Thus, we implemented MIPv6 in a specific operating system for embedded 
devices, ContikiOS, and evaluated its performance considering the time needed to 
handoff and the energy required to the Mobile Node to perform the operation. This 
evaluation was performed in different scenarios, through simulation and using real 
hardware, and considering both intra and inters mobility. The achieved results 
demonstrated first that MIPv6 could evidently run in WSNs, considering the most 
constrained hardware. However, the results also demonstrated that even the time to 
handoff was not considerably high, the energy required could be a problem.  
With MIPv6 running in real WSNs we aimed to improve its performance in order to 
make it really feasible for this type of networks. The possibility of importing IPv6 
technology to things brings a clear advantage, but we must have in mind that most of 
the IPv6 based mechanisms were not designed or developed taking such restrictions and 
constrains in consideration. Hence, we proposed a   μMIPv6 solution in which we 
applied the same concept of 6lowPAN in the original MIPv6 protocol. By coding and 
compressing we proposed a shorter alternative for all the MIPv6 headers, messages and 
options, drastically decreasing their size, but keeping the protocol structure entire. This 
solution was proposed as an Internet draft to the 6lowPAN IETF WG and implemented 
in ContikiOS. When evaluating μMIPv6 we could observe a reduction mainly in the 
energy consumption when compared with the original form. However, that reduction 
was not as significant as we were expecting and therefore another solution should be 
considered if we really wanted to achieve an efficient and global mobility solution for 
WSNs. 
At the same time we were doing these studies we were also working in the case study – 
GINSENG. Designed for guaranteeing the performance of the network in critical 
scenarios, several software modules were developed. A topology control module, a 
performance debugging module, an advanced TDMA MAC protocol, 6lowPAN, a 
power control module, a mobility module, a security module, the application or the 
control loop module amongst others were all developed to run in the tiny resources-

 175 
constrained nodes. However, in the early days of the project we realized that fitting all 
these features in motes was impossible. ROM and RAM restrictions seriously limit the 
use of all these modules.  
This important lesson that we learnt changed our perspective regarding the way we 
should guarantee the support of mobility in WSNs. Loading the MN with an advanced 
and complex module to support mobility would not be the desired solution. Although 
possible, as we had previously demonstrated using MIPv6 or MIPv6, based solutions 
were not feasible because of its weight, and using MAC Layer approaches would make 
it highly complex and case-specific. Hence, we concluded that our solution should 
move from the mote to the network side and thus we started to study the possibility of 
designing a network-based solution specifically for WSNs. 
In this way the Network of Proxies (NoP) solution was born. Deploying extra nodes in 
the network could be beneficial not only to support mobility but also to assist other 
functionalities that do not fit into the constrained motes, but that are highly required to 
achieve the network control of performance, as in GINSENG.  Our NoP idea goes 
further aiming to establish a point of contact between the extra nodes, constituting a 
shared network in which all kind of information can be exchanged. Regarding mobility 
support NoP was thus designed to monitor the link quality of MNs and based on our 
previous work, detect when handoffs must occur, triggering the process. Using the NoP, 
the mobility protocol will exclusively run between the extra nodes, which we called 
proxies. These proxies will first agree who is in the best position to take care of the MN, 
considering its movement, and second will obtain near the best proxy the next 
configuration to the MN. Only after dealing with all the procedure and updating all the 
binding tables, the proxy enters in contact with the MN, ordering this to load the new 
configuration. Once this procedure is all done in the network side, the time it takes to 
affect the MN application and the energy it requires to the MN is drastically reduced 
when compared with the traditional solution, previously evaluated.  
Implemented and evaluated in real hardware the NoP solution is thus the solution we 
were looking for, guaranteeing a plug and play deployment and a configurable 
interoperability with different systems. Applied to GINSENG we demonstrated that this 
solution could guarantee the same level of performance in MNs existent in static nodes, 
which is an extremely good achievement, justifying the extra cost of deploying extra 
nodes in the network. 

 176 
In addition to mobility support we also demonstrated how the NoP solution can assist 
other requirements such as advanced routing, security or QoS, which - as we learnt in 
GINSENG- would be impossible to join in a single tiny and constrained node.  Hence, 
more than mobility support with NoP, we developed a new concept named Assisted 
Wireless Sensor Network – AWSN.  
For future work we may consider the evolution of this concept to the market, 
constituting a plug-and-play box of the size of home router, capable of being easily 
deployed in the middle of an existent WSN, to assist the same in a standalone or 
extended mode.

ANNEX A  
Evaluation of 6lowPAN solutions 
 
In order to evaluate the performance and efficiency of the three presented 
implementations, 6lowpancli, b6lowpan and SICSlowPAN, practical tests were 
performed in our lab. Changing the data length of an UDP packet, from 0 to 1024 bytes, 
we a) measured the time required to send the message and b) calculated the energy 
spent based on the current and voltage required for the used hardware.  
The hardware used was the mote telosB from Crossbow, equipped with a MSP430 
microprocessor.  
To measure the time needed, we used native functions from the respective operating 
systems (TinyOS or Contiki), to compute the difference between the time when the 
UDP packet was created, until the time it was completely sent by the MAC Layer.   
Hence, considering the three implementations: 6lowpancli, b6lowpan and 
SICSlowPAN; and UDP packets with data length of 0, 16, 32, 64, 128, 256, 512, 768 
and 1024; the time and energy required is outlined by Figure 99 and Figure 100, 
respectively. The time values presented in Figure 99 are the average of thirty 
measurements, with a confident interval of 95%. 
 

 190
 
Figure 99: Time needed to send an UDP packet per amount of data. 
 
 
Figure 100: Energy needed to send an UDP packet per amount of data. 
 
It is possible to conclude that the energy required is proportional to the time spent. 
Considering that the telosB in our tests required 18.7mA (measured using a multimeter 
in series) to send with a voltage of 2.6Volts, the energy in joules is given by: 
 
E = t ×18.7 × 22.6         , where t is the time measured. 
 
According to the results SICSlowPAN is the implementation that requires less time and 
energy. Although the newest implementation for TinyOS-2.x, b6lowPAN is really close 
to the consumptions of SICSlowPAN, the 6lowpancli was somewhat below. In fact, 
considering the maximum MTU of 102bytes in IEEE802.15.4, we can observe that the 


 191 
performance of 6lowpancli started to decrease when fragmentation was required. It is 
possible to see this behavior at the 128bytes of data. From that value until the end, the 
time/energy increases significantly when compared with the other solutions. Under the 
same conditions, b6lowpan and SICSlowPAN also increased the required time, but in a 
constant and controlled mode.  
To better analyze the two best solutions, the next figure presents the correlation between 
them, based on the Pearson coefficient.  
 
Table 19: Pearson correlation between SICSlowPAN and b6lowPAN. 
 
 
As we can see in Table 19, the correlation between these two cases is very significant, 
with a value of 0.972, which means a positive linear association. Based on this value we 
prove that the performance of these two solutions is almost the same. Although 
SICSlowPAN requires less energy than b6lowPAN, it does not scale better. We can 
conclude that SICSlowPAN, in general, is more energetically efficient. However, 
considering packet size, b6lowPAN performs as well as SICSlowPAN.  
 
We also evaluated the required ROM and RAM for each case. Figure 101 and Figure 
102 outline the results. 
 


 192
 
Figure 101: Amount of ROM required per data length. 
 
 
Figure 102: Amount of RAM per data length. 
 
As we can observe in Figure 102, there is no difference between the amounts of ROM, 
when we change the packet size. However, the requirements of each implementation is 
significant, being SICSlowPAN the one that requires more ROM. Regarding RAM 
requirements (Figure 102), whereas SICSlowPAN and 6lowpancli required always the 
same amount independently of the packet size, b6lowPAN increased the RAM 
requirements each time the packet size increased.  
Hence, based on ROM and RAM, 6lowpancli is the most efficient implementation. 
B6lowPAN does not scale well considering RAM, but requires less ROM than 
SICSlowPAN.

Abstract 
 
Real deployments of wireless sensor networks (WSN) are rare, and 
virtually all have considerable limitations when node mobility is 
concerned.  On one hand, research in WSNs tends to favour complex 
multi-hop routing protocols and, on the other hand, IP and mobility 
are considered too demanding for these environments.  In this document 
we contradict this general belief by proposing an adaptation model for 
Mobile IPv6 in 6lowPANs.
